Weak recovery, hypothesis testing, and mutual information in stochastic block models and planted factor graphs
Elchanan Mossel, Allan Sly, Youngtak Sohn

Abstract
The stochastic block model is a canonical model of communities in random graphs. It was introduced in the social sciences and statistics as a model of communities, and in theoretical computer science as an average case model for graph partitioning problems under the name of the “planted partition model.” Given a sparse stochastic block model, the two standard inference tasks are: (i) Weak recovery: can we estimate the communities with non-trivial overlap with the true communities? (ii) Detection/Hypothesis testing: can we distinguish if the sample was drawn from the block model or from a random graph with no community structure with probability tending to 1 1 as the graph size tends to infinity? In this work, we show that for sparse stochastic block models, the two inference tasks are equivalent except at a critical point. That is, weak recovery is information theoretically possible if and only if detection is possible. We thus find a strong connection between these two notions of inference for the model. We further prove that when detection is impossible, an explicit hypothesis test based on low-degree polynomials in the adjacency matrix of the observed graph achieves the optimal statistical power. This low-degree test is efficient as opposed to the likelihood ratio test, which is not known to be efficient. Moreover, we prove that the asymptotic mutual information between the observed network and the community structure exhibits a phase transition at the weak recovery threshold. Our results are proven in much broader settings including the hypergraph stochastic block models and general planted factor graphs. In these settings, we prove that the impossibility of weak recovery implies contiguity and provide a condition that guarantees the equivalence of weak recovery and detection.



1
Introduction

The stochastic block model, or simply a block model, is a random graph model generalizing the famous Erdos-Renyi random graph
[
ER+60
]
and is a special case of
inhomegnuous random graphs
[
BJR07
]
.
It has been studied extensively in statistics and the social sciences as a model of communities

[
HLL83
,
SN97
,
BC09
,
RCY11
]

and in computer science as a model to study the average case behavior of clustering algorithms
[
DF89
,
JS98
,
CK01
,
McS01
,
CO10
]
.
In the last decade starting with the work of Decelle et al.
[
DKMZ11
]
, the sparse block model has been extensively studied with fascinating connections to belief propagation and non-backtracking random walks
[
KMM+13
,
BLM15
]
.
Furthermore, the block model is one of the canonical models in high-dimensional statistics which is believed to exhibit a statistical-computational gap, meaning a gap between what is achievable information theoretically and what is achievable with known computationally efficient algorithms.

The two central inference tasks related to the sparse block models are
weak recovery
and
detection
. Weak recovery means estimating communities with non trivial overlap with the true communities. Detection refers to designing a statistical test that, from a single sample, achieves vanishing Type I and Type II errors in determining whether the sample is drawn from the block model or from a
null model
without community structure – that is, a standard random graph model with the same average degree. Numerous papers including
[
MNS15
,
BMNN16
,
COKPZ18
,
COEJ+18
,
COKM20
]
have studied
the information theoretic thresholds for both weak recovery and detection. Notably, it has been shown that, for the two-community case
[
MNS15
]
and the disassortative case
[
COEJ+18
]
, the thresholds for weak recovery and detection coincide, albeit with completely different proofs in each case. This naturally leads to the question:

Is weak recovery information theoretically equivalent to detection in general?

The first objective of this paper is to answer this question for sparse block models, and in greater generality, for
planted factor models

[
COKPZ18
]
which encompass labeled/weighted stochastic block models
[
SH12
,
LMX15
,
XJL20
]
and hypergraph stochastic block models
[
ACKZ15
,
GD17a
,
GD17b
,
PZ21
,
SZ22
]
. We prove that the information theoretic possibility of detection implies the possibility of weak recovery, and find a condition under which the converse holds.

The second objective is to study
hypothesis testing
, a more classical task than detection, involving tests between two simple hypotheses. Notably, the impossibility of detection merely indicates that there is no sequence of statistical tests that can distinguish the block model from the null model with vanishing Type I and Type II errors. This leads us to the question:

Can we characterize the asymptotic power of the optimal (likelihood ratio) test at significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
? If so, can the optimal power achieved efficiently?

In this paper, we explicitly characterize the asymptotic power of the likelihood ratio test. Moreover, we prove that it can be achieved efficiently by a test based on low-degree polynomials of the adjacency matrix when the weak recovery, or equivalently detection, is impossible. We also investigate the relationship between these statistical tasks and the mutual information between the observed graph and the community structure.

1.1
Our results for symmetric block models

To illustrate our general results, we first consider the well-studied setup of sparse symmetric block models with
q\geq 2
communities. Let
V
be a vertex set of size
n\geq 1
. For parameters
a,b>0
, let
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n})
denote the model of random graphs in which each vertex
u\in V
is assigned a label
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}\in\{1,2,...,q\}
uniformly at random, and then each possible edge
(u,v)
is included with probability
\frac{a}{n}
if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}
and with probability
\frac{b}{n}
if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}\neq\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}
. The average degree of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
equals
d\equiv\frac{a+(q-1)b}{q}
. The corresponding random graph with no community structure is
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})\equiv\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{d}{n},\frac{d}{n})
is the sparse Erdos-Renyi graph with average degree
d
. Observe that the parameters
(a,b)
is in one-to-one correspondence with the average degree
d
and the parameter

\lambda\equiv\frac{a-b}{a+(q-1)b}\in\Big(-\frac{1}{q-1}\,,\,1\Big)\,.

Here,
\lambda
corresponds to the second eigenvalue of a certain stochastic matrix, namely the transition matrix from the color of a vertex to its neighbor or child (cf. (
1
)). This parametrization is emphasized in several works on the block model
[
Mas14
,
MNS18
,
BLM15
,
AS15
,
AS18
]
and on phylogeny reconstruction problem
[
Mos04
,
DMR11
,
MRS11
,
RS17
]
. We are interested in high-dimensional setting where
n\to\infty
as
q,d,\lambda
are fixed.

A first observation is that by fixing
\lambda\in(-\frac{1}{q-1},1)
and increasing
d
from
0
to
\infty
, the task of weak recovery is monotone in
d
since larger
d
amounts to more information. Indeed given a sample from the model with a certain
d
by deleting each edge independently with probability
t\in[0,1]
we obtain a perfect sample of the model with the same
\lambda
and the new degree
t\cdot d
. Thus, there exists a critical point
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, which we call the
weak recovery threshold
, at which the weak recovery becomes information theoretically possible (see Definition
2.3
). Our result for the block model shows that the weak recovery and detection is equivalent possibly except for the critical point
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Special case of Theorem
2.13
)
Consider the sparse symmetric block model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n})
with
q\geq 2
communities and parameters
a,b>0
. For average degree
d\equiv\frac{a+(q-1)b}{q}
below the weak recovery threshold
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, the following holds.

(1)

\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n})
is mutually contiguous with the Erdos-Renyi graph
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})
. That is, for a sequence of events
(\mathscr{A}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
if and only if
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{A}_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus, detection is impossible, i.e. there is no test that distinguishes
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
from
\boldsymbol{G}
with power tending to
1
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, there is no consistent estimator for
a,b>0
.

(2)

The mutual information between
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
and the community structure
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
per-vertex
I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})/n
converges to
\frac{d}{2}I_{0}(q,\lambda)
as
n\to\infty
, where
I_{0}(q,\lambda)
is the mutual information of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\lambda}\in[q]
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in[q]
defined in Eq. (
2
) below.

On the contrary, above the weak recovery threshold
d>d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
,

(3)

\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n})
is not mutually contiguous to the Erdos-Renyi graph
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})
. Moreover, there exists a sequence of events
(\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that after passing to a subsequence,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n})\leq 2e^{-\eta n}
and
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n})\geq 1-2e^{-\eta n}
hold for
\eta>0
not depending on
n
. Thus, detection is possible, and along a subsequence, there exists a statistical test that distinguishes
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
from
\boldsymbol{G}
with power tending to
1
exponentially fast as
n\to\infty
.

(4)

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})<\frac{d}{2}I_{0}(q,\lambda)
holds.

Our results significantly advance the understanding of stochastic block models by unifying and extending previous findings. While earlier studies have established mutual contiguity in specific parameter regimes such as the disassortative case where
a<b
through combined results in
[
COKPZ18
,
COEJ+18
,
COKM20
]
, the case of
q=2
communities
[
MNS15
]
, and where
d
is sufficiently small
[
BMNN16
]
. These works each have their own limitations as they rely on methods like the ”interpolation technique,” which fails for the assortative case
a>b
, or the ”second moment method,” which does not hold when
d
is sufficiently close to
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
. Our theorems overcome these limitations by covering the maximal range of parameters. Furthermore, we extend our results to a broader context by applying them to hypergraph stochastic block models, accommodating interactions of order
k\geq 2
among communities and allowing for non-symmetric connection probabilities between different communities. For the most general formulations of our results within the framework of planted factor models, we refer to Section
2
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
hinges on a novel equivalence between the impossibility of weak recovery and the
near-orthogonality
of the samples drawn from the posterior (see Theorem
2.10
). Specifically, the key insight in proving Theorem
1.1
-
(1)
is that below the weak recovery threshold
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, appropriately truncating the likelihood ratio based on
overlaps
of posterior samples reduces the second moment of the likelihood ratio by a multiplicative factor of
e^{\Omega(n)}
, while affecting the first moment by only
1-o(1)
. We integrate this insight with other techniques such as small subgraph conditioning from combinatorics
[
Jan95
,
Wor99
]
and I-MMSE relation from information theory
[
GWSV11
]
. We believe our proof techniques illuminate the relationship between weak recovery and detection and have broad applicability in studying the equivalence of these two tasks in high-dimensional statistics. For detailed proofs, we refer to Section
3
.

Note that Theorem
1.1
does not cover the critical case
d=d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
. This is not a coincidence: there are cases where the equivalence of detection and weak recovery fails for
d=d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
! For example,
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
matches the
Kesten-Stigum threshold

d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}=\lambda^{-2}
when
q=2

[
MNS15
]
or when
q\in\{3,4\}
and
d
is large enough
[
MSS23
]
. In these cases, weak recovery is impossible at the Kesten-Stigum threshold
[
MNS15
,
MSS23
]
. In contrast, it is possible to distinguish the block model from the Erdos Renyi graph with probability approaching
1
by counting short cycles as the asymptotic distribution of the number of cycles of fixed length is Poisson with known parameters
[
BJR07
]
.

The quantity
I_{0}(q,\lambda)
in Theorem
1.1
can be characterized as follows. Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in{\sf Unif}(\{1,\ldots,q\})
be a uniform community assignment. Given
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\lambda}
a sample from the conditional distribution

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\lambda}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}=i)=\begin{cases}\lambda+\frac{1-\lambda}{q}&\text{if $i=j$}\\
\frac{1-\lambda}{q}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1)

Then,
I_{0}(q,\lambda)\equiv I(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
is the mutual information between
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
:

I_{0}(q,\lambda)\equiv I(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\frac{1+(q-1)\lambda}{q}\log\big(1+(q-1)\lambda\big)+\frac{(q-1)(1-\lambda)}{q}\log(1-\lambda)\,,

(2)

Thus, Theorem
1.1
-
(2)
establishes that for all
q\geq 2
and
\lambda\in(-\frac{1}{q-1},1)
, below the weak recovery threshold
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, the asymptotic per-vertex mutual information between
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
admits a “single-letter” characterization. Informally, this means that the mutual information is fully determined by local interactions between neighboring vertices. In contrast, when
d>d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, global correlations in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
given
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
emerge, adding an extra term to the mutual information. For a more formal statement, we refer to Lemma
3.18
and Proposition
3.21
below. In particular, in the case of
q=2
communities with
d\lambda^{2}<1
, our result improves upon that of
[
DAM16
]
, where they required the average degree diverge
d\to\infty
. We further prove in Theorem
2.13
that such single letter characterization holds for (possibly non-symmetric) hypergraph stochastic block model below the weak recovery threshold. Moreover, we provide a general condition under which the phase transition of per-vertex mutual information occurs at the weak recovery threshold.

1.1.1
Hypothesis testing for the existence of communities

Theorem
1.1
establishes that the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
marks the sharp phase transition of the following hypothesis testing from a single observation
G

{\sf H_{0}}:G\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}\Big(n,\frac{d}{n}\Big)\;\;\;\;\textnormal{vs.}\;\;\;\;{\sf H_{1}}:G\sim\mathcal{G}\Big(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n}\Big)

(3)

In particular, if
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, then there is no perfect sequence of statistical tests (i.e. tests have asymptotically vanishing Type I and Type II errors), whereas if
d>d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
there exists a test which achieves vanishing Type I and Type II error exponentially fast at least along a subsequence.

However, for
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, Theorem
1.1
does not provide any information on the largest power achieved by the likelihood ratio (LR) test. We next characterize the asymptotic power obtained by LR test below the weak recovery threshold. Let us denote the likelihood ratio of the block model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n})
with respect to Erdos Renyi graph
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})
by

\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}\,.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Special case of Theorem
2.14
)

For average degree
d\equiv\frac{a+(q-1)b}{q}
below the weak recovery threshold
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, the likelihood ratio under null
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})
converges in distribution

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}:=\prod_{\ell\geq 3}\Big\{\big(1+(q-1)\lambda^{\ell}\big)^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}e^{-\frac{(q-1)(d\lambda)^{\ell}}{2\ell}}\Big\}\,,

(4)

where
(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 3}
are independent Poisson random variables with mean
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2\ell}d^{\ell}
. Here, the random variable
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\equiv\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}(d,\lambda)
satisfies the following.

(1)

For
d\in(0,\lambda^{-2})
, which is a superset of
(0,d_{\ast}(q,\lambda))
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
is well-defined (i.e. the infinite product converges a.s.) and has finite second moment.

(2)

If
1\leq d<\lambda^{-2}
holds, then the random variable
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
does not have a point mass. In particular, in the regime
1\leq d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, the likelihood ratio test for the hypothesis testing task (
3
) at significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
has the asymptotic power
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)\equiv\beta_{\ast}(\alpha;q,d,\lambda)\in(0,1)
defined by

\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)=\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\geq C_{\alpha}\}\big]\,.

(5)

Here,
C_{\alpha}>0
is an arbitrary constant satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\geq C_{\alpha})=\alpha
,
whose existence is guaranteed.

The threshold
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}:=\lambda^{-2}
is called the
Kesten-Stigum
(KS) threshold, which was first discovered in the context of the Markov processes on trees in the seminal work of Kesten and Stigum
[
KS66
]
. In the context of sparse block model, it was first conjectured in
[
DKMZ11
]
that above the KS threshold
d\lambda^{2}>1
, the weak recovery can be done in a computationally efficient way. This conjecture was proved for symmetric block model with
q=2
communities by
[
Mas14
,
MNS18
,
BLM15
]
and for general block model by
[
AS18
]
. In particular, the fact that the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
is at most the KS threshold
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
for any
q\geq 2
and
\lambda\in(-\frac{1}{q-1},1)
follows from the result
[
AS18
]
.

In the subcritical regime
d<1
, the random variable
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
is a discrete random variable supported on the countable set

\Big\{z:z=(1-d\lambda)^{\frac{q-1}{2}}\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\big(1+(q-1)\lambda^{\ell}\big)^{x_{\ell}}\textnormal{ for some $x=(x_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}$ with finitely many non-zero elements.}\Big\}\,.

Thus, for
d<1
the weak convergence of the likelihood ratio obtained in Theorem
1.2
only characterizes the asymptotic power of the
non-randomized
LR test for the hypothesis test (
3
) (see also Corollary
3.14
). This is because the LR test might require randomization since the law of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
is atomic for
d<1
. We refer to
[
LR05
, Section 3.2]
for the description of randomization in LR tests.

1.1.2
Computational aspects of hypothesis testing

We next discuss the computational aspects in the hypothesis testing (
3
). Although the LR test achieves largest power for hypothesis test (
3
) at any significance level by the Neyman-Pearson lemma, it is far from clear how to efficiently approximate the likelihood ratio
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
. In fact,
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
can be interpreted as a certain
partition function
from statistical physics, and it is often NP-hard (or even #P-hard) to approximate partition functions,
see e.g.
[
JS93
,
Ist00
,
GJ07
]
.
Note that given an assignment of the communities
\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V}
and
G=(V,E)
, respectively denote the number of monochromatic edges and non-edges by

e_{0}(G,\underline{\sigma}):=\big|\big\{(u,v)\in E:\sigma_{u}=\sigma_{v}\big\}\big|\,,\;\;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{e}_{0}(G,\underline{\sigma}):=\big|\big\{(u,v)\notin E:\sigma_{u}=\sigma_{v}\big\}\big|\,.

Then, it is straightforward to calculate

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)&=q^{-n}\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\bigg(\frac{n-a}{n-(a+(q-1)b/q}\bigg)^{\widetilde{e}_{0}(G,\underline{\sigma})}\bigg(\frac{n-b}{n-(a+(q-1)b/q}\bigg)^{\binom{n}{2}-|E|-\widetilde{e}_{0}(G,\underline{\sigma})}\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\bigg(\frac{qa}{a+(q-1)b}\bigg)^{e_{0}(G,\underline{\sigma})}\bigg(\frac{qb}{a+(q-1)b}\bigg)^{|E|-e_{0}(G,\underline{\sigma})}\,,\end{split}

and in particular, summing over
\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}
makes the naive computation of
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
intractable.

In contrast, we construct a
computationally efficient
test achieving the (asymptotically) largest power. Given a graph
G
and an integer
\ell\geq 3
, let
X_{\ell}(G)
be the number of cycles of length
\ell
. Then, consider the statistic

\mathcal{T}_{n}(G):=\prod_{3\leq\ell\leq K_{n}}\big(1+(q-1)\lambda^{\ell}\big)^{X_{\ell}(G)}\,,

where the truncation parameter
K_{n}
is chosen to diverge slowly to infnity, say
1\ll K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
.

We note that if
G
is drawn from the sparse block model or the sparse Erdos Renyi graph, then the condition
K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
guarantees that the computation of
\mathcal{T}_{n}(G)
can be done in nearly linear time (up to log factors) with high probability. This is because when
G
is sparse, the number of cycles up to length
\log\log n
can be counted by looking at depth
\log\log n
neighborhood of each nodes, each of which has at most
(Cd)^{\log\log n}
neighbors with high probability.

In addition, note that
\mathcal{T}_{n}(G)
is a degree
K_{n}
polynomial in the indicator of the edges of
G
. Our next theorem establishes that a test based on this low-degree polynomial
\mathcal{T}_{n}(G)
is asymptotically most powerful below the weak recovery threshold.

Theorem 1.3
.

(Special case of Theorem
2.15
)

Let
K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
and
K_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. For a significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, consider the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
which rejects the null
{\sf H_{0}}
in Eq. (
3
) with probability

\phi_{n,\alpha}(G):=\begin{cases}1&\;\;\;\;\textnormal{if}\;\;\;\;\;\mathcal{T}_{n}(G)>C^{\prime}_{n,\alpha}\,;\\
0&\;\;\;\;\textnormal{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Here, the constants
C_{n,\alpha}^{\prime}>0
is chosen so that we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})>C^{\prime}_{n,\alpha})\leq\alpha\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\geq C^{\prime}_{n,\alpha})
, where
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}(n,\frac{d}{n})
. Then, for
1\leq d<\lambda^{-2}
, the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}
achieves the power
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)
defined in Eq. (
5
). That is, under the alternative
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,q,\frac{a}{n},\frac{b}{n})
, we have

\mathbb{E}\phi_{n,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\to\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)\;\;\;\;\textnormal{as}\;\;\;\;n\to\infty\,.

In particular, in the regime
1\leq d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
is asymptotically most powerful for the hypothesis test (
3
).

For
q\geq 5
communities, it is known that for small enough
\lambda
, there is a gap between the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
and the KS threshold
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}\equiv\lambda^{-2}

[
BMNN16
,
AS16
]
. In this case, Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
uncover an important phenomenon: the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
marks the
local to global phase transition
of the block model. Specifically,
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
, the existence of the community structure is fully captured by the
local
structure of the graph, namely the number of short cycles. On the contrary, in the regime
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)<d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, a
global
structure emerges, which cannot be described by the number of short cycles. Indeed, Theorem
1.1
-
(3)
shows that in the latter regime, the asymptotic power of the LR test must equal
1
(cf. Neyman-Pearson lemma) along a subsequence. However, Theorem
1.3
implies that the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}
based on a low-degree polynomial achieves limited power
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)<1
in the intermediate regime
d\in(d_{\ast}(q,\lambda),d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}})
. This discrepancy thus demonstrates
statistical-computational gap
in hypothesis testing. A natural question would be to see if the low-degree test
\phi_{n,\alpha}
achieves the optimal power among all computationally efficient tests in the regime
d\in(d_{\ast}(q,\lambda),d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}})
under a suitable complexity-theoretic conjecture, see e.g.
[
MW23
]
for recent related work.

On the contrary, for
q=2
communities and
q\in\{3,4\}
communities with large enough average degree
d
, it is known that
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)=d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
holds
[
MNS15
,
MSS23
]
. Thus, in these cases, our results yield that there is no statistical-computational gap in hypothesis testing at any significance level. Indeed, if
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)=d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
holds, then the low-degree-polynomial test
\phi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
is most powerful by Theorem
1.1
. Moreover, it is known that above the KS threshold
d>d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, the detection is solvable by efficient algorithms (e.g. by counting short cycles
[
Mas13
,
MNS18
,
Abb18
]
or by a semidefinite programming relaxation
[
BMR21
]
). Specifically, we obtain the following corollary by combining our results with the results of
[
MNS15
,
MSS23
]
.

Corollary 1.4
.

For
q=2
communities or
q\in\{3,4\}
and small enough
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{0}
, where
\lambda_{0}>0
is a universal constant, the results of Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
hold with
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)\equiv\lambda^{-2}
. Thus, in this case, for any average degree
d
such that
d\geq 1
and
d\neq\lambda^{-2}
hold and any significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists an efficient sequence of statistical tests
\varphi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
that is asymptotically most powerful at level
\alpha
.

1.2
Proof techniques

We now summarize the difficulties in proving one of our main results – the convergence of the likelihood ratio
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})
for
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
in Theorem
1.2
– and explain the key insights that we use to overcome them. The implications from Theorem
1.2
to Theorem
1.1
-
(1)
,
(2)
is standard. The results for
d>d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
in Theorem
1.1
-
(3)
,
(4)
rely on a different technique, which involves differentiating
\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
with respect to
d>0
(see Section
3.2
), which we shall not focus here.

A standard approach to proving the convergence of
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})
is through the second moment method supplemented with the small subgraph conditioning method by
[
Jan95
,
Wor99
]
. Since
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})=1
holds by change of measures, the second moment method requires that
\mathbb{E}\big(\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\big)^{2}=O(1)
. Indeed,
[
BMNN16
]
used this approach to show the convergence of
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})
. However, the second moment explodes exponentially fast, i.e.
\mathbb{E}\big(\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\big)^{2}=e^{\Omega(n)}
, beyond the
second moment threshold

d_{\textsf{sec}}(q,\lambda)
(see
[
BMNN16
, Proposition 1]
which is strictly below
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
except when
q=2
, where
d_{\textsf{sec}}(2,\lambda)=d_{\ast}(2,\lambda)=\lambda^{-2}
. Thus, the naive second moment approach fails to cover all
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
when
q\geq 3
.

Our main insight is to relate the impossibility of weak recovery to the
orthogonality
of two i.i.d. samples drawn from the posterior
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\cdot\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
. To be precise, given two samples
\underline{\sigma}^{\ell}\equiv(\sigma^{\ell}_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V},\ell=1,2
, define the
overlap matrix

R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{1}}\equiv\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)\big)_{i,j\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
as

R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V}\mathds{1}\big\{\sigma^{1}_{v}=i\,,\,\sigma^{2}_{v}=j\big\}\,.

Concisely, the overlap matrix
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
represents the empirical distribution of
(\sigma_{v}^{1},\sigma_{v}^{2})_{v\in V}
. In particular, the overlap matrix
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
being close to
\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
, where
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
denotes the prior of the communities, indicates that the two samples
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}
are
near-orthogonal
, i.e. they appear as if they are drawn independently from
\pi^{\otimes V}
. With this notation, we establish in Theorem
2.10
that if the weak recovery is impossible (
d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
) then as
n\to\infty

\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\to 0,

(6)

where
\langle f(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})\rangle_{G}
the expectation with respect to i.i.d. samples from the posterior
(\sigma^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{\star}=\cdot\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G)
(cf. Eq. (
22
)). Intriguingly, we prove the reverse implication where (
6
) implies the impossibility of weak recovery, which may be of independent interest.

Armed with the orthogonality of posterior samples from Eq. (
6
), the crucial step is to truncate
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
to ensure that the two samples drawn from the posterior given
G
are near-orthogonal. Namely, for a sequence of truncation parameters
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, we consider the truncated likelihood function
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{n}(G)\equiv\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{n}(G;\varepsilon_{n})
defined by

\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{n}(G):=\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\varepsilon_{n}\Big\}\,.

Observe that by a change of measure, the truncation affects the first moment by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{G})-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\big|&=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\mathds{1}\big\{\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}}>\varepsilon_{n}\big\}\Big]\\
&=\mathbb{P}\big(\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}>\varepsilon_{n}\big)\,,\end{split}

which tends to
0
by Eq. (
6
) and Markov’s inequality for
\varepsilon_{n}
that tends to
0
sufficiently slowly. Thus, it follows that
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{G})=1-o(1)
.

In contrast to the first moment, the truncation drastically reduces the second moment. We prove in Proposition
5.7
that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{G})^{2}\big]=O(1)
in the
entire regime

d<d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
. Note that this contrasts with
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})^{2}\big]=e^{\Omega(n)}
for
d\in(d_{\textsf{sec}}(q,\lambda),d_{\ast}(q,\lambda))
except for
q=2
.

We emphasize that although similar truncation techniques appear in
[
COKPZ18
,
COEJ+18
]
, which study the disassortative case (
\lambda<0
), there are two major differences in our approach. First, for the assortative case, the conditions
BAL
and
POS
used in those works fail, conditions that were crucial for characterizing
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
via a variational formula. In particular, the condition
POS
enables the “interpolation technique” developed by
[
Gue03
]
for spin glasses. Indeed, it was recently shown that the interpolation technique
provably fails
for (a variant of) assortative stochastic block model in
[
DM24
]
, and determining
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
for
\lambda>0
is a well-known open problem
[
DM22
]
. Therefore, one cannot hope to modify the techniques of
[
COKPZ18
,
COEJ+18
]
in the assortative case. Second, the random variable under consideration in the works
[
COKPZ18
,
COEJ+18
]
is not the likelihood ratio
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})
, and it is rather the
partition function
from statistical physics. In the notation introduced in Definition
2.2
below, this is
Z_{n}(G)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})
in the block model. While the partition function is more natural from the statistical physics point of view, for the assortative block model, it is far from clear how to relate
Z_{n}(G)
with weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
. While the partition function is natural in statistical physics, for the assortative block model it is unclear how to relate
Z_{n}(G)
to the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
. One complication for
\lambda>0
is that
\sigma\mapsto\psi_{G}(\sigma)
is maximized at configurations where all spins are equal, making it difficult to argue that the dominant contributions to quantities like the second moment come from
near-orthogonal
configurations.

Instead, the equivalence of
near-orthogonality
of the posterior samples and the impossibility of weak recovery we establish allows us to relate
d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
with the likelihood ratio function. From a high-level, in the expression of the likelihood ratio function
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\frac{\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}}(\underline{\sigma})]}
in the notations of Definition
2.2
, the term
\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}}(\underline{\sigma})]
“balances” the
\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})
appropriately, enabling the second moment calculations to succeed. For this reason, likelihood ratio function
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
turns out to be more suitable than the partition function
Z_{n}(G)
in second moment computations.

Finally, our proof incorporates additional ideas, such as establishing mutual orthogonality above the weak recovery threshold
d>d_{\ast}(q,\lambda)
(without relying on the conditions
BAL
and
POS
from
[
COEJ+18
]
), and a resampling technique to show that contiguity implies the absence of consistent estimators for
a,b>0
(see Section
3.1
).

1.3
Further related work

Stochastic block models and their hypergraph analogs:
The stochastic block model, first introduced in
[
HLL83
]
, has been studied extensively in statistics
[
SN97
,
BC09
,
RCY11
]
and in computer science
[
DF89
,
JS98
,
CK01
,
McS01
,
CO10
]
. The block model in the sparse regime became a major object of research due to the landmark paper
[
DKMZ11
]
. In particular,
[
DKMZ11
]
conjectured a different set of phase transitions within the sparse block model, where the Kesten-Stigum (KS) threshold
[
KS66
]
plays a crucial role. Later, it was shown in a series of works
[
Mas14
,
MNS18
,
BLM15
,
AS15
,
AS18
]
that above the KS threshold, there is an efficient algorithm that achieves both weak recovery and detection. The tightness/non-tightness of KS threshold for weak recovery was also studied by a number of papers
[
MNS15
,
AS16
,
BMNN16
,
RTSZ19
,
MSS23
]
. We refer to the survey by Abbe
[
Abb18
]
for the developments of the block model for more references.

More recently, community detection in sparse hypergraph has also gained significant interest. When the average degree is at least of order
\Omega(\log^{2}n)
, Ghoshdastidar and Dukkipati
[
GD17a
]
proved that a spectral algorithm achieves near-perfect recovery of the community structure. When the average degree is of order constant,
[
ACKZ15
]
conjectured that a phase transition occurs at the KS threshold - above this threshold, efficient weak recovery is possible, and impossible below the threshold. Efficient recovery above the KS threshold was proved for hypergraph models for 2 communities by Pal and Zhu
[
PZ21
]
and general case by Stephan and Zhu
[
SZ22
]
. Gu and Polyanskiy
[
GP23
]
recently showed that the information theoretic threshold for weak recovery is the KS threshold within the
r
uniform hypergraph stochastic block model for
r=3,4
while the same is not true for
r\geq 7
.

General factor models:
The framework for factor models capture many standard models in theoretical computer science such as k-SAT models and in statistical physics such as Potts models (see e.g. Chapter 9 in
[
MM09
]
). The planted factor models was initially introduced to study the phase transitions of random constraint satisfaction problems
[
ACO08
,
KZ09
]
. Later, Coja-Oghlan, Krzakala, Perkins, Zdeborová
[
COKPZ18
]
studied the information theoretic thresholds for planted factor models that satisfy certain convexity and balanced conditions. In particular,
[
COKPZ18
]
obtained an explicit variational principle for the weak recovery threshold for the disassortative block models. Subsequently, Coja-Oghlan, Efthymiou, Jaafari, Kang, Kapetanopoulos
[
COEJ+18
]
proved that in the disassortative case, the planted and the null models are mutually contiguous below the weak recovery threshold. The work
[
COEJ+18
]
also introduced the Kesten-Stigum bound for planted factor models in the uniform prior
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
case. The analysis of
[
COKPZ18
,
COEJ+18
]
was generalized to factor models with hard constraints by
[
COKM20
]
. The planted models also encompass certain high-dimensional Bayesian inference problems as studied in
[
BPS21
,
BP22
]
.

Statistical Computational Gaps:
In studying the computational complexity of statistical tasks such as weak recovery and
detection, it is natural to question the average case complexity of the task. Many recent works attempt to answer this question using a number of different perspectives.
These include average case reduction to a widely-believed statistically hard problems such as the hidden-clique problem
[
Jer92
,
Kuč95
]
, see e.g.
[
BB20
]
.
A different approach is to study a restricted class of algorithms such as local algorithms (see e.g.
[
GS14
]
and follow up work), SQ algorithms
[
Kea98
]

or low-degree polynomials. In particular, a recent line of work (see e.g.

[
HS17
,
Hop18
,
KWB19
,
BKW20
,
GJW20
,
HW20
,
BH21
,
Wei20
]
) uses a “low-degree heuristic” to predict computational-statistical gaps for a variety of problems. In this context, Hopkins and Steurer
[
HS17
]
showed that below the KS threshold, functions that can determine if two vertices are in the same community better than random have to be of degree at least
\Omega(n)
.

2
Main results in the general case

Our results in the most general setting are stated in the framework of planted factor models, which is a generalization of block models. In Sections
2.1
,
2.2
, we define and state our main results for the planted factor model and the associated inference tasks: weak recovery and detection. In Section
2.3
, we apply these results to hypergraph stochastic block models (HSBM).

2.1
Planted factor models

Recall that the sparse symmetric block model is defined by by first “planting” a community structure
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v})_{v\in V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}{\sf Unif}(\{1,\ldots,q\})
. Subsequently, based on this planted structure, a linear number of edges are drawn. Likewise, in the planted factor models, communities (also termed as spins) are represented as
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v})_{v\in V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\pi
, where
\pi
encodes the community’s prior. Using
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
, we create a linear number of clauses, with each clause connecting
k
nodes. When
k=2
, these clauses are analogous to the edges of the block model. However, allowing
k\geq 3
enables us to consider hypergraph analogs of the block models called the hypergraph stochastic block models (HSBM) considered in
[
GD17a
,
GD17b
,
ACKZ15
]
. In addition, a notable extension in planted factor models is the consideration of clause connectivity probabilities as random variables. Such extension ensures that the planted factor models encompass planted constraint satisfaction problems studied in statistical physics and computer science
[
AJM05
,
JMS07
,
KMZ14
,
FPV15
]
.

We first define the necessary notations that will be used throughout the paper. Given
q\geq 2
, we denote by
[q]\equiv\{1,\ldots,q\}
the finite set of spins or communities. We let
\pi\in\mathscr{P}([q])
be a probability measure on
[q]
which represents the prior of different communities.

We let
k\geq 2
denote the number of interaction between different nodes. Further, we let
\Psi
be a finite set of
weight functions
descirbed by

\psi:\{1,\ldots,q\}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,.

Here, we assumed the weight functions to have positive values, which corresponds to
positive temperature
models in statistical physics language. Let
p\in\mathscr{P}(\Psi)
be a prior distribution of the weight functions. Without loss of generality we let
\Psi=\textnormal{supp}(p)
since otherwise we can reduce the set
\Psi
.

Throughout, we assume the following: for any permutation
\theta\in S_{k}
, let
\psi^{\theta}:[q]^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
defined by
\psi^{\theta}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k})=\psi(\sigma_{\theta(1)},\ldots,\sigma_{\theta(k)})
. Then, for any
\psi\in\Psi
and
\theta\in S_{k}
, we assume that

\psi^{\theta}\in\Psi\quad\textnormal{and}\quad p(\psi^{\theta})=p(\psi)>0\,,

(7)

which reflects the exchangeability of the model with respect to the different variables.

A (bipartite) factor graph
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
consists
of the following.

•

The set of variables
V:=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and the set of clauses (a.k.a. function nodes)
F:=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
.

•

The set of edges
E
, where
e=(av)\in E
connects
a\in F
and
v\in V
. Here, each clause
a\in F
is connected to
k
variables, and we denote its neighborhood by
\delta a:=\{v\in V:a\sim v\}
with the convention that
\delta a=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})
is ordered. We let
\delta_{i}a\equiv v_{i}
be the
i
’th variable adjacent to
a
for
1\leq i\leq k
.

•

The set of weight function
(\psi_{a})_{a\in F}
. Here,
\psi_{a}\in\Psi
is the weight function assigned to a clause
a\in F
. Since
\delta a
is ordered, for an assignment of communities
\underline{x}=(x_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V}
, the expression
\psi_{a}(\underline{x}_{\delta a})\equiv\psi_{a}(x_{v_{1}},\ldots,x_{v_{k}})
is well-defined.

Given the prior
p
on
\Psi
, the random graph model with no community (a.k.a. planted) structure corresponds to the
null model
defined below.

Definition 2.1
.

(
The null model
) Given
V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
,
F=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
, and
p\in\mathscr{P}(\Psi)
, the null model
\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)\equiv\boldsymbol{G}(n,m,p)
is a factor graph
(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
defined as follows. For each
a\in F
, its neighborhood
\delta a\in V^{k}
is drawn independently and uniformly at random from
V^{k}
. The weight function
\psi_{a}
is drawn i.i.d. from
p
, i.e.
(\psi_{a})_{a\in F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}p
.

Given a factor graph
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
and
\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}
, let

\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma}):=\prod_{a\in F}\psi_{a}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})\,.

(8)

Subsequently, the random graph model with a community structure corresponds to the
planted model
.

Definition 2.2
.

(
The planted model
) Given
V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
,
F=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
, and priors
\pi\in\mathscr{P}([q])
and
p\in\mathscr{P}(\Psi)
, the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},p,\pi)
is a factor graph defined as follows. First draw a planted/community structure
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{n})
by
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\pi
. Given
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
, draw
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
from the distribution

\mathbb{P}\Big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\Big)=\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G)\cdot\frac{\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})]}\,,

where
\mathbb{E}
denotes the expectation with respect to the null model
\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)
. Equivalently, given
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=(\sigma_{v})_{v\in V}
, independently draw for each clause
a\in F
the neighborhood
\delta a
and the weight function
\psi_{a}
from the distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\delta a=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\,,\,\psi_{a}=\psi\big)=\frac{p(\psi)\psi(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\sum_{\psi^{\prime}\in\Psi}p(\psi^{\prime})\sum_{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{k}\in V}\psi^{\prime}(\sigma_{v^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots\sigma_{v^{\prime}_{k}})}=\frac{1}{n^{k}}\cdot\frac{p(\psi)\psi(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\,,

(9)

where
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim u:={\sf Unif}(V^{k})
.

The planted model can be specialized to sparse symmetric block model by taking
k=2
,
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
, and
p
to put all of its mass on a specific weight function (see Eq. (
118
)). Within this context, a clause
a\in F
corresponds to an edge connecting
\delta_{1}a
and
\delta_{2}a
in the block model. Thus
m
corresponds to the number of edges in the block model, which is approximately Poisson with mean
dn/2
. Therefore, in the broader planted factor model, we let the clause count
m
to follow a Poisson distribution. Using a concise notation, we let

\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},p,\pi)\,,\;\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{G}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}(n,\boldsymbol{m},p)\,,\;\;\;\;\;\textnormal{where}\;\;\boldsymbol{m}\sim{\sf Poi}(dn/k)\,.

(10)

Further, we respectively denote the probability distribution of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
and
\boldsymbol{G}
by

\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi):={\sf Law}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\,,\;\;\;\;\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p):={\sf Law}(\boldsymbol{G})\,.

(11)

Given a single observation
G
, the two central tasks in statistical inference are
weak recovery
and
detection
.

Definition 2.3
.

(
Weak recovery
)
Consider the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
. We say that
weak recovery is possible
at
d
, if
there exists an
\varepsilon>0
and (sequence of) estimators
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})

that takes as an input the factor graph
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
and returns

\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv(\hat{\sigma}_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V}
, such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon\,,

(12)

where the
overlap
between
\underline{\sigma}^{1}\equiv(\sigma^{1}_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V}
and
\underline{\sigma}^{2}\equiv(\sigma^{2}_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V}
is defined by

A(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}):=\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{\big|\{v\in V:\sigma^{1}_{v}=i,\sigma^{2}_{v}=\Gamma(i)\}\big|}{\big|\{v\in V:\sigma^{1}_{v}=i\}\big|}\Bigg\}\,.

(13)

Here, the maximum is taken with respect to permutations
\Gamma
among the set of communities
[q]
. By fixing
p,\pi
and varying
d>0
, the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}\equiv d_{\ast}(p,\pi)
is defined by

d_{\ast}\equiv d_{\ast}(p,\pi):=\inf\Big\{d>0:\textnormal{weak recovery is possible at $d$ for $\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)$}\Big\}\,.

We remark that Definition
2.3
appeared previously in
[
AS18
,
MSS23
]
. In particular, the maximum over
\Gamma\in S_{q}
is taken in the definition of the overlap
A(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})
to account for symmetries, if any, of the labels of the communities. For example, if
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
, inherent symmetries between communities imply that communities can only be recovered up to a permutation. For an equivalent, but slightly different formulation, see
[
AS18
, Definition 2.3]
.

We further remark that in Theorem
2.10
below, we give equivalent descriptions of the weak recovery that involve two-point correlations and orthogonality of the samples drawn from the posterior. Furthermore, it is crucial to note that in Proposition
3.1
, we characterize a simple condition
{\sf(SYM)}
which ensures that the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(p,\pi)
is non-trivial, lying in the interval
d_{\ast}(p,\pi)\in[\frac{1}{k-1},\infty)
.

Before proceeding further, we make two elementary observations. First, note that the task of weak recovery is monotone in
d>0
. Specifically, if weak recovery is impossible at
d
, then weak recovery is impossible at
d^{\prime}
for all
0<d^{\prime}<d
. This is validated by deleting the clauses of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
independently with probability
d^{\prime}/d
yields a sample drawn from
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d^{\prime},p,\pi)
. In particular, for all
d>d_{\ast}(p,\pi)
, weak recovery is possible at
d>0
. Second, for arbitrary
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}\in[q]^{V}
, the overlap satisfies
A(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})\geq\frac{1}{q}
by taking average over
\Gamma\in S_{q}
instead of taking maximum as in (
13
). Thus, the impossibility of weak recovery is equivalent to

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]=\frac{1}{q}\,,\quad\textnormal{for any (sequence of) estimators $\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})$\,.}

(14)

Definition 2.4
.

(
Detection
)
Consider the following hypothesis testing task given a single observation
G
:

{\sf H_{0}}:G\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)\;\;\;\;\textnormal{vs.}\;\;\;\;{\sf H_{1}}:G\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)

(15)

We say that
detection is possible
at
d
, if there exists a sequence of test
\big(\phi_{n}(G)\big)_{n\geq 1}
, which rejects the null
{\sf H_{0}}
with probability
\phi_{n}(G)\in[0,1]
, such that it achieves vanishing Type
1
and Type
2
errors. That is,

\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[1-\phi_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\big]\to 0\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{as}\;\;\;\;n\to\infty\,,

(16)

where
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
. If there does not exist such sequence of tests, we say
detection is impossible
at
d
.

2.2
Main results for the factor models

In our first result for the planted factor model, we prove that below the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}
and the (generalized) Kesten-Stigum (KS) threshold
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}\equiv d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(\pi,p)
defined in Section
3.1
, the detection is impossible.

Theorem 2.5
.

Consider the planted factor model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
in the regime
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
. Then, the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
is mutually contiguous with the null model
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
. That is, for any sequence of events
(\mathscr{A}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}(\mathscr{A}_{n})\to 0
if and only if
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{G}}(\mathscr{A}_{n})\to 0
. In particular, the detection is impossible at
d
.

In some special cases, it is known that the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}\equiv d_{\ast}(p,\pi)
is at most the KS threshold
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}\equiv d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(p,\pi)
in which case the condition
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
in Theorem
2.5
can be simplified to
d<d_{\ast}
. This is the case for sparse (non-symmetric) block model, which corresponds to
k=2
and
p
being Dirac measure, by
[
AS18
]
. The same holds when
k\geq 3
,
p
is a Dirac measure, and
\pi
is the uniform measure
{\sf Unif}([q])
by
[
SZ22
]
. It would be interesting to show that for general planted factor models
d_{\ast}\leq d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
holds.

In order to prove Theorem
2.5
, we will prove a stronger result (cf. Theorem
3.12
) which characterizes the asymptotic power of the likelihood ratio test. Since such characterization requires extra technical notations regarding
\zeta
-cycles
, we defer the statement to Section
3
. The proof of Theorem
2.5
is in Section
3.1
.

2.2.1
Consequences of contiguity in point estimation

As a consequence of the mutual contiguity in Theorem
2.5
, we establish that it is impossible to consistently estimate the set of weight functions
\Psi\equiv\textnormal{supp}(p)
. Specifically, we consider the following notion of consistency.

Definition 2.6
.

Given
T\geq 1
, consider the parametric family of planted factor models
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
with fixed size of the support
T\equiv|\textnormal{supp}(p)|
. Let
\widehat{\Psi}_{n}(G)
be an estimator that takes as an input a factor graph
G
with
n
variables and outputs a set of weight functions
\{\widehat{\psi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\psi}_{T}\}
, where
\widehat{\psi}_{t}:[q]^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for
1\leq t\leq T
. For a fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we say that the sequence of estimators
(\widehat{\Psi}_{n}(G))_{n\geq 1}
is
\varepsilon
-locally consistent at
(d,p,\pi)
if the following holds. Let
\Psi=\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{T}\}
. Then, for any
\Psi^{\prime}=\{\psi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\psi_{T}^{\prime}\}
such that
\|\psi^{\prime}_{t}-\psi_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
holds for all
1\leq t\leq T
, we have as
n\to\infty

\widehat{\psi}_{t}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\psi^{\prime}\,,\;\;\;\;\;\textnormal{for}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p^{\prime},\pi)\;\;\;\textnormal{and}\;\;\;1\leq t\leq T\,.

Here,
p^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{P}(\Psi^{\prime})
is defined by letting
p^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=p(\psi_{t})
for
1\leq t\leq T
.

To clarify the definition, the non-existence of
\varepsilon
-locally consistent estimator describes circumstances in which, despite having access to the information of the prior of the communities
\pi\in\mathscr{P}([q])
and the mass of each weight functions
(p(\psi))_{\psi\in\Psi}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
, it remains infeasable to estimate the set of weight functions
\Psi\equiv\textnormal{supp}(p)
even within its
\varepsilon
neighborhood. Consequently, our definition of an
\varepsilon
-locally consistent estimator is notably weaker than the usual definition of consistent estimator, which imposes no parameter restriction. Our subsequent corollary whose proof is in Section
3.1
demonstrate that, even within this weaker notion of consistency, estimating
\Psi
below the weak recovery threshold and the KS threshold is impossible.

Corollary 2.7
.

If
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
holds, then for any
\varepsilon>0
, there does not exist
\varepsilon
-locally consistent estimator of
\Psi
at
(d,p,\pi)
.

2.2.2
Asymptotic mutual information

We next consider the mutual information
I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
between the planted factor model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
and the community structure
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\in[q]^{V}
, and the
Kuller-Leibler divergence

D_{\textup{KL}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\,\|\,\boldsymbol{G})
between the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
and the null model
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
. In the regime
d>d_{\ast}
, our results are stated under the following assumption:

•

(MIN):
For a probability vector
\pi\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, define the set

\mathcal{R}_{\pi}:=\Big\{R\in[0,1]^{q\times q}:\sum_{i\in[q]}R(i,j)=\pi_{j}\textnormal{ for }j\in[q]\,,\textnormal{ and }\sum_{j\in[q]}R(i,j)=\pi_{i}\textnormal{ for }i\in[q]\Big\}\,.

(17)

Let the function
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{R}_{\pi}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be defined by

\mathcal{F}(R):=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau})]\prod_{s=1}^{k}R(\sigma_{s},\tau_{s})\,.

(18)

Then, the
R\mapsto\mathcal{F}(R)
is uniquely minimized at
R=\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
.

We remark that in the case
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
, the assumption
(MIN)
was first considered in
[
COEJ+18
]
. In addition, it is straightforward to verify that the sparse symmetric block model satisfies such assumption (see Eq. (
30
) below). The following result shows that under such condition, the normalized mutual information
I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})/n
exhibits a phase transition at the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}(p,\pi)
defined in Definition
2.3
.

Theorem 2.8
.

We have the following.

(1)

Suppose that
d<d_{\ast}
holds. Then as
n\to\infty
, we have

\frac{1}{n}D_{\textup{KL}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\,\|\,\boldsymbol{G})\to 0\,,\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\to\frac{d}{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\log\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\Big)\bigg]\,,

(19)

where
\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}\sim\pi^{\otimes k}
, and we denoted
\xi:=\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]
.

(2)

Conversely, suppose that
d>d_{\ast}
and assume that the condition
(MIN)
holds. Then, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}D_{\textup{KL}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\,\|\,\boldsymbol{G})>0\,,\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})<\frac{d}{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\log\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\Big)\bigg]\,.

(3)

Assuming the condition
(MIN)
, the following holds for any
d_{0}>0
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon)>0
not depending on
n
such that if
\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{0}^{\star})
where
\boldsymbol{G}_{0}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d_{0},p,\pi)
, then
for all
d>d_{0}
, there is an event
\mathscr{A}_{n}^{\ast}
satisfying

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{A}_{n}^{\ast})\leq 2e^{-\eta n}\,,\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}_{n}^{\ast})\geq 1-2e^{-\eta n}\,,

(20)

where
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
. In particular, if
d>d_{\ast}
and the condition
(MIN)
holds, then there exists a sequence of events
(\mathscr{A}_{n}^{\ast})_{n\geq 1}
, a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
, and a constant
\eta>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{A}_{n_{\ell}}^{\ast})\leq 2e^{-\eta n_{\ell}}
while
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}_{n_{\ell}}^{\ast})\geq 1-2e^{-\eta n_{\ell}}
. Thus,
\boldsymbol{G}
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
are mutually orthogonal along a subsequence.

Remark 2.9
.

We note that in general it is not known that if there exists a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
such that the weak recovery is possible at
d
along
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
, i.e. there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
\ell\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}),\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n_{\ell},d,p,\pi)
, then the weak recovery is possible at
d
(or even for
d^{\prime}>d
) along every subsequence. Thus, if we replace
\limsup
with
\liminf
in Eq. (
A
) in Definition
2.3
we may in principle obtain a different threshold for weak recovery. The fact that we do not know that the ‘
\limsup
’ and ‘
\liminf
’ thresholds are the same is the reason that the statements in Theorem
1.1
-(3) and Theorem
2.8
-(3) use subsequences. However, like in many other random graph and spin-glass models
[
COKPZ18
]
we believe that these thresholds should be the same for weak recovery, and similarly for detection. Moreover, as seen in Theorem
2.8
-(3), if we let
d_{\ast}^{\prime}
be the ‘
\liminf
’ threshold where we replace
\limsup
with
\liminf
in Eq. (
A
), then we have mutual orthogonality for the entire sequence above this (possibly different) threshold
d>d_{\ast}^{\prime}
.

2.2.3
Equivalent notions of weak recovery

The proof of Theorem
2.5
is based on a novel equivalence between the impossibility of weak recovery and near-orthogonality of the samples drawn from the posterior. To state the equivalence, we introduce more notations. Given a factor graph
G
with
n
variables and
m
clauses, we denote the posterior
\mu_{G}\in\mathscr{P}([q]^{V})
by

\mu_{G}(\underline{\sigma})=\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=G\big)\,,\quad\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}\,.

(21)

We denote by
\langle\cdot\rangle_{G}
the expectation with respect to samples
(\sigma^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mu_{G}
from from the posterior. That is, for any
L\geq 1
and
f:([q]^{V})^{L}\to\mathbb{R}
, we let

\big\langle f\big(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\ldots,\underline{\sigma}^{L}\big)\big\rangle_{G}:=\sum_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\ldots,\underline{\sigma}^{L}\in[q]^{V}}f(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\ldots,\underline{\sigma}^{L})\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}\mu_{G}(\underline{\sigma}^{\ell})\,.

(22)

Given two samples
\underline{\sigma}^{\ell}\equiv(\sigma^{\ell}_{v})_{v\in V}\in[q]^{V},\ell=1,2
, the
overlap matrix

R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{1}}\equiv\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)\big)_{i,j\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
is defined by

R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V}\mathds{1}\big\{\sigma^{1}_{v}=i\,,\,\sigma^{2}_{v}=j\big\}\,.

(23)

Concisely, the overlap matrix
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
represents the empirical distribution of
(\sigma_{v}^{1},\sigma_{v}^{2})_{v\in V}
. In particular, the overlap matrix
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
being close to
\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
indicates that the two samples
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}
are
near-orthogonal
, i.e.
\underline{\sigma}^{1}
and
\underline{\sigma}^{2}
appear as if they are drawn independently from
\pi^{\otimes V}
. In our subsequent result, we establish that the impossibility of weak recovery is
equivalent
to the near-orthogonality of two samples drawn from the posterior. It plays a crucial role in deriving Theorem
2.5
, but it might also be of independent interest.

Theorem 2.10
.

Given
k\geq 2,d>0,\pi,p
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

Weak recovery is impossible at
d
. That is, (
14
) holds.

(b)

For any
2
distinct variables
u\neq v
and
i,j\in[q]
, we have that as
n\to\infty
,

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\,.

(c)

For any
2
distinct variables
u\neq v
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\,,\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\,.

(d)

The overlap matrix
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
of the samples
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}
drawn from the posterior is trivial. That is, as
n\to\infty
,

\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\to 0\,.

Theorem
2.10
is actually established within a more general framework, where
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
can be replaced by any random variable
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}(\underline{\sigma}^{\star})
that satisfy a certain condition
(EXG)
(cf. Proposition
4.3
).

2.3
Main results for hypergraph stochastic block models

In this section, we apply Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
,
3.12
in the setting of hypergraph stochastic block models (HSBM). Notably, HSBM significantly generalizes the symmetric block model considered in Section
1
. In particular, the results stated in this section immediately imply Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
whose proofs are proved at the end of this section. To begin with, we define HSBM and the associated inference tasks.

Definition 2.11
.

(
Hypergraph Stochastic Block Model
)

Given
k,q\geq 2
, let
M\equiv\big(M(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\big)_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots i_{k}\leq q}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k\geq 2
with positive entries. That is, for any permutation
\omega\in S_{q}
,
M(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})=M(\omega(i_{1}),\ldots,\omega(i_{k}))>0
holds. Also, let
\pi=(\pi_{i})_{i\leq q}\in[0,1]^{q}
be a probability vector, which encodes the prior of different communities. Given
n\geq 1
, let
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\sf HSBM}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,M,\pi)
be a random
k
-uniform hypergraph model with
n
vertices defined as follows. Every vertex
v\in V
is assigned a community
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{v}^{\star}\in\{1,2\ldots,q\}
independently according to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{v}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\pi
. Given the community structure
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
, each possible hyperedge
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})
consisting of distinct vertices
v_{1},\ldots v_{k}\in V
is included independently with probability

M(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{k}})/\binom{n}{k-1}
.

Note that by restricting to
k=2
, HSBM specializes to stochastic block models with prior
\pi\in\mathscr{P}([q])
and connection probabilities encoded by an arbitrary symmetric matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{q\times q}
. Let the average degree of HSBM denoted by

d:=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[q]}M(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}\,.

(24)

Observe that the normalized tensor
M_{0}\equiv\big(M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\big)_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq q}:=M/d
must satisfy

1=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[q]}M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}\,.

(25)

By fixing such
M_{0}
and varying
d
, the weak recovery threshold
d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\equiv d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big)
is defined by

d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\equiv d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big):=\inf\Big\{d>0:\textnormal{weak recovery is possible at $d$ for $\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}\big(n,dM_{0},\pi\big)$}\Big\}\,.

Here, the weak recovery in HSBM is defined analogously to Definition
2.3
. That is, we say that the weak recovery is possible at
d
for
\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}\big(n,dM_{0},\pi\big)
if there exists an
\varepsilon>0
and (sequence of) estimators
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\sf HSBM})
such that Eq. (
A
) is satisfied.

It is crucial to observe that the weak recovery threshold is trivial if the average degree around every node is not the same. That is,
d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big)>0
holds only if the following is satisfied:

1=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1}\in[q]}M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},i)\prod_{s=1}^{k-1}\pi_{i_{s}}\,,\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad 1\leq i\leq q\,.

(26)

To see this, note that weak recovery is possible at
d
just by assigning the communities to each vertex based on their degree. Thus, statements such as “For
d<d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, …” are not vacant only if Eq. (
26
) is satisfied. We further note that if Eq. (
26
) is satisfied, Proposition
3.1
below shows that
d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big)\geq\frac{1}{k-1}
holds.

In the case
M_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{k,q}
, the all-
1
-tensor, the random graph model
\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}^{\sf H}(n,d,k)\equiv\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,d\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k,q},\pi)
does not have community structure, and does not depend on
\pi
. This is the hypergraph analog of sparse Erdos-Renyi graphs with average degree
d
. Thus, the hypothesis test corresponding to Eq. (
3
) which determines the existence of a community structure for a k-uniform hypergraph
G
is given by

{\sf H_{0}}:G\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}^{\sf H}(n,d,k)\;\;\;\;\textnormal{vs.}\;\;\;\;{\sf H_{1}}:G\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,dM_{0},\pi)

(27)

Analogously to Definition
2.4
, we say that detection is possible at
d
if there exists a sequence of tests
\big(\phi_{n}(G)\big)_{n\geq 1}
which achieves vanishing Type 1 and Type 2 errors (cf. Eq. (
16
)).

2.3.1
Contiguity, point estimation, and mutual information in HSBM

We now specialize Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
, and Corollary
2.7
to HSBM. To this end, we first define the KS threshold for HSBM, which is simple to state. Given
M_{0}
and
\pi
, define the matrix
B\equiv\big(B(i,j)\big)_{i,j\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
by

B(i,j)=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-2}\in[q]}M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-2},i,j)\pi_{j}\prod_{s=1}^{k-2}\pi_{i_{s}}\,.

(28)

Note that under the degree condition in Eq. (
26
), the matrix
B
is a stochastic matrix. Thus, if we let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{q}
be the eigenvalues of
B
ordered in decreasing absolute values, then by Perron-Frobenius theorem, we have

1=\lambda_{1}>|\lambda_{2}|\geq\ldots\geq|\lambda_{q}|\,.

Then, the KS threshold for HSBM is defined by

d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}\equiv d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi):=|\lambda_{2}|^{-2}\,.

(29)

The following definition is the translation of local consistent estimators (cf. Definition
2.6
) within HSBM.

Definition 2.12
.

Given
k,q,\pi,M
, consider an estimator
\widehat{M}(G)
, which takes as an input a
k
-uniform hypergraph
G
, and outputs a symmetric tensor of order
k\geq 2
with positive entries. For
\varepsilon>0
, we say that
\widehat{M}(\cdot)
is
\varepsilon
-locally-consistent for
M
at
(d,\pi,M)
if for all symmetric tensor
M^{\prime}
of order
k
such that
\|M^{\prime}-M\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon
holds, we have that as
n\to\infty
,

\widehat{M}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}M^{\prime}\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{for}\;\;\;\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,M^{\prime},\pi)\,.

We reiterate that
\varepsilon
-locally consistency is a weaker notion than standard consistency since the parameter space is restricted to be the
\varepsilon
neighborhood of
M
. Our results establish that below the weak recovery threshold, even this weaker notion of parameter estimation cannot be achieved.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
M_{0}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k\geq 2
with positive entries, and
\pi
be a
q
-dimensional probability vector for
q\geq 2
. For average degree
d
below the weak recovery threshold and the KS threshold
d<d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big)\wedge d_{\textsf{{KS}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, the following holds.

(1)

\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}\big(n,dM_{0},\pi\big)
is mutually contiguous with the Erdos-Renyi hypergraph
\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}^{\sf H}(n,d,k)
. Thus, detection is impossible. Moreover, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, there exists no
\varepsilon
-locally-consistent estimator for
M
.

(2)

The asymptotic per-vertex mutual information between
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}
and the community structure
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
is given by

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=\frac{d}{k}\cdot\sum_{i_{1},\ldots i_{k}=1}^{q}M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\log\big(M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\big)\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}\,.

On the other hand, suppose the average degree is above the weak recovery threshold
d>d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
and the condition
{\sf(MIN)}
holds. Then, we have

(3)

There exists a sequence of events
(\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
, and a positive constant
\eta>0
not depending on
\ell
such that for any
\ell\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n_{\ell}})\leq 2e^{-\eta n_{\ell}}\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{and}\;\;\;\;\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n_{\ell}})\geq 1-2e^{-\eta n_{\ell}}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf ER}^{\sf H}(n_{\ell},d,k)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}\big(n_{\ell},dM_{0},\pi\big)
. Thus, detection is possible along the subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
.

(4)

We have that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})<\frac{d}{k}\cdot\sum_{i_{1},\ldots i_{k}=1}^{q}M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\log\big(M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\big)\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}\,.

We remark the condition
(MIN)
within HSBM is translated to the following.

•

(MIN):
Recall the set
\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
in (
17
). Consider the function
\mathcal{F}_{M_{0}}:\mathcal{R}_{\pi}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by

\mathcal{F}_{M_{0}}(R):=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{k}}M_{0}(\underline{\sigma})M_{0}(\underline{\tau})\prod_{s=1}^{k}R(\sigma_{s},\tau_{s})\,.

Then, the
\mathcal{F}_{M_{0}}(R)
for
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
is uniquely minimized at
R=\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
.

Symmetric HSBM corresponds to the uniform prior
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
and the tensor
M_{0}
taking at most two values
a,b>0
, where
M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})=a
if
i_{1}=\ldots=i_{k}
and
M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})=b
, otherwise. The symmetric HSBM cleary satisfies
(MIN)
since for
R\in\mathcal{R}_{q^{-1}\mathbf{1}}
, it is straightforward to compute

\mathcal{F}_{M_{0}}(R)=1+(a-b)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{q}R(i,i)^{k}\,,

(30)

which is uniquely minimized at
R=q^{-2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}
.

2.3.2
Hypothesis testing in HSBM

Our next result concerns the asymptotic power of the liklihood ratio of the hypothesis test (
27
). To this end, consider the likelihood ratio

\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}_{{\sf ER}}(n,d,k)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,dM_{0},\pi)
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
M_{0}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k\geq 2
with positive entries, and
\pi
be a
q
-dimensional probability vector for
q\geq 2
. For average degree
d
below the weak recovery threshold and the KS threshold
d<d_{\ast}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}\big(M_{0},\pi\big)
, the likelihood ratio under the null
\boldsymbol{G}_{\sf ER}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}_{{\sf ER}}(n,d,k)
converges in distribution to

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}:=\prod_{\ell=2+\mathds{1}\{k=2\}}^{\infty}\frac{(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}}{\mathbb{E}(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}}\,.

Here,
(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
are independent Poisson random variables with mean
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2\ell}\big((k-1)d\big)^{\ell}
, and the constants
(\alpha_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
are defined by

\alpha_{\ell}:=\tr(B^{\ell})-1=\sum_{i=2}^{q}\lambda_{i}^{\ell}\,,

(31)

where
B\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
is defined in Eq. (
28
). Moreover, the random variable
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\equiv\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}(d,M_{0},\pi)
satisfies the following.

(1)

Below the Kesten-Stigum threshold
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
is well-defined (i.e. the infinite product converges a.s.) and has finite second moment
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}^{2}<\infty
.

(2)

For
\frac{1}{k-1}\leq d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, the random variable
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
does not have a point mass. In particular, in the regime
\frac{1}{k-1}\leq d<d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, the asymptotic power of the likelihood ratio test for the hypothesis test (
3
) at significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
is given by
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)\in(0,1)
, where

\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)=\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\geq C_{\alpha}\}\big]\,.

(32)

Here,
C_{\alpha}>0
is an arbitrary constant satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}\geq C_{\alpha})=\alpha
,
whose existence is guaranteed.

We next construct a computationally efficient and most powerful test based on a low-degree polynomial of the adjacency matrix. Given a
k
-uniform hypergraph
G
with
n
nodes, let
X_{\ell}(G)
count the number of cycles of length
\ell
. Here, a cycle of length
\ell
is a set of vertices
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell},v_{\ell+1}\equiv v_{1}\}
, where
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
are connected by an hyperedge for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Then, consider the following statistic based on the cycle counts of
G
:

\mathcal{T}_{n}(G):=\prod_{\ell=2+\mathds{1}\{k=2\}}^{K_{n}}(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{X_{\ell}(G)}\,,

where the truncation parameter is chosen so that
1\ll K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
M_{0}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k\geq 2
with positive entries, and
\pi
be a
q
-dimensional probability vector for
q\geq 2
. Also, let
K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
and
K_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. For a significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, consider the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
which rejects the null
{\sf H_{0}}
in (
27
) with probability

\phi_{n,\alpha}(G):=\begin{cases}1&\;\;\;\;\textnormal{if}\;\;\;\;\;\mathcal{T}_{n}(G)>C^{\prime}_{n,\alpha}\,;\\
0&\;\;\;\;\textnormal{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Here, the constants
C_{n,\alpha}^{\prime}>0
is chosen so that we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}})>C^{\prime}_{n,\alpha})\leq\alpha\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}})\geq C^{\prime}_{n,\alpha})\,,

where
\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}_{{\sf ER}}(n,d,k)
. Then, for
\frac{1}{k-1}\leq d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}
achieves the power
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)
in Eq. (
32
). That is, under the alternative
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,dM_{0},\pi)
, we have

\mathbb{E}\phi_{n,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})\to\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)\;\;\;\;\textnormal{as}\;\;\;\;n\to\infty\,.

In particular, in the regime
\frac{1}{k-1}\leq d<d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, the test
\phi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
is asymptotically most powerful for the hypothesis test (
3
).

For symmetric HSBM, it was shown in
[
GP23
]
that the weak recovery threshold equals the KS threshold for
q=2
and
k\in\{3,4\}
. Thus, combining this with our results give the following corollary.

Corollary 2.16
.

Consider symmetric HSBM with
q=2
communities and
k\in\{3,4\}
interactions. Then, Theorems
2.13
,
2.14
,
2.15
hold with
d_{\ast}^{H}(M_{0},\pi)\equiv d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
. Thus, in these cases, for any average degree
d
