such that
d\geq\frac{1}{k-1}
and
d\neq d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
hold, and any significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists an efficient sequence of statistical tests
\varphi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
that is asymptotically most powerful at level
\alpha
.

3
Proofs

This section provides the proof of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
, and Corollary
2.7
while deferring some of the technical steps to the appendices. Theorems
2.10
,
2.13
,
2.14
, and
2.15
are derived from our results for planted factor models and their proof is deferred to Appendix
C
.

Notations:

The notation
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}
is used for the expectation with respect to
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k})\sim\pi^{\otimes k}
, while
\mathbb{E}_{p}
is used for the expectation with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
. Additionally
u={\sf Unif}(V^{k})
denotes the uniform distribution among
k
tuples of variables, and
\mathbb{E}_{u}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim u
. These notations can also be used jointly. For instance,
\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim\pi
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}\sim\pi^{\otimes k}
. We reserve the greek letter
\xi
to denote

\xi:=\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]\,.

We denote by
\mathscr{P}(\Omega)
the set of probability measures on a finite set
\Omega
.

3.1
Proof of Theorem
2.5
and Corollary
2.7

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.5
and its consequences in hypothesis testing (see Corollaries
3.14
,
3.16
) and point estimation in Corollary
2.7
. The proof of Theorem
2.5
and Corollary
2.7
is at the end of Section
3.1.3
and
3.1.5
respectively. To begin with, we consider the following property, which guarantees that the task of weak recovery is non-trivial.

•

{\sf(SYM):}
For any
\psi\in\Psi
,
1\leq s\leq k
, and
\tau\in[q]
,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s}=\tau]=\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]
holds.

Indeed, we prove that if
{\sf(SYM)}
is violated, then weak recovery is possible for any
d>0
based on estimating communities based on the degree of each variables. On the contrary, if
{\sf(SYM)}
is satisfied, then we prove that the task of weak recovery is non-trivial. Specifically, the following result is proven in Section
A
.

Proposition 3.1
.

If
{\sf(SYM)}
does not hold for
p,\pi
, then weak recovery is possible for any
d>0
. That is, the violation of
{\sf(SYM)}
implies
d_{\ast}(p,\pi)=0
. On the other hand, if
{\sf(SYM)}
is satisfied for
p,\pi
and at least one of the weight function
\psi\in\Psi
are not constant functions, then we have
\frac{1}{k-1}\leq d_{\ast}(p,\pi)<\infty
.

Note that Theorem
2.5
and Corollary
2.7
is trivial if
d_{\ast}(p,\pi)=0
. Therefore, by Proposition
3.1
, we may safely assume
{\sf(SYM)}
throughout the proof.

3.1.1
Generalized Kesten-Stigum threshold

This section defines the KS threshold for the planted factor model, which is a generalization of the uniform prior case
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
considered in
[
COEJ+18
]
and sparse (non-symmetric) block model case considered in
[
AS18
]
. For
\psi\in\Psi
, let the matrix
\Phi_{\psi}\equiv\Phi_{\psi}(i,j)_{i,j\in[q]}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
be defined by

\Phi_{\psi}(i,j):=\xi^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2}=j\big]\cdot\pi_{j}\,.

(33)

With a slight abuse of notation, we often identify a matrix
A\equiv\big(A(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\big)_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[q]}
with the linear map
x\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\to Ax\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
. With this convention, let the linear operator
\Xi\equiv\Xi_{p,\pi}
on
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
defined by

\Xi\equiv\Xi_{p}:=\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\otimes\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big]\,,

where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product. In addition, define the linear operator
\Xi_{\ast}\equiv\Xi_{\ast,p,\pi}
on
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
by

\Xi_{\ast}\equiv\Xi_{\ast,p,\pi}:=\mathbb{E}_{p}\bigg[\Big(\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Big)\otimes\Big(\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Big)\bigg]\,,

where
\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
denote the all-
1
-vector. Here, we identified the matrix
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-1\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
with the corresponding linear map on
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Let
\langle\cdot\,,\,\cdot\rangle_{\pi}
denote the unique inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
that satisfies

\langle x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\,,\,y_{1}\otimes y_{2}\rangle_{\pi}:=x_{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)y_{1}\cdot x_{2}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)y_{2}\,,

(34)

where
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\diag(\pi)\equiv\diag\big((\pi)_{i\leq q}\big)
. Further, let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
be the subspace defined by

\mathcal{S}:=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}:\langle x,w\otimes\mathbf{1}\rangle_{\pi}=\langle x,\mathbf{1}\otimes w\rangle_{\pi}=0\,,~\textnormal{for all $w\in\mathbb{R}^{q}$}\big\}\,.

We denote the projection operator onto the subspace
\mathcal{S}
by
\proj_{\mathcal{S}}
. In order to define the KS threshold, we need the following lemma whose proof is deferred to Section
5
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assuming
{\sf(SYM)}
, the following holds for the linear operator
\Xi
on
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

(1)

\Xi
is self-adjoint on the inner product space
\big(\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot\,,\,\cdot\rangle_{\pi}\big)
.

(2)

\Xi(\mathcal{S})\subset\mathcal{S}
and
\Xi(\mathcal{S}^{\perp})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
hold, where
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal subspace of
\mathcal{S}
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\,\cdot\rangle_{\pi}
.

(3)

The composition of
\Xi
and
\proj_{\mathcal{S}}
is given by
\Xi\circ\proj_{\mathcal{S}}=\Xi_{\ast}
.

Having Lemma
3.2
in hand, we denote the eigenspace of
\Xi
restricted to the subspace
\mathcal{S}
by

\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi):=\big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:\Xi x=\lambda x~\textnormal{for some $x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{0\}$}\big\}\,.

(35)

By Lemma
3.2
and the spectral theorem,
\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)
is non-empty whose non-zero elements equal the non-zero elements of
\Eig(\Xi_{\ast})
, the set of eigenvalues of
\Xi_{\ast}
. The KS threshold is defined as follows.

Definition 3.3
.

(Kesten-Stigum threshold)
Let
\lambda_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}\equiv\lambda_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(p,\pi):=\max_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)}|\lambda|=\max_{\lambda\in\Eig(\Xi_{\ast})}|\lambda|
.
The Kesten-Stigum (KS) threshold is defined by
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}\equiv\big((k-1)\lambda_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}\big)^{-1}
.

Notably, the KS threshold for HSBM in Eq. (
29
) is a special case of Definition
3.3
, where the prior
p
puts all of its mass on a single weight function (or equivalently a tensor) on
M
.

3.1.2
Second moment of the truncated likelihood ratio

For a factor graph
G
with
n
variables and
m
clauses, the likelihood ratio (LR) for the hypothesis test (
15
) evaluated at
G
is given by

L(G)\equiv L(G;p,\pi):=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=G\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G\big)}\,,

(36)

where
\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)
is the null model in Definition
2.1
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
is the planted factor model in Definition
2.2
. To show that
\boldsymbol{G}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
are mutually contiguous for
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, we apply the second moment method to a suitable truncation of the likelihood ratio supplemented with the small subgraph conditioning method
[
Jan95
,
Wor99
]
.

A standard observation is that because the randomness of the number of clauses
\boldsymbol{m}\sim{\sf Poi}(dn/k)
causes the second moment
\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G})^{2}
to diverge, it is necessary to first condition on
\boldsymbol{m}=m_{n}
for typical values of
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. In addition, note that
\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m))=1
holds for
n,m\geq 1
by a simple change of measure, thus the second moment method requires to prove that
\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))^{2}=O(1)
holds.

However, even after conditioning on
\boldsymbol{m}=m_{n}
,
\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))^{2}=O(1)
fails to hold in general for all range of
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
. Indeed, by the results of
[
BMNN16
]
, even restricted to symmetric block model with
3
or more communities, there is a non-trivial regime below
d_{\ast}
such that the second moment is exponentially large, i.e.
\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))^{2}=e^{\Omega(n)}
.

To overcome such difficulty, we truncate the likelihood ratio
L(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))
to ensure that the two samples drawn from the posterior is near-orthogonal. Such truncation is motivated by Theorem
2.10
, which is detailed as follows. For a sequence of truncation parameters
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, let the truncated LR be defined by

L^{\ast}(G)\equiv L^{\ast}(G;\varepsilon_{n}):=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=G\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G\big)}\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\varepsilon_{n}\Big\}\,,

(37)

where we recall that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{G}
denotes the expectation taken w.r.t. the sampled
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}
drawn from the posterior (see Eq. (
22
)). A crucial observation is that by a change of measure, the truncation affects the first moment by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\big|L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)-L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))\big|&=\mathbb{E}L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\mathds{1}\big\{\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})}>\varepsilon_{n}\big\}\\
&=\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})}>\varepsilon_{n}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(38)

Note that if
d<d_{\ast}(p,\pi)
and
m_{n}
is sufficiently close to
dn/k
, Theorem
2.10
suggests that the right hand side tends to
0
by choosing
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
appropriately. Indeed, the following lemma guarantees that the truncation does not affect the first moment below the weak recovery threshold for some
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
. Its proof is deferred to Section
5
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
d<d_{\ast}
and
|m_{n}-dn/k|\leq n^{2/3}
for
n\geq 1
. Then, there exists a sequence
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that as
n\to\infty
,
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
and the following convergence is satisfied.

\mathbb{E}\left|L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)-L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))\right|\to 0\,.

On the other hand, the truncation in
L^{\ast}(G)
reduces the second moment tremendously. In particular, the following second moment estimate is at the heart of the proof of Theorem
2.5
. Its proof is the most technical piece of the paper and it is deferred to Section
5
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and assume the condition
(SYM)
. Then, for any sequences
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
|m_{n}-dn/k|\leq n^{2/3}
and
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we have

\mathbb{E}L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)^{2}\leq\big(1+o_{n}(1)\big)\prod_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(k-1)d\lambda}}\,.

(39)

3.1.3
Cycles and small subgraph conditioning

Proposition
3.5
guarantees that
L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
is bounded away from zero with uniformly positive probability by the Payley-Zygmund inequality (a.k.a. second moment method). To boost this probability to close to one, we use the second moment method conditioned on the number of small cycles. This is formalized by the
small subgraph conditioning method
developed by
[
Jan95
,
Wor99
]
. In particular, we have the following theorem from
[
Wor99
]
(see also
[
Jan95
, Theorem 1]
and
[
JLuR00
, Theorem 9.12]
).

Theorem 3.6
.

[
Wor99
, Theorems 4.3]
,
[
JLuR00
, Theorem 9.12]

Let
(X_{in})_{i\geq 1}
be a set of non-negative integer valued random variables indexed by
n\geq 1
, and let
Y_{n}
be another non-negative random variable on the same probability space as
(X_{in})_{i\geq 1}
. Suppose that
\mathbb{E}Y_{n}>0
for large enough
n
, and there exist
\lambda_{i}>0
and
\delta_{i}\geq-1
such that the following hold.

(A1)

X_{in}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{i\infty}
as
n\to\infty
, jointly for all
i
, where
X_{i\infty}
are independent
{\sf Poi}(\lambda_{i})
distributed random variables.

(A2)

For any finite sequence
x_{1},\ldots,x_{L}
of non-negative integers, as
n\to\infty
,

\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\mathds{1}\{X_{1n}=x_{1},\ldots,X_{Ln}=x_{L}\}]}{\mathbb{E}Y_{n}}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{L}\frac{\big((1+\delta_{i})\lambda_{i}\big)^{x_{i}}}{x_{i}!}e^{-(1+\delta_{i})\lambda_{i}}\,.

(A3)

\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}\delta_{i}^{2}<\infty
.

(A4)

\frac{\mathbb{E}Y_{n}^{2}}{(\mathbb{E}Y_{n})^{2}}\leq\exp\big(\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}\delta_{i}^{2}\big)+o_{n}(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Then, we have as
n\to\infty
that

\frac{Y_{n}}{\mathbb{E}Y_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}W\equiv\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1+\delta_{i})^{X_{i\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{i}\delta_{i}}\,.

Moreover, this and the convergence in
(A1)
hold jointly.

Remark 3.7
.

An implicit conclusion of Theorem
3.6
is that
W
is well-defined as the almost-sure-limit of
W_{m}\equiv\prod_{i\leq m}(1+\delta_{i})^{X_{i\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{i}\delta_{i}}
as
m\to\infty
. This can be easily seen by martingale convergence theorem since
(W_{m})_{m\geq 1}
is a martingale with mean
1
and bounded variance by
(A3)
.

In random graph theory, it is common to take
(X_{in})_{i\geq 1}
in Theorem
3.6
as the number of cycles in the random graph with
n
nodes. Indeed, the works
[
COW18
,
COEJ+18
,
NSS24
]
applied Theorem
3.6
for various factor graphs by setting
(X_{in})_{i\geq 1}
as the number of cycles with specific signatures. We consider the following specific notion of cycles with signature
\zeta
, which is essentially the same as the one considered in
[
COEJ+18
]
.

Definition 3.8
.

(
\zeta
-cycle) A signature of order
\ell\geq 1
is defined by a family

\zeta=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell},s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell},t_{\ell})\,,

where
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\in\Psi
, and
s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell},t_{\ell}\in[k]
that satisfy
s_{i}\neq t_{i}
for
i\leq\ell
. In a factor graph
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
, for variables
v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{\ell}}\in V
and clauses
a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{\ell}}\in F
, we call
\{v_{i_{1}},a_{j_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{\ell}},a_{j_{\ell}}\}
a
\zeta
-cycle if it satisfies the following conditions.

•

The indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in[n]
are distinct such that
i_{1}=\min\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
. Similarly,
j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}\in[m]
are distinct and
j_{1}=\min\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}\}
.

•

For
1\leq h\leq\ell
, the weight function
\psi_{a_{j_{h}}}
assigned to the clause
a_{j_{h}}
in
G
is given by
\psi_{a_{j_{h}}}=\psi_{h}
.

•

For
1\leq h\leq\ell
, the
s_{h}
’th variable adjacent to the clause
a_{j_{h}}
in
G
is
v_{i_{h}}
, i.e.
\delta_{s_{h}}a_{j_{h}}=v_{i_{h}}
. Similarly, for
1\leq h\leq\ell-1
,
\delta_{t_{h}}a_{j_{h}}=v_{i_{h+1}}
holds, and
\delta_{t_{\ell}}a_{j_{\ell}}=v_{i_{1}}
.

We let
X_{\zeta}(G)
be the number of
\zeta
-cycles in a factor graph
G
. Also, we let
S_{\ell}
denote the set of signatures
\zeta
of order
\ell
, and let
S:=\cup_{\ell\geq 1}S_{\ell}
be the set of signatures.

The first condition
i_{1}=\min\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\},j_{1}=\min\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}\}
is to avoid overcounting. Also, note that the set
S_{\ell}
is finite for each
\ell\geq 1
because we assumed that
\Psi
is finite. Thus, the set
S
is countable.

We will take
(X_{in})_{i\geq 1}=(X_{\zeta}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big))_{\zeta\in S}
and
Y_{n}=L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
in Theorem
3.6
. To verify the assumptions
(A1)
and
(A2)
, we describe the asymptotic distribution of
X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))\big)
and
X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n}))\big)
. To this end, we introduce additional notations. Let the matrix
\Phi_{\psi,s,t}\equiv\big(\Phi_{\psi,s,t}(i,j)\big)_{i,j\in[q]}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
for
\psi\in\Psi
and
s,t\in[k]
be defined by

\Phi_{\psi,s,t}(i,j):=\xi^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}=j\big]\cdot\pi_{j}\,.

(40)

Notably,
\Phi_{\psi,1,2}=\Phi_{\psi}
holds by definition. Moreover, for a signature
\zeta=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell},s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell},t_{\ell})\in S_{\ell}
, we let
\Phi_{\zeta}:=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\psi_{i},s_{i},t_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
and define the constants
\lambda_{\zeta},\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}
by

\lambda_{\zeta}\equiv\lambda_{\zeta}(k,d,p):=\frac{1}{2\ell}\left(\frac{d}{k}\right)^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p(\psi_{i})\,,\quad\quad\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}\equiv\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}(k,d,\pi,p):=\lambda_{\zeta}\cdot\tr\Big(\Phi_{\zeta}\Big)\,.

(41)

In order to obtain our results for hypergraph stochastic block models, we condition the planted factor model on a specific event
\mathscr{G}_{n}
defined as follows. Let the event
\mathscr{G}_{n}
consist of factor graphs
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
with
|V|=n
variables such that

(H1)

Every clauses is connected to
k
different variables, i.e. for any
a\in F
,
|\delta a|=k
.

(H2)

For any
a,a^{\prime}\in F
with
a\neq a^{\prime}
, the set of neighbors of
a
is distinct from
a^{\prime}
, i.e.
\delta a
does not equal any permutation of
\delta a^{\prime}
.

By using the moment method,
[
COEJ+18
, Proposition 3.12]
deduced the following result.

Fact 3.9
.

[
COEJ+18
, Proposition 3.12]
Assume the condition
{\sf(SYM)}
. Then, for
|m_{n}-dn/k|\leq n^{2/3}
, we have the following.

(1)

X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\zeta,\infty}
as
n\to\infty
, jointly for all
\zeta\in\cup_{\ell\geq 1}S_{\ell}
, where
X_{\zeta,\infty}
are independent
{\sf Poi}(\lambda_{\zeta})
distributed random variables.

(2)

X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}X^{\star}_{\zeta,\infty}
as
n\to\infty
, jointly for all
\zeta\in\cup_{\ell\geq 1}S_{\ell}
, where
X^{\star}_{\zeta,\infty}
are independent
{\sf Poi}(\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta})
distributed random variables.

(3)

Let
C(G):=\sum_{\zeta\in S_{1}}X_{\zeta}(G)+\mathds{1}\{k=2\}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{2}}X_{\zeta}(G)
. Then, note that for
k=2
, the event
\mathscr{G}_{n}
equals
\{C(G)=0\}
, and in general
\mathscr{G}_{n}\subseteq\{C(G)=0\}
holds. For
k\geq 3
, the probability of
\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
satisfying
(H2)
is
O(1/n)
, and we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\big(C(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))=0\big)&=\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\in\mathscr{G}_{n}\big)+O(1/n)\,,\\
\mathbb{P}\big(C(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n}))=0\big)&=\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})\in\mathscr{G}_{n}\big)+O(1/n)\,.\end{split}

Remark 3.10
.

We remark that the results of
[
COEJ+18
, Proposition 3.12]
are only stated for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}(n,m_{n})
instead of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
,
\pi={\sf Unif}([q])
, and when
p
satisfies an extra condition called
{\sf(BAL)}
. Here,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}(n,m_{n})
is a certain ‘tweaked version’
of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
defined in Section 3.2 therein. In particular, the condition
{\sf(BAL)}
therein guarantees that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}(n,m)
is mutually contiguous with
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)
(see
[
COEJ+18
, Lemma 3.2]
). However, a cursory examination of
[
COEJ+18
, Proposition 3.12 and Lemma 8.2]
reveals that the claim was proven in two stages: first, they calculate the joint moments of
\big(X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n}))\big)_{\zeta\in\cup_{\ell\geq 1}S_{\ell}}
up to
o(1)
error (see Eq. (8.10)) to determine its asymptotic distribution and then transalte the result to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}(n,m_{n})
using the condition
{\sf(BAL)}
. Since the argument of
[
COEJ+18
, Proposition 3.12]
works verbatim to show Fact
3.9
, we omit its proof.

To verify the assumptions
(A3)
and
(A4)
, we need the following lemma which relates the quantities
\lambda_{\zeta},\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}
in (
41
) and
\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi\circ\Lambda^{\otimes 2})
in (
35
). Its proof is deferred to Section
5
.

Lemma 3.11
.

For a signature
\zeta
, define the constant

\delta_{\zeta}:=\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}/\lambda_{\zeta}-1=\tr(\Phi_{\zeta})-1\,.

Assuming
{\sf(SYM)}
and
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, we have that

\exp\bigg(\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}\bigg)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(k-1)d\lambda}}\,.

By combining our second moment estimate in Proposition
3.5
and small subgraph conditioning method in Theorem
3.6
, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 3.12
.

For average degree
d
below the weak recovery threshold and the Kesten-Stigum threshold
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, the likelihood ratio evaluated at the null model
\boldsymbol{G}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
converges in distribution to

L(\boldsymbol{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}:=\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X_{\zeta,\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,,

(42)

where
X_{\zeta,\infty}\sim{\sf Poi}(\lambda_{\zeta})
are independent Poisson random variables and
\delta_{\zeta}\equiv\lambda_{\zeta}^{\star}/\lambda_{\zeta}-1
. Moreover, the convergence in (
42
) holds jointly with the convergence in Fact
3.9
-(1).

Proof.

By Proposition
3.1
, we assume
{\sf(SYM)}
without loss of generality. Fix any sequence
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
|m_{n}-dn/k|\leq n^{2/3}
holds. We use Theorem
3.6
by setting
Y_{n}\equiv L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
and
(X_{in})_{i\geq 1}\equiv\big(X_{\zeta}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\big)_{\zeta\in S}
. To this end, we check the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A4)
. Firs, Fact
3.9
-(1) implies the first assumption
(A1)
. In addition, Fact
3.9
-(2) and a simple change of measure guarantee that for a finite sequence of non-negative integers
x_{1},\ldots x_{L}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\mathds{1}\big\{X_{\zeta_{1}}=x_{1},\ldots,X_{\zeta_{L}}=x_{L}\big\}\Big]\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{L}\mathbb{P}\big(X^{\star}_{\zeta_{i}\infty}=x_{i}\big)\,.

Meanwhile, Lemma
3.4
shows that the LHS equals
\mathbb{E}\big[L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\mathds{1}\big\{X_{\zeta_{1}}=x_{1},\ldots,X_{\zeta_{L}}=x_{L}\big\}\big]
up to
o_{n}(1)
error. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)=1-o_{n}(1)
holds by Lemma
3.4
since
\mathbb{E}L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)=1
. Thus, it follows that

\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\mathds{1}\big\{X_{\zeta_{1}}=x_{1},\ldots,X_{\zeta_{L}}=x_{L}\big\}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{L}\mathbb{P}\big(X^{\star}_{\zeta_{i}\infty}=x_{i}\big)\,,

which verifies
(A2)
.
(A3)
is immediate from Lemma
3.11
. Furthermore, for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, Proposition
3.5
shows that

\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big(L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\Big)^{2}}{\Big(\mathbb{E}L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\Big)^{2}}\leq\big(1+o_{n}(1)\big)\mathbb{E}\Big(L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\Big)^{2}\leq\big(1+o_{n}(1)\big)\prod_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi\circ\Lambda^{\otimes 2})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(k-1)d\lambda}}\,,

where the first inequality holds since
\mathbb{E}L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)=1-o_{n}(1)
. Since Lemma
3.11
shows that the final product equals
(1+o_{n}(1))\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}\big)
,
(A4)
also holds. Therefore, by Theorem
3.6
, we have for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
that

\frac{L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)}{\mathbb{E}L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\equiv\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X_{\zeta\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,,

(43)

and this convergence holds jointly with the convergence of
X_{\zeta}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
in Fact
3.9
-(1). For
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, Lemma
3.4
allows us to translate this convergence to the convergence of
L(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))
:

L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\,,

where the convergence is also joint with the convergence in Fact
3.9
-(1). Finally, since
|\boldsymbol{m}-dn/k|\leq n^{2/3}
holds with probability tending to one by a Chernoff bound, this concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Let
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
. First, observe that the likelihood ratio of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
and
\boldsymbol{G}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
is given
L(\cdot)
since for a factor graph
G
with
n
variables and
m
clauses, we have

\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G)\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{m}=m)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G)\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{m}=m)}=L(G)\,.

(44)

Moreover, Theorem
3.12
guarantees that for
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, the convergence
L(\boldsymbol{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
holds as
n\to\infty
. Note that
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}=1
holds since
X_{\zeta,\infty}\sim{\sf Poi}(\lambda_{\zeta})
are independent Poisson random variables. In addition,
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>0
holds a.s.. Therefore, Le Cam’s first lemma (see e.g.
[
Vaa98
, Lemma 6.4]
or
[
JLuR00
, Proposition 9.49]
) implies that
\boldsymbol{G}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
are mutually contiguous.
∎

3.1.4
Consequences in hypothesis testing

We next state the consequences of likelihood ratio convergence in hypothesis testing. To do so, we need the the following result, which shows that the distribution of
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
contains a point mass depending on the average degree
d>0
. Its proof is deferred to Section
B
.

Lemma 3.13
.

In the subcritical regime
d\in(0,\frac{1}{k-1})
, the support of the random variable
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
equals the countable set

\Big\{z:z=\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{x_{\zeta}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}~\textnormal{ for some $x=(x_{\zeta})_{\ell\geq 1,\zeta\in S_{\ell}}$ with finitely many non-zero elements.}\Big\}\,.

(45)

On the contrary, in the critical or subcritical regime
d\in[\frac{1}{k-1},d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}})
, the random variable
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
does not have a point mass.

As an immediate consequence of Theorem
3.12
and Lemma
3.13
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.14
.

In the subcritical regime
d\in(0,\frac{1}{k-1})
, the asymptotic power of non-randomized likelihood ratio test for the hypothesis test (
15
) at significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
is given by

\beta_{\ast}(\alpha,d,p,\pi):=\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>C_{\alpha}\}\big]\,.

(46)

Here,
C_{\alpha}>0
is an arbitrary constant that satisfy

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>C_{\alpha})\leq\alpha\leq\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\geq C_{\alpha})\,.

In the critical or supercriticial regime
d\in[\frac{1}{k-1},d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}})
, the asymptotic power of (possibly randomized) likelihood ratio test for the hypothesis test (
15
) at significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
is given by
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha,d,p,\pi)
.

Next, we consider computationally efficient hypothesis using the following statistic based on
\zeta
-cycles:

T_{n}(G):=\prod_{1\leq\ell\leq K_{n}}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X_{\zeta}(G)}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,,

where the truncation parameter is taken so that
1\ll K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
. Then, we prove the following lemma in Section
B
by utilizing Fact
3.9
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Under the condition
(SYM)
, let
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and
1\ll K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
. Then, under the null model
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
,

T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\equiv\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X_{\zeta,\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,,

where
X_{\zeta,\infty}\sim{\sf Poi}(\lambda_{\zeta})
are independent Poisson random variables. Under the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
,

T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}\equiv\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X^{\star}_{\zeta,\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,,

where
X^{\star}_{\zeta,\infty}\sim{\sf Poi}(\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta})
are independent Poisson random variables.

We note that the random variable
\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}
is well-defined for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, i.e. the infinite product over
\ell\geq 1
converges almost surely, since
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
is well-defined (cf. Remark
3.7
) with mean
1
from which
\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}
can be obtained by a change of measure (see Eq. (
49
) below). As a corollary, we have the following result.

Corollary 3.16
.

Under the condition
(SYM)
, let
1\leq d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and
1\ll K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
. At significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, consider the non-randomized test
\varphi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
which rejects the null
{\sf H_{0}}
in Eq. (
15
) with probability

\varphi_{n,\alpha}(G):=\begin{cases}1&\;\;\;\;\textnormal{if}\;\;\;\;\;T_{n}(G)>C_{n,\alpha}\,;\\
0&\;\;\;\;\textnormal{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Here, the constants
C_{n,\alpha}>0
is chosen so that we have

\mathbb{P}(T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})>C_{n,\alpha})\leq\alpha\leq\mathbb{P}(T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\geq C_{n,\alpha})\,,

(47)

where
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
. Then, for
1\leq d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, the test
\varphi_{n,\alpha}
achieves the power
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha,d,p,\pi)
defined in Eq. (
46
). That is, under the alternative
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
, we have

\mathbb{E}\varphi_{n,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\to\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)\;\;\;\;\textnormal{as}\;\;\;\;n\to\infty\,.

In particular, in the regime
1\leq d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
, the test
\varphi_{n,\alpha}(\cdot)
is asymptotically most powerful for the hypothesis test (
3
).

Proof.

Let
1\leq d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
. By passing to a subsequence, let
\tilde{C}_{\alpha}\in[-\infty,\infty]
be the limit of
(C_{n,\alpha})_{n\geq 1}
. We first claim that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha})=\alpha
. To see this, note that by Lemma
3.15
and Skohorod embedding, there exists a coupling such that
T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})
converges to
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
almost surely. Moreover,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}=\tilde{C}_{\alpha})=0
holds by Lemma
3.13
. Thus, it follows that

\mathds{1}\{T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})>C_{n,\alpha}\}\to\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha}\}\;\;\;\;\textnormal{as}\;\;\;\;n\to\infty\;\;\;\textnormal{almost surely.}

Similarly,
\mathds{1}\{T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\geq C_{n,\alpha}\}
converges to
\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha}\}=\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\geq\tilde{C}_{\alpha}\}
a.s.. Hence, by dominated convergence theorem, sending
n\to\infty
in (
47
) yield that

\alpha=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})>C_{n,\alpha})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})\geq C_{n,\alpha})=\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha})\,,

(48)

which proves our first claim. Next, note that for any Borel measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\in A\}\big]&\equiv\sum_{x=(x_{\zeta})_{\zeta}}\mathds{1}\Big\{\prod_{\zeta}(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{x_{\zeta}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\in A\Big\}\prod_{\zeta}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{x_{\zeta}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\zeta,\infty}=x_{\zeta})\Big\}\\
&=\sum_{x=(x_{\zeta})_{\zeta}}\mathds{1}\Big\{\prod_{\zeta}(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{x_{\zeta}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\in A\Big\}\prod_{\zeta}\frac{(\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta})^{x_{\zeta}}}{x_{\zeta}!}e^{-\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}}\\
&=\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}\in A\big)\,,\end{split}

(49)

where the second equality holds since
1+\delta_{\zeta}\equiv\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}/\lambda_{\zeta}
. In particular, the distribution of
\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}
does not have a point mass by Lemma
3.13
. Moreover, by repeating the argument in deriving (
48
), Lemma
3.15
shows that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\varphi_{n,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})>C_{n,\alpha})=\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}^{\star}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha})\,.

Meanwhile, the final term equals
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}^{\star}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha})=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha}\}]
by (
49
). Since
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}>\tilde{C}_{\alpha})=\alpha
holds by our first claim, the final term further equals
\beta_{\ast}(\alpha,d,p,\pi)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

3.1.5
Proof of Corollary
2.7

We establish the impossibility of point estimation in Corollary
2.7
based on the mutual contiguity in Theorem
2.5
and a
resampling argument
. Given a factor graph
G=(V,F,E,\{\psi_{a}\}_{a\in F})
and
\gamma\in[0,1]
, consider the
\gamma
-resampling procedure as follows. For each clause
a\in F
, toss a coin independently with probability
\gamma
and let
(Z_{a})_{a\in F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}{\sf Ber}(\gamma)
. If
Z_{a}=1
, then independently resample the neighborhood
\delta a
and the factor
\psi_{a}
of the clause
a
from the “null distribution”:

\mathbb{P}\big(\delta a=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\,,\,\psi_{a}=\psi\big)=\frac{p(\psi)}{n^{k}}\,,

(50)

and if
Z_{a}=0
, keep
(\delta a,\psi_{a})
unchanged. We denote by
G_{\gamma}
the resulting factor graph obtained from
G
by the
\gamma
-resampling procedure. For instance,
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
denotes the factor graph obtained from the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
by the
\gamma
-resampling procedure. Note that since
\gamma
fraction of clauses are resampled according to the null distribution, the probability distribution of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
only gets closer to that of the null model
\boldsymbol{G}
as
\gamma
becomes large. Indeed, in the extreme case
\gamma=1
,
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{1}
is distributed the same as
\boldsymbol{G}
. Thus, the family
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in[0,1]}
is a continuous interpolation of the planted and the null model.

Our next result, which is used for the proof of Corollary
2.7
, shows that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
is contiguous to another planted model described as follows. Let
\Psi=\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{T}\}
and
p\in\mathscr{P}(\Psi)
. Then, let
\Psi_{\gamma}
and
p_{\gamma}\sim\mathscr{P}(\Psi_{\gamma})
be their
\gamma
-modifications defined by

\Psi_{\gamma}:=\{\psi_{1,\gamma},\ldots,\psi_{T,\gamma}\}\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{where}\;\;\;\;\psi_{t,\gamma}(\cdot):=(1-\gamma)\psi_{t}(\cdot)+\gamma\xi\,,

(51)

and
p_{\gamma}(\psi_{t,\gamma}):=p(\psi_{t})
for
1\leq t\leq T
. Here, we recall the notation
\xi\equiv\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]
. The proof of the following lemma is deferred to Section
B
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Suppose that the priors
p\in\mathscr{P}(\Psi)
and
\pi\in\mathscr{P}([q])
satisfy
{\sf(SYM)}
, and consider the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
. Then, for
\gamma\in[0,1]
, the
\gamma
-resampled factor graph
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
is mutually contiguous to the planted distribution with
\gamma
-modified weight functions
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p_{\gamma},\pi)
.

Proof of Corollary
2.7
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
d<d_{\ast}\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
. By Proposition
3.1
, w.l.o.g. we assume
{\sf(SYM)}
.

We first argue that the
\gamma
-resampled graph
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
of the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
is mutually contiguous to the null model
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
for any fixed
\gamma\in[0,1]
. To see this, recall that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
has
\boldsymbol{m}\sim{\sf Poi}(dn/k)
clauses. By Poisson thinning,
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
has
\boldsymbol{m}_{1}\sim{\sf Poi}((1-\gamma)dn/k)
clauses distributed as Eq. (
9
) and
\boldsymbol{m}_{2}
clauses distributed as Eq. (
50
). Thus, the likelihood ratio (a.k.a. Radon-Nikodym derivative) of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
and
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p)
is the same as the likelihood ratio of
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,(1-\gamma)d,p,\pi)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,(1-\gamma)d,p)
. Noting that
(1-\gamma)d<d_{\ast}(p,\pi)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
holds, combining this equality between likelihood ratios with Theorem
2.5
proves that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
is mutually contiguous to
\boldsymbol{G}
.

Hence, by Lemma
3.17
, the planted distribution with
\gamma
-modified weight functions
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p_{\gamma},\pi)
is mutually contiguous to the null model
\boldsymbol{G}
for any
\gamma\in[0,1]
. Note that by taking
\gamma>0
small enough compared to
\varepsilon
, the weights
\psi_{t,\gamma}
defined in (
51
) satisfy
\|\psi_{t,\gamma}-\psi_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
for any
1\leq t\leq T
. Therefore, the mutual contiguity of
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p_{\gamma},\pi)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p_{\gamma^{\prime}},\pi)
for sufficiently small
\gamma\neq\gamma^{\prime}
imply the non-existence of
\varepsilon
-locally consistent estimator of
\Psi
at
(d,p,\pi)
(cf. Definition
2.6
).
∎

3.2
Proof of Theorem
2.8

The key ingredient to prove Theorem
2.8
is to analyze the (normalized) log likelihood ratio evaluated at the planted model
\frac{1}{n}\log L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\big)
. Its relationship between the KL divergence and the mutual information is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.18
.

We have the equality
D_{\textup{KL}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\,\|\,\boldsymbol{G})=\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
. Moreover, the normalized mutual information can be approximated by

\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})+\frac{d}{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\log\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\Big)\bigg]+o_{n}(1)\,.

Thus, to prove Theorem
2.8
, it suffices to analyze
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\big)
. By borrowing terminology from statistical physics, we call
\frac{1}{n}\log L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\big)
the
free energy
since
L(G)
can be interpreted as a partition function by the equality

L(G)\equiv\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})=G\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G\big)}=\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\frac{\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})]}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\,.

Because the weight functions
\psi\in\Psi
are strictly positive, it is well-known that the free energies concentrate tightly around their expectation (see e.g.
[
COKPZ18
, Lemma 3.3]
).

Fact 3.19
.

There exists a constant
c=c(\Psi)>0
that only depends on the set of weight functions
\Psi
such that the following hold. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\bigg|\geq\varepsilon\Bigg)\leq e^{-cn\varepsilon^{2}}\,.

Proof.

Note that if we have two factor graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
, which differ only in a single clause, then
\big|n^{-1}\log L(G)-n^{-1}\log L(G^{\prime})\big|\leq C
holds, where
C\equiv C(\Psi)>0
. Since conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
, the clauses in
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
are independent, the desired inequality is immediate from Azuma-Hoeffding inequality applied to to the Doob martingale w.r.t. the clause revealing filtration.
∎

The next step is to calculate derivative of the expected free energy w.r.t.
d
. Recall that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{G}
denotes the expectation with respect to samples
(\underline{\sigma}^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mu_{G}
from the posterior. If there are only one sample, i.e.
\ell=1
, we omit the superscript and write
\langle f(\underline{\sigma}^{1})\rangle_{G}\equiv\langle f(\underline{\sigma})\rangle_{G}
for simplicity.

Proposition 3.20
.

For any
d>0
and
n\geq 1
, the derivative of the expected free energy is given by

\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\frac{1}{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\bigg[\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\bigg]\,,

(52)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}
is with respect to
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
, and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim u\equiv{\sf Unif}(V^{k})
. The expectation
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}
in the denominator is with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\sim p
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\sim u
.
Furthermore, we can approximate

\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\frac{1}{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}}{\xi}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}}{\xi}\bigg)\Bigg]+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,,

(53)

where
f=O_{k,q,\Psi}(g)
if there exists a constant
C
that only depends on
k,q,\Psi
such that
|f|\leq Cg
.

The first equality (
53
) can be interpreted as the analog of I-MMSE relation from information theory
[
GWSV11
]
within the planted factor model. By appealing to Theorem
2.10
, we prove that below the weak recovery threshold
d<d_{\ast}
, the RHS of (
53
) tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
, whereas for
d>d_{\ast}
it is uniformly positive along a subsequence under the
{\sf(MIN)}
condition. As a consequence, we prove the following Proposition, which plays a crucial role in the proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Proposition 3.21
.

The following holds.

(1)

For any
n\geq 1
and
d>0
, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq 0
.

(2)

If
d<d_{\ast}
, then we have

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\longrightarrow 0\quad\textnormal{as}\quad n\to\infty\,.

(3)

Assume that the condition
(MIN)
holds. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon)>0
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon)
not depending on
n
nor
d>0
such that if
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
and
n\geq n_{0}
, then
\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq\eta
holds.

(4)

If
d>d_{\ast}
and
(MIN)
holds, then there exists a constant
\eta>0
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq\eta>0

The proofs of Lemma
3.18
and Propositions
3.20
,
3.21
are deferred to Section
6
. Here, we prove that they imply Theorem
2.8
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

The first statement is an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.18
and Proposition
3.21
-(2) while the second statement follows from Lemma
3.18
and Proposition
3.21
-(4).

To prove the third statement, assume the condition
{\sf(MIN)}
. Suppose that for some
d_{0}>0
and
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\breve{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}})
where
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d_{0},p,\pi)
. Then, note that for all
d>d_{0}
,
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
where
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d,p,\pi)
. This is because starting from
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
, subsampling each clause independently with probability
d_{0}/d
yields a sample drawn from
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n,d_{0},p,\pi)
by Poisson thinning. Thus, if we let
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon)
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon)
be as in Proposition
3.21
-(3), then
\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq\eta
holds for all
d>d_{0}
and
n\geq n_{0}
. Since in general
\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq 0
holds by Proposition
3.21
-(1) and
\eta
does not depend on
d
, it follows that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq\eta(d-d_{0})\equiv\eta_{d}>0\,.

Then, consider the event

\mathscr{A}_{n}^{\ast}:=\big\{G:L(G)\geq e^{\eta_{d}n/2}\big\}\,.

Then by Fact
3.19
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that for
n\geq n_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n})\geq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\bigg|\leq\frac{\eta}{2}\bigg)\geq 1-e^{-cn\eta_{d}^{2}}\,.

Meanwhile, note that
\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G})=1
holds since
L(\cdot)
is Radon-Nikodym derivative of the law of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
and
\boldsymbol{G}
. Thus, we have by Markov’s inequality that for any
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n})=\mathbb{P}\big(L(\boldsymbol{G})\geq e^{\eta_{d}n/2}\big)\leq e^{-\eta_{d}n/2}\,.

Therefore, by adjusting
\eta
appropriately to account for small
n<n_{0}
, the desired claim (
20
) holds for any
n\geq 1
under the stated assumptions, concluding the proof.
∎

4
Equivalent notions of weak recovery

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.10
. We will prove a generalized statement, where
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},p,\pi)
is replaced by an arbitrary random variable
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}
defined on the same probability space as
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
and satisfy a certain condition
{\sf(EXG)}
. This generalization is stated in Proposition
4.3
, which will be useful for the proof of Lemma
3.4
, but it is interesting in its own right. We first specify the task of weak recovery with respect to
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}
, extending Definition
2.3
. Recall the definition of
A(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})
in (
13
).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random variables which are defined on the same probability space as
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
and take values in a probability space
\mathscr{X}
. We say that weak recovery is possible for
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
and an estimator (i.e. a measurable function)
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})
that takes as an input
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}
and returns
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\in[q]^{V}
such that
\mathbb{E}[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
for large enough
n\geq 1
. Otherwise, we say that the weak recovery is impossible for
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.

Given ‘data’
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}
, we define the ‘posterior’
\mu_{X}\in\mathscr{P}([q]^{V})
as

\mu_{X}(\underline{\sigma})=\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}=X\big)\,,\quad\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}\,.

(54)

We denote by
\langle\cdot\rangle_{X}
the expectation with respect to samples
(\sigma^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mu_{X}
from from the posterior.

The condition we impose on the data
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is

•

Exchangeability: for any
n\geq 1
and any pairs of distinct variables
(u_{1},v_{1})
and
(u_{2},v_{2})
with
u_{i}\neq v_{i},i=1,2
,

{\sf(EXG):}~\Big(\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=j\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}\big)\Big)_{i,j\in[q]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Big(\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{2}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{2}}=j\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}\big)\Big)_{i,j\in[q]}

(55)

By the symmetry of variables, the planted factor model satisfies the property
(EXG)
as verified by the next lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any sequence of
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, if we let
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}=\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
, then the sequence of random variables
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})
satisfy
(EXG)
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}=\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
satisfies
(EXG)
.

Proof.

Fix two sets of distinct variables
(u_{i},v_{i})
,
i=1,2
. We show that for a continuous and bounded test function
f:\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}f\Big(\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)\Big\}_{i,j\leq q}\Big)=\mathbb{E}f\Big(\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{2}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{2}}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)\Big\}_{i,j\leq q}\Big)\,.

(56)

Since
u_{i}\neq v_{i}
, note that there exists a permutation
\kappa:V\to V
such that
\kappa(u_{1})=u_{2}
and
\kappa(v_{1})=v_{2}
. With abuse of notation, denote
\kappa\circ\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{\kappa(v)})_{v\in V}
and for a factor graph
G
, let
\kappa\circ G
be the factor graph defined by mapping
G
by a graph isomorphism that permutes the labels of the variables according to
\kappa
. That is,
\kappa(v)\sim a
in
\kappa\circ G
if and only if
v\sim a
in
G
. Then, the key observation is as follows. Recalling the definition of the planted model (cf. Definition
2.2
) and our assumption that
p(\psi^{\theta})=p(\psi)
holds for
\theta\in S_{\kappa}
, we have

\big(\kappa\circ\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\kappa\circ\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)\,.

Thus,
\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=j\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{2}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{2}}=j\,\big|\,\kappa\circ\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)
holds for any factor graph
G
and
i,j\in[q]
. As a consequence, the LHS of (
56
) equals

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}f\Big(\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)\Big\}_{i,j\leq q}\Big)\\
&=\sum_{G}f\Big(\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)\Big\}_{i,j\leq q}\Big)\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)\\
&=\sum_{G}f\Big(\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{2}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{2}}=j\,|\,\kappa\circ\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)\Big\}_{i,j\leq q}\Big)\mathbb{P}\big(\kappa\circ\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)\\
&=\mathbb{E}f\Big(\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{2}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{2}}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)\Big\}_{i,j\leq q}\Big)\,,\end{split}

which concludes the proof of (
56
).
∎

Under the condition
{\sf(EXG)}
, we then establish the analog of Theorem
2.10
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Assume that the sequence of random variables
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfies the condition
{\sf(EXG)}
. Then, the impossibility of weak recovery for
(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n})
is equivalent to the (analog of) conditions in Theorem
2.10
-
(b)
,
(c)
,
(d)
, where one replaces
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
therein with
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}
.

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

This is immediate from Lemma
4.2
and Proposition
4.3
.
∎

For the rest of this section, we prove Proposition
4.3
. In Section
4.1
, we show that
(a)\Rightarrow(b)\Rightarrow(c)\Rightarrow(a)
holds. In Section
4.2
, we show that
(c)\Leftrightarrow(d)
holds. Throughout, we abbreviate
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}_{n}
. We recall that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{X}
denotes the expectation taken w.r.t. the posterior
\mu_{X}
.

4.1
Triviality of two point correlation

We first substitute the quantity
A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})
in Definition
2.3
by
\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})
, which will be more convenient for the proof of
(a)\Rightarrow(b)
and
(c)\Rightarrow(a)
. For
\underline{\sigma}^{\ell}\equiv(\sigma^{\ell}_{u})_{u\in V}\in[q]^{V},\ell=1,2
, define

\widetilde{A}(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})\equiv\widetilde{A}(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2};\pi):=\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{1}{n\pi_{i}}\sum_{u\in V}\mathds{1}\{\sigma^{1}_{u}=i,\sigma^{2}_{u}=\Gamma(i)\}\,.

(57)

That is
\widetilde{A}(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})
is defined by replacing
|\{v\in V:\sigma_{v}^{1}=i\}|
term in (
13
) by
n\pi_{i}
. If
\underline{\sigma}^{1}=\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
, then such replacement is valid by the concentration of
|\{v\in V:\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=i\}|
around
n\pi_{i}
as shown in the lemma below.

Lemma 4.4
.

There exists a constant
C\equiv C_{q,\pi}>0
such that for any estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
, we have

\big|\mathbb{E}[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})]-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})]\big|\leq Cn^{-1/3}\,.

Proof.

Note that we can write
A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})
by

A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})=\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{\frac{1}{n\pi_{i}}\sum_{u\in V}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\Gamma(i)\}}{\frac{1}{n\pi_{i}}\sum_{u\in V}\mathds{1}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\}}\,.

We show that on a w.h.p. event, the denominator above is close to
1
: since
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v})_{v\in V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\pi
, we have by Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}})\geq 1-q\exp(-2n^{1/3})\,,~~\textnormal{where}~~\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}:=\bigg\{\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in V}\mathds{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\big\}-\pi_{i}\bigg|\leq n^{-1/3},~\textnormal{for all}~i\in[q]\bigg\}\,.

(58)

Thus, it follows that on the event
\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}
, for any estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}
,

1-(\min_{i}\pi_{i})^{-1}\cdot n^{-1/3}\leq\frac{A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})}{\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})}\leq 1+(\min_{i}\pi_{i})^{-1}\cdot n^{-1/3}\,.

Further,
\widetilde{A}(\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2})\in[0,(\min_{i}\pi_{i})^{-1}]
holds, so it follows that on the event
\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}
,

\big|A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})-\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big|\leq(\min_{i}\pi_{i})^{-2}\cdot n^{-1/3}\,.

Combining with (
58
) concludes the proof.
∎

With Lemma
4.4
in hand, we prove
(a)\Rightarrow(b)\Rightarrow(c)\Rightarrow(a)
. We first start with
(a)\Rightarrow(b)
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that weak recovery is impossible for
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}
at
d
. Then, for any
u\neq v
and
i,j\in[q]
,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}
holds as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

We first prove that the impossibility of weak recovery implies
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}
for any
u\in V
and
i\in[q]
. By
(EXG)
, this implies that for every
u\in V
,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i_{0}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
does not converge in probability to
\pi_{i_{0}}
. Now, note that
\max_{i\leq q}\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})}{\pi_{i}}\geq 1
, almost surely, since
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})=1
holds. Hence, it follows that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\max_{i\in[q]}\bigg\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})}{\pi_{i}}\bigg\}\Bigg]>1\,,

(59)

since otherwise,
\max_{i\leq q}\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)}{\pi_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
holds, which implies that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}
for every
i\in[q]
. We now show that (
59
) implies that weak recovery is possible. To this end, for
X\in\mathscr{X}
, consider the estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(X)\equiv(\hat{\sigma}_{u}(X))_{u\in V}
defined by

\hat{\sigma}_{u}(X):=\argmax_{i\in[q]}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}=X)}{\pi_{i}}\right\}\,.

Then, by taking
\Gamma=\id
in the definition of
\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})
(cf. (
57
)), and using
{\sf(EXG)}
(cf. (
55
)), we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{A}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big)\Big]\geq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{1}{\pi_{i}}\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\hat{\sigma}_{u}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})=i\big)=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathds{1}(\hat{\sigma}_{u}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})=i)\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)}{\pi_{i}}\Bigg]\,,

(60)

where the last equality is due to tower property and the fact that
\hat{\sigma}_{u}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
is
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}
-measurable. Note that the RHS equals
q^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\max_{i\leq q}\pi_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big]
. Thus, combining with (
59
), we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{A}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big)\Big]\geq\frac{1}{q}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\max_{i\in[q]}\bigg\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})}{\pi_{i}}\bigg\}\Bigg]>\frac{1}{q}\,.

Therefore, by Lemma
4.4
, weak recovery is possible at
d
, which contradicts our assumption. We thus conclude that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}
for any
u\in V
and
i\in[q]
.

Next, we show that the impossibility of weak recovery implies
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}
for any
u\neq v
and
i,j\in[q]
by using a similar argument as above. Assume by contradiction that there exists
u_{0}\neq v_{0}
and
i_{0},j_{0}\in[q]
such that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{0}}=i_{0},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{0}}=j_{0}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
does not converge in probability to
\pi_{i_{0}}\pi_{j_{0}}
. Since we have shown that we must have
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{0}}=j_{0}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{j_{0}}
, this implies that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{0}}=i_{0}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{0}}=j_{0})
does not converge in probability to
\pi_{i_{0}}
. Then, by the same argument as above, the property
{\sf(EXG)}
in (
55
) shows that for any two district variables
u\neq v
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\max_{i\in[q]}\bigg\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j_{0})}{\pi_{i}}\bigg\}\Bigg]>1\,,

(61)

since otherwise,
\max_{i\in[q]}\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{0}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{0}}=j_{0})}{\pi_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
holds, which implies that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{0}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{0}}=j_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}
holds for any
i\in[q]
. Now, for
X\in\mathscr{X}
, consider the estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}^{\prime}(X)\equiv\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}^{\prime}(X)\big)_{u\in V}
defined by

\hat{\sigma}_{u}^{\prime}(X):=\argmax_{i\in[q]}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}=X,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j_{0})}{\pi_{i}}\right\}\,.

Then, taking
\Gamma=\id
in the definition of
\widetilde{A}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big)
and using a tower property as in (
60
), we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{A}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big)\Big]\geq\frac{1}{q}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\max_{i\in[q]}\bigg\{\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j_{0})}{\pi_{i}}\bigg\}\Bigg]>\frac{1}{q}\,,

where the last inequality is by (
61
). Therefore, by Lemma
4.4
, weak recovery is possible at
d
, which contradicts our assumption. We thus conclude that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=j\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}
holds for any
u\neq v
and
i,j\in[q]
.
∎

The step
(b)\Rightarrow(c)
is trivial, thus we next prove
(c)\Rightarrow(a)

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
hold as
n\to\infty
. Then, weak recovery is impossible at
d
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big]=q^{-1}+o_{n}(1)
holds for any estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
. For simplicity, denote
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\equiv(\hat{\sigma}_{u})_{u\in V}
. Then, note that we can express

\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big]-\frac{1}{q}=\mathbb{E}\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{1}{n\pi_{i}}\sum_{u\in V}\bigg(\mathds{1}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i,\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\Gamma(i)\big)-\pi_{i}\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\Gamma(i)\big)\bigg)\Bigg\}\,,

Thus, by triangle inequality, we can bound

\begin{split}\left|\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big]-\frac{1}{q}\right|&\leq\big(q\min_{i\leq q}\pi_{i}\big)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{1}{n}\bigg|\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\Gamma(i)\big)\bigg|\Bigg\}\\
&\leq\big(q\min_{i\leq q}\pi_{i}\big)^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\bigg|\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=j\big)\bigg|\,.\end{split}

(62)

By Cauchy-Schwarz, we can bound the right most term by

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=j\big)\bigg|\leq\frac{q^{2}}{n}\Bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=j\big)\bigg)^{2}\Bigg)^{1/2}\,.

(63)

Note that we can expand the sum inside the square root by

\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=j\big)\bigg)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\sum_{u,v\in V}\mathds{1}(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\hat{\sigma}_{v})\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\,.

Since
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\equiv(\hat{\sigma}_{u})_{u\in V}
is
\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}
-measurable, tower property shows that the RHS above equals

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{u,v\in V}\mathds{1}(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\hat{\sigma}_{v})\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{q}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\,\,\bigg|\,\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\bigg]\Bigg]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\sum_{u,v\in V}\mathds{1}(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=\hat{\sigma}_{v})\Big(\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\Big)\,.\end{split}

By dividing the sum over
u,v\in V
into the cases
u=v
and
u\neq v
, and using triangle inequality, we can bound

\begin{split}&\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=j\big)\bigg)^{2}\\
&\leq 2n+n(n-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big|\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-2\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\Big|\,,\end{split}

where we fixed two distinct variables
u_{1}\neq v_{1}
. Plugging the inequality above into (
63
), we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sum_{u\in V}\big(\mathds{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i)-\pi_{i}\big)\cdot\mathds{1}\big(\hat{\sigma}_{u}=j\big)\bigg|\\
&\leq q^{2}\cdot\bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big|\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-2\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\Big|+\frac{2}{n}\bigg)^{1/2}\,.\end{split}

(64)

Since
\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
hold by our assumption, the RHS of (
64
) converges to
0
as
n\to\infty
since the convergent random variables are bounded. This further implies that the RHS of (
62
) converges to
0
. We thus conclude that
\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\big]=q^{-1}+o_{n}(1)
holds, which implies that the weak recovery is impossible at
d
by Lemma
4.4
.
∎

4.2
Triviality of the overlap

Next, we establish
(c)\Leftrightarrow(d)
. The proof of
(c)\Rightarrow(d)
resembles the proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
hold as
n\to\infty
. Then,
\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\to 0
holds as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can bound

\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\leq q\cdot\bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\big)^{2}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\bigg)^{1/2}\,.

(65)

Recalling the definition of
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)
in (
23
), we can expand

\begin{split}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\big)^{2}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}=&\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in V}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\mathds{1}\big(\sigma^{1}_{u}=i,\sigma^{1}_{v}=j\,,\,\sigma^{2}_{u}=i,\sigma^{2}_{v}=j\big)\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\\
&~-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{u\in V}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\mathds{1}\big(\sigma^{1}_{u}=i\,,\,\sigma^{2}_{u}=j\big)\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\pi_{j}^{2}\,.\end{split}

Recalling that
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}
are independent samples from the posterior
\mu_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}
, we can simplify the RHS above by using the property
{\sf(EXG)}
in (
55
) as

\frac{1}{n}+\frac{n-1}{n}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)\Big)^{2}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)\Big)^{2}+\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\Big)^{2}\,,

where
u\neq v
are distinct variables. By our assumption,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
converge in probability to
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
, which further implies
L^{2}
convergence since they are bounded. Therefore, we have

\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\big)^{2}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\to 0
, and combining with (
65
) concludes the proof.
∎

The implication
(d)\Rightarrow(c)
is a consequence of the following lemma, which is a generalization of
[
COKPZ18
, Lemma 4.8]
to non-uniform priors and is built on
[
BCO16
, Corollary 2.2]
.

Lemma 4.8
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
q\geq 2
, there exists
\delta\equiv\delta(\varepsilon,q)>0
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon,q)
such that if
n\geq n_{0}(\varepsilon,q)
, the following holds. Suppose that a probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}([q]^{n})
and a probability vector on
[q]
,
\pi\in\mathbb{R}^{q},\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}=1,\pi_{i}\geq 0
, satisfy

\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\Fr}^{2}\Big\rangle_{\mu}<\delta\,,

(66)

where
\big\langle\cdot\big\rangle_{\mu}
is the expectation with respect to independent samples
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sim\mu
,
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
is defined in (
23
), and
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{\Fr}
denotes the Frobenius norm. Then, we have

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\big\|\mu_{u,v}-\pi^{\otimes 2}\big\|_{\tv}<\varepsilon\,,

where
\mu_{u,v}
denotes the marginal distribution of
(\sigma_{u},\sigma_{v})
when
\underline{\sigma}\equiv(\underline{\sigma}_{u})_{u\in[n]}\sim\mu
.

Proof.

The following is a restatement of
[
BCO16
, Corollary 2.2]
: for all
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, there exists
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon_{0},q)
and
n_{0}=n_{0}(\varepsilon_{0},q)
such that for every
n\geq n_{0}
and
\mu\in\mathscr{P}([q]^{n})
, the following holds. There exists a decomposition
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{N}
of
[q]^{n}
such that it satisfies

•

(C1):

\mu(S_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and for any
1\leq k\leq N
,
\mu(S_{k})\geq\eta
.

•

(C2):
For
1\leq k\leq N
and
u,v\in[n]
, denote by
\mu_{u,v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})
the marginal distribution of
(\sigma_{u},\sigma_{v})
, where
\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{u})_{u\in V}
is drawn from the conditional distribution
\mu(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})\equiv\mu(\cdot\cap S_{k})/\mu(S_{k})
. Similarly, denote by
\mu_{u}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})
the marginal distribution of
\sigma_{u}
, where
\underline{\sigma}\sim\mu(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})
. Then, for any
1\leq k\leq N
,

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\big\|\mu_{u,v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})-\mu_{u}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})\otimes\mu_{v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})\big\|_{\tv}<\varepsilon_{0}\,.

We proceed by taking advantage of such decomposition guaranteed by
[
BCO16
, Corollary 2.2]
. Note that it suffices to prove the lemma for small enough
\varepsilon>0
, so we fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/100q)
. Given such
\varepsilon>0
, we take
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\delta
small enough depending only on
\varepsilon
and
q
whose precise value is determined below. Suppose that for a probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}([q]^{n})
satisfies (
66
). Given
\mu
, consider the decomposition
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{N}
of
[q]^{n}
, which satisfies
{\sf(C1),(C2)}
above, and denote by
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{k}
the average w.r.t.
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mu(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})
. Then, by our assumption (
66
) and since
\mu(S_{k})\geq\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon_{0},q)
holds by
(C1)
, we have for any
1\leq k\leq N
,

\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\Fr}^{2}\Big\rangle_{k}<\frac{\delta}{\eta^{2}}\,.

(67)

Recalling the definition of
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
in (
23
), we can expand the LHS by

\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\Fr}^{2}\Big\rangle_{k}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\Bigg(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\mu_{u,v}(i,i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{u,v}(j,j\,|\,S_{k})-\frac{2\pi_{i}\pi_{j}}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{u}(j\,|\,S_{k})+\pi_{i}^{2}\pi_{j}^{2}\Bigg)\,.

(68)

Note that by
(C2)
, we can replace
\mu_{u,v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})
with
\mu_{u}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})
with error depending on
\varepsilon_{0}
:

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\mu_{u,v}(i,i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{u,v}(j,j\,|\,S_{k})\geq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{v}(i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{u}(j\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{v}(j\,|\,S_{k})-4\varepsilon_{0}\,,

thus plugging the above bound into (
68
) and combining with (
67
) gives

\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{v}(j\,|\,S_{k})-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\bigg)^{2}<\frac{\delta}{\eta^{2}}+4\varepsilon_{0}\,.

Thus, by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have that

\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})\mu_{v}(j\,|\,S_{k})-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\bigg|\leq q^{2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\delta}{\eta^{2}}+4\varepsilon_{0}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon^{3}\,,

(69)

where in the last inequality we took
\varepsilon_{0}\equiv\frac{\varepsilon^{6}}{8q^{4}}
and
\delta\equiv\frac{\eta^{2}\varepsilon^{6}}{2q^{4}}
for
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon_{0},q)
. Observe that restricting the sum over
i,j
in (
69
) to
i=j
, and pushing the sum over
j
inside the absolute value by triangle inequality shows that

\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})^{2}-\pi_{i}^{2}\bigg|\bigg)\vee\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})-\pi_{i}\bigg|\bigg)\leq\varepsilon^{3}\,.

Thus, by triangle inequality,

\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\big(\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})-\pi_{i}\big)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})^{2}-\pi_{i}^{2}\bigg)-2\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})-\pi_{i}\bigg)\leq 3\varepsilon^{3}\,.

Then, it follows from Cauchy-Schwarz that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{u\in[n]}\Big|\mu_{u}(i\,|\,S_{k})-\pi_{i}\Big|\leq(3q\varepsilon^{3})^{1/2}<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\,,

(70)

where the last inequality holds since
\varepsilon<(100q)^{-1}
. Consequently, combining with
(C2)
, we have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\big\|\mu_{u,v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\tv}&\leq\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\big\|\mu_{u}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})\otimes\mu_{v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\tv}\\
&\leq\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{u\in[n]}\big\|\mu_{u}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})-\pi\big\|_{\tv}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\,,\end{split}

(71)

where the last inequality is due to (
70
). Therefore, by triangle inequality, we have that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\big\|\mu_{u,v}-\pi^{\otimes 2}\big\|_{\tv}&\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in[n]}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\big\|\mu_{u,v}(\cdot\,|\,S_{k})-\pi^{\otimes 2}\big\|_{\tv}\cdot\mu(S_{k})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mu(S_{0})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mu(S_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{<}}\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<\varepsilon\,,\end{split}

where
(a)
is due to (
71
) and
(b)
is due to
(C1)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

With Lemma
4.8
in hand, the step
(d)\Rightarrow(c)
is straightforward.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
