\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
hold as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

By Markov’s inequality,
\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\big\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
under
\mathbb{E}\mu_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}^{\otimes 2}
for any
i,j\in[q]
. Since
\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(i,j)-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\big\|\leq 1
, convergence in probability further implies
L^{2}
convergence. Summing over
i,j\in[q]
, we have
\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\Fr}^{2}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\to 0
. Hence, Lemma
4.8
further implies that

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in V}\Big\|\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\cdot\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=\cdot\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)-\pi^{\otimes 2}\Big\|_{\tv}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0\,.

Note that the LHS above is at most
1
, thus above further implies
L^{1}
convergence. Moreover,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in V}\mathbb{E}\Big\|\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\cdot\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=\cdot\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)-\pi^{\otimes 2}\Big\|_{\tv}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=j\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)-\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\Big|+O(n^{-1})\,,\end{split}

(72)

where we used the property
{\sf(EXG)}
in the equality above for distinct variables
u_{1}\neq v_{1}
. Therefore, we have that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}
.
∎

Finally, we prove a non-asymptotic version of the implication
(d)\Rightarrow(a)
, which is used for the proof of Proposition
3.21
(see Lemma
6.4
).

Lemma 4.10
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon,q,\pi)>0
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
such that the following holds. Suppose that
n\geq n_{0}
and there exists an estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds. Then, we have
\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\geq\eta
.

Proof.

Throughout, we write
C\equiv C(q,\pi)>0
for a positive constant that only depends on
q,\pi
that may differ from line to line. Also, we fix
\varepsilon>0
and write
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
for an integer that only depends on
\varepsilon,q,\pi
. Suppose that
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})
. By Lemma
4.4
, we have
\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{A}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
for
n\geq n_{0}
. Subsequently, by the inequalities (
62
) and (
64
) in the proof of Lemma
4.6
, it follows that for
n\geq n_{0}

C\varepsilon^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\Big|\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-2\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}^{2}\Big|\,,

where
u_{1}\neq v_{1}
are two distinct variables. By triangle inequality, the RHS is upper bounded by

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{q}\big(\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-\pi_{i}^{2}\big)-2\sum_{i=1}^{q}\pi_{i}\big(\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=i\,|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star})-\pi_{i}\big)\Big|\\
&\leq 3\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=\cdot\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=\cdot\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)-\pi^{\otimes 2}\big\|_{\tv}\,.\end{split}

Thus, combining the two inequalities above,
\mathbb{E}\big\|\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u_{1}}=\cdot\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}}=\cdot\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)-\pi^{\otimes 2}\big\|_{\tv}\geq C\varepsilon^{2}
holds for
n\geq n_{0}
. Recalling the equality (
72
) and using that the total variation distance is at most
1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in V}\big\|\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{u}=\cdot\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v}=\cdot\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}\big)-\pi^{\otimes 2}\big\|_{\tv}\geq C\varepsilon^{2}\bigg)\geq C\varepsilon^{2}\,.

Then by Lemma
4.8
, there exists a constant
\delta\equiv\delta(\varepsilon,q,\pi)>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\Fr}^{2}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{X}^{\star}}\geq\delta\bigg)\geq C\varepsilon^{2}\,.

Finally, note that
\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\Fr}^{2}\leq\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}
holds since the entries of
R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
have absolute values at most
1
. Therefore, setting
\eta\equiv C\delta\varepsilon^{2}
concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

This is immediate from combining Lemmas
4.5
,
4.6
,
4.7
, and
4.9
.
∎

5
Contiguity below the weak recovery threshold

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.2
, Proposition
3.5
and Lemma
3.11
, which were crucial for the proof of Theorem
2.5
. In Section
5.3
, we prove Proposition
3.5
by appealing to a central limit theorem. In Section
5.1
, we prove Lemmas
3.2
and
3.11
.

5.1
Proof of Lemma
3.2
and Lemma
3.11

Throughout, we assume
{\sf(SYM)}
. We start with the first statement of Lemma
3.2
.

Lemma 5.1
.

The linear operator
\Xi
is self-adjoint on the inner product space
(\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi})
.

Proof.

By linearity, it suffices to check
\big\langle\Xi(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}),e_{s}\otimes e_{t}\big\rangle_{\pi}=\big\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{j},\Xi(e_{s}\otimes e_{t})\big\rangle_{\pi}
for
i,j,s,t\in[q]
, where
(e_{i})_{i\in[q]}
are standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Note that by definition of
\Xi
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi}
, we have

\begin{split}\big\langle\Xi(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}),e_{s}\otimes e_{t}\big\rangle_{\pi}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}p(\psi)\cdot\big\langle\Phi_{\psi}e_{i}\otimes\Phi_{\psi}e_{j},e_{s}\otimes e_{t}\big\rangle_{\pi}=\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[e_{i}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)e_{s}\cdot e_{j}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)e_{t}\Big]\,.\end{split}

Recalling the condition (
7
) and the definition of
\Phi_{\psi}
in (
33
), note that we have

\diag(\pi)\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)\quad\textnormal{for}\quad\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p\,.

(73)

Thus, it follows that
\big\langle\Xi(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}),e_{s}\otimes e_{t}\big\rangle_{\pi}=\big\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{j},\Xi(e_{s}\otimes e_{t})\big\rangle_{\pi}
holds for
i,j,s,t\in[q]
.
∎

Consider the matrix
\Phi\equiv\Phi_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
defined by the average of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
.

\Phi\equiv\Phi_{p}:=\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big]\,.

As before, we often identify the matrix
\Phi
with the linear map
x\to\Phi x
. Further, consider the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{q}
defined by
\langle x,y\rangle_{\pi,1}:=x^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)y
. The notation
1
is to distinguish
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1}
from the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi}
in (
34
), which is the tensor product of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1}
with itself. Then, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

The linear operator
\Phi
is self-adjoint on the inner product space
(\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1})
such that
\Phi\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
. Further, for
w\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we have that
\Xi(\mathbf{1}\otimes w)=\mathbf{1}\otimes\Phi w
.

Proof.

Note that since
\diag(\pi)\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)
holds for
\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
(cf. (
73
)),
\diag(\pi)\Phi=\Phi\diag(\pi)
holds. Thus, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we have that

\big\langle\Phi x,y\big\rangle_{\pi,1}=x^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Phi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)y=x^{{\mathsf{T}}}\diag(\pi)\Phi y=\big\langle x,\Phi y\big\rangle_{\pi,1}\,.

Further, note that by
{\sf(SYM)}
, for any
\psi\in\Psi
, we have the equality
\Phi_{\psi}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
. Thus,
\Phi\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
holds and we have for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
that

\Xi(\mathbf{1}\otimes w)=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}p(\psi)\cdot\Phi_{\psi}\mathbf{1}\otimes\Phi_{\psi}w=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}p(\psi)\cdot\mathbf{1}\otimes\Phi_{\psi}w=\mathbf{1}\otimes\Phi w\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Observe that by Lemma
5.2
, there exists an orthonormal basis
\{w_{1}=1,w_{2},\ldots,w_{q}\}
of the inner product space
(\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1})
such that
w_{i}
’s are eigenvectors of
\Phi
. We denote by
\lambda_{i}
the eigenvalue corresponding to
w_{i}
, where
\lambda_{1}=1
. Then, we have the following lemma. Below,
\proj_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}
and
\proj_{w_{i}\otimes w_{j}}
for
i,j\in[q]
respectively denotes the projection operator in
(\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi})
onto the subspace
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
and the subspace spanned by the vector
w_{i}\otimes w_{j}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

The subspaces
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
is invariant under
\Xi
. Further, we have that

\Xi\circ\proj_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}=\proj_{\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}}+\sum_{i=2}^{q}\lambda_{i}\proj_{\mathbf{1}\otimes w_{i}}+\sum_{i=2}^{q}\lambda_{i}\proj_{w_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}}\,.

Thus,
\Xi\rvert_{S^{\perp}}
has eigenvalues
\{1,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{q},\lambda_{q}\}
counting multiplicities.

Proof.

Since
(w_{i})_{i\leq q}
are eigenvectors of
\Phi
, Lemma
5.2
implies that
1\otimes w_{i}
and
w_{i}\otimes 1
are eignevectors of
\Xi
with
\lambda_{i}
the corresponding eigenvalue. Meanwhile, since
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
is finite dimensional,
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
is given by

\mathcal{S}^{\perp}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}:x=w\otimes\mathbf{1}~\textnormal{or}~x=\mathbf{1}\otimes w~\textnormal{for some}~w\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\}\,.

Thus, the set
\mathcal{W}:=\{\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}\otimes w_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{1}\otimes w_{q},w_{2}\otimes\mathbf{1},\ldots,w_{q}\otimes\mathbf{1}\}
is an orthonormal basis of the inner product space
(\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi})
, which are also eigenvectors of
\Xi
. Hence, the subspace of
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
is invariant under
\Xi
, which further implies that
\mathcal{S}
is invariant since
\Xi
is self-adjoint by Lemma
5.1
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{W}
is the eigenbasis of
\Xi\rvert_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}
, the rest of the claims follow by the spectral theorem.
∎

Having Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.3
in hand, we now prove Lemma
3.2
and Lemma
3.11
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

The first and second statements follow immediately from Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
5.3
, thus it remains to prove the third statement. Note that applying the spectral theorem on the self-adjoint operator
\Phi
on
(\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1})
(cf. Lemma
5.2
), we have

\Phi=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\lambda_{i}\proj_{w_{i}}=\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}+\sum_{i=2}^{q}\lambda_{i}\proj_{w_{i}}\,,

where
\proj_{w_{i}}
denotes the projection operator in
(\mathbb{R}^{q},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi,1})
onto the subspace spanned by the vector
w_{i}
. In particular, the last equality follows since
\proj_{\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
. Thus, combining with Lemma
5.3
, we have

\Xi\circ\proj_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}=\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\otimes\Phi+\Phi\otimes\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)-\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\otimes\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\,.

Since
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
are invariant subspaces of
\Xi
by Lemma
5.3
,
\Xi=\Xi\circ\proj_{\mathcal{S}}+\Xi\circ\proj_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}
holds by the spectral theorem. Thus, it follows that

\Xi\circ\proj_{\mathcal{S}}=\Xi-\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\otimes\Phi-\Phi\otimes\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)+\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\otimes\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)=\mathbb{E}_{p}\bigg[\Big(\Phi-\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Big)^{\otimes 2}\bigg]\equiv\Xi_{\ast}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

For each
\ell\geq 1
, we have by definition,

\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}=\sum_{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\in\Psi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell},t_{\ell}\in[k]\\
s_{i}\neq t_{i},1\leq i\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2\ell}\left(\frac{d}{k}\right)^{\ell}\bigg(\tr\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\psi_{i},s_{i},t_{i}}\Big)-1\bigg)^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p(\psi_{i})\,.

Note that given
s,t\in[k]
with
s\neq t
, there exists a permutation
\theta
in
[k]
such that
\theta(1)=s,\theta(2)=t
, and for such
\theta
, we have
\Phi_{\psi,s,t}=\Phi_{\psi^{\theta}}
, where
\psi^{\theta}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k})\equiv\psi(\sigma_{\theta(1)},\ldots,\sigma_{\theta(k)})
. Thus, by our assumption that
p(\psi^{\theta})=p(\psi)
in (
7
), we can express the equation above by

\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}=\sum_{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\in\Psi}\frac{\big((k-1)d\big)^{\ell}}{2\ell}\bigg(\tr\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\psi_{i}}\Big)-1\bigg)^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p(\psi_{i})=\frac{\big((k-1)d\big)^{\ell}}{2\ell}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\tr\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}}\Big)-1\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]\,,

where
(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i})_{i\leq\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}p
. Note that we can expand the rightmost term by

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\tr\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}}\Big)-1\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\tr\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Big(\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}}\otimes\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}}\Big)\bigg)\bigg]-2\mathbb{E}\bigg[\tr\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}}\Big)\bigg]+1=\tr\big(\Xi^{\ell}\big)-2\tr\big(\Phi^{\ell}\big)+1\,,

where the last equality holds since the expectation and trace is exchangeable and
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}
’s are independent. Since
\Xi
and
\Phi
are self-adjoint by Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
, we have

\tr\big(\Xi^{\ell}\big)-2\tr\big(\Phi^{\ell}\big)+1=\sum_{\lambda\in\Eig(\Xi)}\lambda^{\ell}-\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Eig(\Phi)}(\lambda^{\prime})^{\ell}+1=\sum_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)}\lambda^{\ell}\,,

where the last equality follows from Lemma
5.3
. Thus, combining the
3
equations in the displays above and summing over
\ell\geq 1
, we have

\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)}\frac{\big((k-1)d\big)^{\ell}}{2\ell}\lambda^{\ell}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)}\frac{1}{2}\log\big(1-(k-1)d\lambda\big)\,,

where the last equality holds for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
since
\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{x^{\ell}}{\ell}=\log(1-x)
holds for for
|x|<1
. Therefore, exponentiating the equation above concludes the proof.
∎

5.2
Proof of Lemma
3.4

Fix
d<d_{\ast}
. We first claim that for any
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
\big|m_{n}-dn/k\big|\leq n^{2/3}
, weak recovery is impossible for
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
. We refer to Definition
4.1
for a general definition of weak recovery. To this end, fix such
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
\eta>0
such that
d<d+\eta<d_{\ast}
holds. Then, weak recovery is impossible at
d+\eta
by definition of
d_{\ast}
. That is, for any estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})\big)\in[q]^{V}
, where
\boldsymbol{m}\sim{\sf Poi}\big((d+\eta)n/k\big)
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})]=\frac{1}{q}\,.

(74)

Note that by definition of the planted model (cf. Definition
2.2
), conditional on the event
\boldsymbol{m}\geq m_{n}
, the subgraph of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
formed by excluding
\boldsymbol{m}-m_{n}
clauses is distributed the same as
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
. Moreover, since
(d+\eta)n/k\geq m_{n}+\Omega(n)
, it follows from Chernoff bound that
\boldsymbol{m}>m_{n}
holds with high probability. As a consequence, for any estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})\big)
of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
, (
74
) must also hold. Therefore, for any
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
\big|m_{n}-dn/k\big|\leq n^{2/3}
, weak recovery is impossible for
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})
.

Note that since
m_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, Lemma
4.2
implies that
\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})\big)_{n\geq 1}
satisfies the properties
(EXG)
. Thus, it follows from Proposition
4.3
that as
n\to\infty
,

\delta_{n}:=\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})}\to 0\,.

Let
\varepsilon_{n}:=\delta_{n}^{1/2}
and consider
L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))\equiv L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n});\varepsilon_{n})
. Then, we have by a change of measure (cf. (
38
)) that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|L\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)-L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n}))\Big|\bigg]&=\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})}>\varepsilon_{n}\Big)\\
&\leq\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m_{n})}=\delta_{n}^{1/2}\,,\end{split}

where the second equality is due to the change of measure and the inequality is by Markov’s inequality. Since
\delta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, this concludes the proof.

5.3
Proof of Proposition
3.5

Throughout this subsection, we fix
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and assume the condition
{\sf(SYM)}
. For any factor graph
G
with
n
variables and
m
clauses, note that we can express the square of the truncated likelihood ratio
L^{\ast}(G)^{2}
as

\begin{split}&L^{\ast}(G)^{2}=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}}L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(G)\,,\quad\textnormal{where}\\
&L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(G):=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma},\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\tau},\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G\big)^{2}}\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\varepsilon_{n}\Big\}\,.\end{split}

(75)

We divide the sum above into the
near-independent
regime where
\underline{\sigma},\tau\in[q]^{V}
satisfies
\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
, and
correlated
regime where
\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}>\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
. Namely, we have
L^{\ast}(G)^{2}=L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}(G)+L^{2}_{{\sf cor}}(G)
, where

\begin{split}L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}(G)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}:\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}}L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(G)\,,\quad\quad L^{2}_{{\sf cor}}(G)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}:\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}>\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}}L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(G)\,.\end{split}

(76)

An important consequence of the truncation
\mathds{1}\big\{\big\langle\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\varepsilon_{n}\big\}
in
L^{\ast}(G)
is that the contribution to its second moment from the correlated regime is negligible compared to the contribution from the near-independence regime as seen by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any factor graph
G
,
L^{2}_{{\sf cor}}(G)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\cdot\big(L^{\ast}(G)\big)^{2}
holds.

Proof.

Recalling the posterior
\mu_{G}(\underline{\sigma})
(cf. (
21
)), note that
L_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(G)=\big(L^{\ast}(G)\big)^{2}\mu_{G}(\underline{\sigma})\mu_{G}(\underline{\tau})
holds. Thus, it follows that

L^{2}_{{\sf cor}}(G)=\big(L^{\ast}(G)\big)^{2}\cdot\bigg\langle\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}>\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\Big\}\bigg\rangle_{G}\leq\varepsilon_{n}^{-1/2}\big(L^{\ast}(G)\big)^{2}\Big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\Big\rangle_{G}\,.

where the inequality is due to Markov’s inequality. Since
L^{\ast}(G)=0
holds if
\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|\big\rangle_{G}>\varepsilon_{n}
, the RHS can be further bounded by
L^{2}_{{\sf cor}}(G)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\big(L^{\ast}(G)\big)^{2}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Having Lemma
5.4
in hand, we now compute
\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
. The first step is to compute
\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)\big)
.

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
and
n,m\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)\big)\leq\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]}\right)^{m}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\tau})\,.

Proof.

Recall that by definition of the planted model (cf. Definition
2.2
),
\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)=G)}=\frac{\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})]}
holds for a factor graph
G
with
n
variables and
m
clauses. Thus, by dropping the indicator in the definition of
L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(G)
in (
75
), we have

\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)\big)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\tau)\big]}{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\tau)\big]}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\tau})\,.

Meanwhile, for the null model
\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)
, its weight functions
(\psi_{a})_{a\in F}
and the neighborhoods
(\delta a)_{a\in F}
are drawn i.i.d. from
p
and
u:={\sf Unif}(V^{k})
respectively. Thus, we have

\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\tau)\big]}{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\tau)\big]}=\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]}\right)^{m}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma
5.5
shows that in order to bound
\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
, it suffices to compute the expected value of
\Big(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}]}\Big)^{m}
under
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\pi^{\otimes V}
conditioned on the event
\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
, which happens w.h.p. since we assumed
n^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\to\infty
. As we will see next,
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]
and
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]
can be computed in terms of the normalized overlap matrix
X\equiv X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\equiv\big(X(i,j)\big)_{i,j\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
, where

X\equiv X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}:=\sqrt{n}\left(R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\right)\,.

(77)

Note that such normalization guarantees that
\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}X(i,j)=0
and
X
has
O(1)
fluctuations: by viewing
X
as a
q^{2}
dimensional vector and denoting
\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\equiv X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}
for
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\sim\pi^{\otimes n}
, the central limit theorem shows that

\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{N}\big(0,\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}-(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})^{\otimes 2}\big)\,.

(78)

Here, for two matrices
A=(A_{i,j})_{i,j\leq q}
and
B=(B_{i,j})_{i,j\leq q}
, we recall that their tensor product
A\otimes B
is defined by the
q^{2}\times q^{2}
matrix with
\big((i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})\big)
entry
(A\otimes B)_{(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})}\equiv A_{i_{1},i_{2}}B_{j_{1},j_{2}}
.

In addition, for
\psi\in\Psi
, we define the matrix
\widehat{\Phi}_{\psi}\equiv\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{\psi}(i,j)\big)_{i,j\leq q}
by

\widehat{\Phi}_{\psi}(i,j):=\xi^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}=i,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2}=j\big]\,.

Here, we note that
\widehat{\Phi}_{\psi}\diag(\pi)=\Phi_{\psi}
holds by definition. We then define the the matrix
\widehat{\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
by
\widehat{\Phi}:=\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\widehat{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big]
and define the matrix
\widehat{\Xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}\times q^{2}}
by the averaged tensor product of the matrix
\widehat{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
with itself:

\widehat{\Xi}:=\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\widehat{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\otimes\widehat{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\Big]\,.

Note that viewing
X
as a
q^{2}
dimensional vector, quantities such as
\big\langle\widehat{\Xi}X,X\big\rangle
is well-defined, where

\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}X,X\Big\rangle\equiv\sum_{(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})\in[q]^{2}}\widehat{\Xi}\big((i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})\big)X(i_{1},j_{1})X(i_{2},j_{2})\,.

We also recall that
\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
denoted all
1
-vector.

Lemma 5.6
.

For
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
, we have for
X\equiv X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}
that

\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\xi^{2}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{k(k-1)}{2n}\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}X\,,\,X\Big\rangle+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}}{n^{3/2}}\bigg)\bigg)\,,

(79)

where we used the notation
f=O_{k,q,\Psi}\Big(\frac{\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}}{n^{3/2}}\Big)
to indicate that there exists a constant
C\equiv C_{k,q,\Psi}
which only depends on
k,q,\Psi
such that
|f|\leq C\frac{\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}}{n^{3/2}}
holds. Further, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\xi\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{k(k-1)}{2n}\Big\langle\big(\widehat{\Phi}\otimes\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)X\,,\,X\Big\rangle+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}}{n^{3/2}}\bigg)\bigg)\,,\\
&\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\xi\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{k(k-1)}{2n}\Big\langle\big(\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\widehat{\Phi}\big)X\,,\,X\Big\rangle+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}}{n^{3/2}}\bigg)\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(80)

Proof.

We start with the proof of (
79
). By first calculating the expecation w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim u:={\sf Unif}(V^{k})
, we can express
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]
by summing the following over
\underline{i}\equiv(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}),\underline{j}\equiv(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})\in[q]^{k}
:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]&=\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{j}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{i})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{j})\big]\prod_{s=1}^{k}\Big(\pi_{i_{s}}\pi_{j_{s}}+n^{-1/2}X(i_{s},j_{s})\Big)\\
&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{S\subseteq[k]:|S|=\ell}n^{-\ell/2}\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{j}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{i})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{j})\big]\prod_{t\in[k]\setminus S}\pi_{i_{t}}\pi_{j_{t}}\prod_{s\in S}X(i_{s},j_{s})\,.\end{split}

Given
\ell
and
S
with
|S|=\ell
, we divide the sum
\underline{i}\in[q]^{k}
into the sum over
\underline{i}_{1}\equiv(i_{s})_{s\in S}\in[q]^{S}
and
\underline{i}_{2}\equiv(i_{t})_{t\notin S}\in[q]^{[k]\setminus S}
, and similarly for the sum
\underline{j}\in[q]^{k}
. Note that given
\underline{i}_{1},\underline{j}_{1}\in[q]^{S}
, we have

\sum_{\underline{i}_{2},\underline{j}_{2}\in[q]^{[k]\setminus S}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{i})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{j})\big]\prod_{t\in[k]\setminus S}\pi_{i_{t}}\pi_{j_{t}}=\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{S}=\underline{i}_{1}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{S}=\underline{j}_{1}\big]\Big]\,,

where
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}
is taken w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}\sim\pi^{\otimes k}
conditional on
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{S}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s})_{s\in S}
. By our assumption that
\boldsymbol{\psi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\theta}
for
\psi\sim p
and any permutation
\theta
in (
7
), the RHS above does not depend
S
given
\underline{i}_{1},\underline{j}_{1}
. Thus, by fixing
S=[\ell]
, we can express
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]
by

\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}n^{-\ell/2}\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{j}\in[q]^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{[\ell]}=\underline{i}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{[\ell]}=\underline{j}\big]\Big]\cdot\prod_{s=1}^{\ell}X(i_{s},j_{s})\,.

(81)

We now divide the summand above into the cases
\ell\in\{0,1,2\}
and
\ell\geq 3
. For
\ell=0
, by
{\sf(SYM)}
,

\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\Big)^{2}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}p(\psi)\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\Big)^{2}=\xi^{2}\,.

(82)

For
\ell=1
, again by
{\sf(SYM)}
, we have

kn^{-1/2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}X(i,j)\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{1}=i\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{1}=j\big]\Big]=kn^{-1/2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}X(i,j)\xi^{2}=0\,,

(83)

where the last equality follows since
\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}X(i,j)=0
. For
\ell=2
, we have by definition of
\widehat{\Xi}
that

\binom{k}{2}n^{-1}\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{j}\in[q]^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{\{1,2\}}=\underline{i}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{\{1,2\}}=\underline{j}\big]\Big]\cdot\prod_{s=1}^{2}X(i_{s},j_{s})=\binom{k}{2}\frac{\xi^{2}}{n}\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}X\,,\,X\Big\rangle\,.

(84)

Finally, for
\ell\geq 3
, we can crudely bound

\begin{split}&\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\binom{k}{\ell}n^{-\ell/2}\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{j}\in[q]^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{[\ell]}=\underline{i}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{[\ell]}=\underline{j}\big]\Big]\cdot\prod_{s=1}^{\ell}X(i_{s},j_{s})\\
&\leq\max_{\psi\in\Psi}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\binom{k}{\ell}n^{-\ell/2}q^{2\ell}\cdot\|X\|_{\infty}^{\ell}\leq\max_{\psi\in\Psi}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot(1+q)^{2k}\cdot n^{-3/2}\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}\,,\end{split}

(85)

where the last inequality follows since
\|X\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{n}
by definition of
X
in (
77
). Therefore, plugging in the equalities (
82
)-(
84
) and the bound (
85
) into (
81
), we have

\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\xi^{2}\cdot\bigg(1+\frac{k(k-1)}{2n}\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}X\,,\,X\Big\rangle+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}}{n^{3/2}}\bigg)\bigg)\,.

(86)

Note that since
\psi(\cdot)>0
for any
\psi\in\Psi
, there exists a constant
c_{i}\equiv c_{i,\Psi}
for
i=1,2
, such that
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]\in[c_{1}\xi^{2},c_{2}\xi^{2}]
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C\equiv C_{c_{1},c_{2}}>0
such that for
1+x\in[c_{1},c_{2}]
,
e^{x-Cx^{2}}\leq 1+x\leq e^{x}
holds. Applying this inequality for
x=k(k-1)\big\langle\widehat{X},X\big\rangle/n+O_{k,q,\Psi}\big(\|X|\|_{\infty}^{3}/n^{3/2}\big)
and using the fact that
\|X\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{n}
, we obtain the estimate (
79
).

Next, we prove (
80
) by a similar argument. Denote by
X(i,\cdot):=\sum_{j=1}^{q}X(i,j)
. Then, proceeding in the same manner as in (
81
), we can express
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]
by

\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}n^{-\ell/2}\sum_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{[\ell]}=\underline{i}\big]\Big]\prod_{s=1}^{\ell}X(i_{s},\cdot)=:\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}G(\ell)\,.

(87)

Then, by the same calculations done in (
82
) and (
83
),
G(0)=\xi
and
G(1)=0
hold by
{\sf(SYM)}
. For
\ell=2
, we have

\begin{split}G(2)&\equiv\binom{k}{2}n^{-1}\sum_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{\{1,2\}}=\underline{i}\big]\Big]\prod_{s=1}^{2}X(i_{s},\cdot)\\
&=\binom{k}{2}\frac{\xi}{n}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in[q]}\Phi(i_{1},i_{2})\prod_{s=1}^{2}X(i_{s},j_{s})=\binom{k}{2}\frac{\xi}{n}\Big\langle\big(\widehat{\Phi}\otimes\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)X\,,\,X\Big\rangle\,.\end{split}

For
\ell\geq 3
, proceeding in the same manner as done in (
85
), we can bound

\sum_{\ell\geq 3}G(3)\equiv\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\binom{k}{\ell}n^{-\ell/2}\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{j}\in[q]^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{[\ell]}=\underline{i}\big]\Big]\prod_{s=1}^{\ell}X(i_{s},j_{s})\leq\max_{\psi\in\Psi}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot(1+q)^{2k}\cdot n^{-3/2}\|X\|_{\infty}^{3}

By plugging in the obtained bounds into (
87
), and using the inequality
e^{x-O(x^{2})}\leq 1+x\leq e^{x}
for
1+x
bounded away from
0
and
\infty
as before, we obtain the first inequality of (
80
). The second inequality of (
80
) then follows from the first by exchanging the role of
\underline{\sigma}
and
\underline{\tau}
.
∎

Define the matrix
\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}\times q^{2}}
by

\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}=\widehat{\Xi}-\widehat{\Phi}\otimes\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\widehat{\Phi}+\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\,.

We note that the matrix
\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}
is related with the linear operator
\Xi_{\ast}
on
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
as follows. By identifying the vector spaces
\mathbb{R}^{q}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}}
by the unique isomorphism that maps
e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\to e_{(i,j)}
for
i,j\in[q]
, where
(e_{i})_{i\leq q}
is the standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
(e_{(i,j)})_{i,j\leq q}
is the standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}}
,
\Xi
can be identified with the
q^{2}\times q^{2}
matrix
\Xi\big((i,j),(s,t)\big)_{i,j,s,t\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}\times q^{2}}
, where

\Xi\big((i,j),(s,t)\big)\equiv\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(i,s)\cdot\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(j,t)\Big]\,.

Similarly,
\Xi_{\ast}
can be identified with a
q^{2}\times q^{2}
matrix. With such identification, we have that
\Xi=\widehat{\Xi}\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}
, thus

\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}=\Xi-\Phi\otimes\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}-\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\Phi+\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\otimes\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)=\Xi_{\ast}.

(88)

As a consequence of Lemmas
5.4
,
5.5
, and
5.6
, we have the following proposition. We recall the random variable
\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\equiv X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}\cong\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}}
, where
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\sim\pi^{\otimes n}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

There exists a constant
C\equiv C_{k,q,\Psi}>0
which only depends on
k,q,\Psi
such that the following holds. For
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and any
(m_{n})_{n\geq 1},(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
|m_{n}-dn/k|\leq n^{2/3}
and
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
,
n^{2/3}\varepsilon_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, we have for large enough
n
that

\mathbb{E}\Big(L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\Big)^{2}\leq\big(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\big)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\bigg(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\Big(\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\,,\,\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\Big\rangle+C\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)\bigg)\mathds{1}\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}\Big\}\Bigg]

Proof.

Recall the decomposition
L^{\star}(G)^{2}=L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}(G)+L^{2}_{{\sf cor}}(G)
in (
76
). By Lemma
5.4
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Big(L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\Big)^{2}\leq(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}})^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\,.

(89)

Meanwhile, by Lemma
5.5
,
\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)
can be upper bounded by

\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}:\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]}\right)^{m_{n}}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\tau})\,.

Observe that for
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
, the restriction
\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
is equivalent to
\|X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}
. In particular,

\|X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\|_{\infty}^{3}/n^{3/2}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|X_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}\|_{\infty}
holds under such restriction. Thus, for
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
such that
\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
, we have by Lemma
5.6
that

\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\right)^{m_{n}}\leq\exp\Bigg(\frac{m_{n}}{n}\binom{k}{2}\bigg(\bigg\langle\Big(\widehat{\Xi}-\widehat{\Phi}\otimes\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\widehat{\Phi}\Big)X\,,\,X\bigg\rangle+C\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|X\|_{\infty}^{2}\bigg)\Bigg),

where
C\equiv C_{k,q,\Psi}>0
denotes a constant that only depend on
k,q,
and the set
\Psi
. Note that since
\big\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{{\mathsf{T}}},X\big\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}X(i,j)=0
, the inner product above equals
\big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}X,X\big\rangle
. Moreover, since
m_{n}\leq dn/k+n^{3/2}
and
n^{-1/3}\ll\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
holds, the factor
\frac{m_{n}}{n}\binom{k}{2}
in the RHS above can be replaced by
\frac{(k-1)d}{2}
for large enough
n
with the modification of the constant
C
. Therefore,

\mathbb{E}L^{2}_{{\sf ind}}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\bigg(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\Big(\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\,,\,\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\Big\rangle+C^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)\bigg)\mathds{1}\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}\Big\}\Bigg]\,,

for some constant
C^{\prime}\equiv C^{\prime}_{k,q,\Psi}
. Combining this with (
89
) concludes the proof.
∎

Having Proposition
5.7
in hand, the final ingredient to prove Proposition
3.5
is the uniform integrability of
(\boldsymbol{Z}_{n})_{n\geq 1}\equiv\big(\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}(d,k,\underline{\varepsilon},C)\big)_{n\geq 1}
, where
\underline{\varepsilon}\equiv(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and

\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}:=\exp\bigg(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\Big(\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\,,\,\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\Big\rangle+C\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)\bigg)\mathds{1}\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}\Big\}\,.

Here, we emphasize that the indicator
\mathds{1}\big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}\big\}
due to the truncation
\mathds{1}\big\{\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|\big\rangle_{G}\leq\varepsilon_{n}\big\}
in
L_{\ast}(G)
is crucial for uniform integrability stated below.

Lemma 5.8
.

For
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and
\underline{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, where
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, the random variables
(\boldsymbol{Z}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are uniformly integrable.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathscr{P}_{n}([q]^{2})
the set of
R\equiv\big(R(i,j)\big)_{i,j\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
such that
R(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}/n
for any
i,j\leq q
and
\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}R(i,j)=1
. Further, denote by
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}\equiv R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}
the overlap matrix under
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\pi^{\otimes n}
. Then, by Sanov’s theorem (a.k.a. Stirling’s approximation), we have for any
R\in\mathscr{P}_{n}([q]^{2})
that

\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{R}_{n}=R\big)=n^{O_{q}(1)}\exp\big(-n\cdot D_{\textup{KL}}(R\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})\big)\,,

(90)

where
O_{q}(1)
denotes a bounded constant that only depends on
q
and
D_{\textup{KL}}(\mu\,\|\,\nu):=\sum_{i,j\in[q]}\mu(i,j)\log\big(\frac{\nu(i,j)}{\mu(i,j)}\big)
for
\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{P}([q]^{2})
is the KL divergence (a.k.a. relative entropy) between
\mu
and
\nu
. By viewing
R\in\mathscr{P}_{n}([q]^{2})
as a
q^{2}
dimensional vector, we first claim that for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
,

\nabla^{2}_{R}D_{\textup{KL}}(R\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})\Big\rvert_{R=\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}}\succ(k-1)d\cdot\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\,,

(91)

where
\nabla^{2}_{R}
denotes the Hessian with respect to
R\in\mathbb{R}^{q^{2}}
. Indeed, note that a direct computation gives
\nabla^{2}_{R}D_{\textup{KL}}(R\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})\big\rvert_{R=\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}}=\diag\big((\pi_{i}^{-1})_{i\leq q}\big)^{\otimes 2}
, thus (
91
) is equivalent to
I_{q^{2}\times q^{2}}\succ(k-1)d\cdot\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}
, which follows for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
by Lemma
3.2
since the maximum eigenvalue of
\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}
equals
\max_{\lambda\in\Eig(\Xi_{\ast})}|\lambda|
by (
88
). Hence, (
91
) holds for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
.

Now, observe that since
R\to D_{\textup{KL}}(R\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})
is convex with
D_{\textup{KL}}(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})=0
, (
91
) implies that there exists small enough
\varepsilon,\delta>0
such that if
\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon
, then

D_{\textup{KL}}(R\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})\geq(1+\delta)\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}(R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})\,,\,R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Big\rangle+\delta\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{2}^{2}\,.

(92)

For such
\delta>0
, we now claim that
\big(\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}^{1+\delta}\big)_{n\geq 1}
is bounded, which is sufficient for uniform integrability. Note that
\boldsymbol{X}_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\boldsymbol{R}_{n}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})
holds by definition. Thus, by (
90
), we can bound
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}^{1+\delta}
by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{P}_{n}([q]^{2})\\
\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\end{subarray}}\exp\bigg(\frac{(1+\delta)(k-1)dn}{2}\Big(\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}(R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})\,,\,R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Big\rangle+C\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)-nD_{\textup{KL}}\big(R\,\|\,\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\bigg).

Observe that since
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
, the above sum is restricted to
\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}<\varepsilon
for large enough
n
. Thus, we can use the inequality (
92
) to further bound

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}^{1+\delta}&\leq\sum_{R\in\mathscr{P}_{n}([q]^{2})}\exp\bigg(-n\delta\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{C(1+\delta)(k-1)d}{2}n\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{\infty}^{2}\bigg)\\
&\leq\sum_{R\in\mathscr{P}_{n}([q]^{2})}\exp\bigg(-\frac{n\delta}{2}\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)\leq C^{\prime}\,,\end{split}

where the second inequality follows for large enough
n
since
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
and
\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{\infty}\leq\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{2}
, and the last inequality follows by Gaussian integration for some constant
C^{\prime}\equiv C^{\prime}(\delta,q)<\infty
that only depends on
\delta,q
. Therefore,
\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}^{1+\delta}<\infty
holds, and uniform integrability of
(\boldsymbol{Z}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

Note that
\mathbb{E}\big(L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\big)^{2}
increases as
\varepsilon_{n}
increases. Thus, w.l.o.g., we may assume that
n^{2/3}\varepsilon_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Also, recall that
\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\equiv\sqrt{n}(R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\tau}}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})
, where
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\tau}}\sim\pi^{\otimes n}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{X}_{\infty}\sim\mathsf{N}\big(\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}-(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})^{\otimes 2}\big)
holds by the central limit theorem (cf. (
78
)). Recalling the random variable
\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}\equiv\exp\Big(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\Big(\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{n},\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\Big\rangle+C\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)\Big)\mathds{1}\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}\Big\}
, we claim that as
n\to\infty
,

\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\infty}:=\exp\bigg(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\Big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{\infty}\,,\,\boldsymbol{X}_{\infty}\Big\rangle\bigg)\,.

(93)

To see this, note that
\exp\Big(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{n},\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\big\rangle\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\exp\Big(\frac{(k-1)d}{2}\big\langle\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\boldsymbol{X}_{\infty},\boldsymbol{X}_{\infty}\big\rangle\Big)
holds by the continuous mapping theorem. Moreover, since
n\varepsilon_{n}\to\infty
and
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we have that

\exp\big(C\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{\infty}^{3}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\;\;\;\;\;\textnormal{and}\;\;\;\;\mathds{1}\big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{n\varepsilon_{n}}\big\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1

Thus, by Slutsky, (
93
) holds. Meanwhile,
(\boldsymbol{Z}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable by Lemma
5.8
, thus

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}=\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\infty}\,.

Note that
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\infty}
can be calculated explicitly as follows. Recall that if
Z\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma)
and the eigenvalues of
A\Sigma
is bounded by
1
in absolute value for a symmetric matrix
A
, then
\mathbb{E}\exp\big(Z^{{\mathsf{T}}}AZ/2\big)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Eig(A\Sigma)}(1-\lambda)^{-1/2}
holds, where
\Eig(\Sigma^{1/2}A\Sigma)=\Eig(A\Sigma)
denotes the set of eignevlues of
A\Sigma
(see e.g.
[
MP92
, Theorem 3.2a.2]
). Also, note that

\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\big(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)^{\otimes 2}=\mathbb{E}_{p}\Big[\widehat{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\widehat{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Big]-\widehat{\Phi}\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}-\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\otimes\widehat{\Phi}\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}+\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)\otimes\big(\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big)=0\,,

where the last equality holds because
{\sf(SYM)}
yields
\widehat{\Phi}_{\psi}\pi=\mathbf{1}
for any
\psi\in\Psi
. Thus, it follows that
\widehat{\Xi}_{\ast}\big(\diag(\pi)^{\otimes 2}-(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})^{\otimes 2}\big)=\Xi_{\ast}
holds. Therefore, by Proposition
5.7
, we can bound

\mathbb{E}\Big(L^{\ast}\big(\boldsymbol{G}(n,m_{n})\big)\Big)^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}=\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\infty}+o_{n}(1)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Eig(\Xi_{\ast})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(k-1)d\lambda}}+o_{n}(1)\,,

which concludes the proof since non-zero elements of
\Eig(\Xi_{\ast})
equals the non-zero elements of
\Eig_{\mathcal{S}}(\Xi)
by Lemma
3.2
.
∎

6
Mutual information between the planted and the null model

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.18
and Propositions
3.20
,
3.21
. In Section
6.1
, we prove Lemma
3.18
. In Section
6.2
, we prove Proposition
3.20
. In Section
6.3
, we prove Proposition
3.21
.

6.1
Proof of Lemma
3.18

By invoking the identity (
44
), we have

\frac{1}{n}D_{\textup{KL}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\,\|\,\boldsymbol{G})\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G)\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\,,

which finishes the proof of the first equality. Turning to the mutual information, we have by definition that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})&\equiv\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G,\underline{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\log\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G)}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}\bigg)\\
&=-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G,\underline{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}\,,\end{split}

(94)

where the last equality is due to (
44
). We now simplify the last sum above. Recall that by Definition
2.2
, if
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
is a factor graph with
m
clauses, then

\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}=\frac{\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})]}=\prod_{a\in F}\frac{\psi_{a}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\,,

(95)

where
\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim{\sf Unif}(V^{k})
. Thus, if we denote by
(\psi_{a_{i}},\delta a_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\boldsymbol{m}}
the random weight functions and neighborhoods of clauses of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
, which are i.i.d. with distribution (
9
), then it follows that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G,\underline{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}=G)}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{m}}\log\frac{\psi_{a_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\delta a_{i}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\Bigg]=\frac{d}{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\log\frac{\psi_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\delta a_{1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\bigg]\,,

(96)

where the last equality holds since
(\psi_{a_{i}},\delta a_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\boldsymbol{m}}
are i.i.d., and
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{m}]=dn/k
. Since
(\psi_{a_{1}},\delta a_{1})
is distributed according to (
9
), the RHS equals

\frac{d}{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\log\frac{\psi_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\delta a_{1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\bigg]=\frac{d}{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\cdot\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\bigg]\,,

(97)

where the outer expectation in the RHS is w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\sim\pi^{\otimes V}
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\sim p
, and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\sim{\sf Unif}(V^{k})
. Observe that since
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\sim\pi^{\otimes V}
, the total variation distance between the empirical distribution of
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
and
\pi
is at most
O(n^{-1/3})
with probability tending to one. Thus, w.h.p.,
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\big]+O(n^{-1/3})\equiv\xi+O(n^{-1/3})
holds. In addition, by our assumption that
\psi(\cdot)\in(0,\infty)
and
\Psi
is finite, there exists constants
c,C\in(0,\infty)
such that
\psi(\cdot)\in[c,C]
holds for any
\psi\in\Psi
. As a consequence, we have that

\begin{split}\frac{d}{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\cdot\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\bigg]&=\frac{d}{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\xi}\cdot\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\xi}\bigg]+o_{n}(1)\\
&=\frac{d}{k}\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\cdot\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})}{\xi}\bigg]+o_{n}(1)\,,\end{split}

(98)

where in the last equality, we again used the fact that the total variation distance between the empirical distribution of
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
and
\pi
is at most
O(n^{-1/3})
with probability tending to one. Combining (
96
), (
97
), (
98
) with (
95
) concludes the proof of Lemma
3.18
.

6.2
Proof of Proposition
3.20

It is well-known that given a function
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
at most of exponential growth and
\alpha>0
, the map
d\to\mathbb{E}f(X_{d})
for
X_{d}\sim{\sf Poi}(\alpha d)
is differentiable w.r.t.
d
with derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}f(X_{d})=\alpha\big(\mathbb{E}f(X_{d}+1)-\mathbb{E}f(X_{d})\big)
. Thus, it follows that

\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\frac{1}{k}\Big(\mathbb{E}\log L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m}+1)\big)-\mathbb{E}\log L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})\big)\Big)\,.

(99)

We couple
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m}+1)
to show that the RHS can be calculated as follows.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any
d>0
and
n,m\geq 1
, there exists a coupling between
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)
such that

\mathbb{E}\log\bigg(\frac{L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)\big)}{L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)}\bigg)=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m),p,u}\Bigg[\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\Bigg]\,,

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m),p,u}
is with respect to
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m),\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim p,\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim u:={\sf Unif}(V^{k})
, and the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}
in the denominator is with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\sim p
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\sim u
.

Proof.

Recalling the Definition
2.2
of the planted model
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
, we can couple
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)
by first drawing
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\sim\pi^{\otimes n}
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
, and then conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
, adding an independent clause
a_{m+1}
to
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)
with neighborhood
\delta a_{m+1}
and the weight function
\psi_{a_{m+1}}
from the distribution

\mathbb{P}\Big(\delta a_{m+1}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\,,\,\psi_{a_{m+1}}=\psi\,\Big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\Big)=\frac{1}{n^{k}}\cdot\frac{p(\psi)\psi(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\star}_{v_{k}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}]}\,.

(100)

Recalling the notation
\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})
in (
8
), we can express
L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)\big)
under such coupling by

L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)\big)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\frac{\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m+1)}(\underline{\sigma})\big]}\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\frac{\psi_{a_{m+1}}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a_{m+1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\cdot\frac{\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\big]}\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)\,.

(101)

Meanwhile, by Bayes rule, the posterior
\mu_{G}(\underline{\sigma})
in (
21
), where
G
have
n
variables
m
clauses, equals

\mu_{G}(\underline{\sigma})=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)=G\big)}=\frac{\frac{\psi_{G}(\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\boldsymbol{G}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\big]}\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}{L(G)}\,.

By taking
G=G^{\star}(n,m)
and combining with (
101
), it follows that

\mathbb{E}\log\bigg(\frac{L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m+1)\big)}{L\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)}\bigg)=\mathbb{E}\log\bigg(\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\frac{\psi_{a_{m+1}}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a_{m+1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\mu_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}(\underline{\sigma})\bigg)=\mathbb{E}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\psi_{a_{m+1}}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a_{m+1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\,,

(102)

where in the last expression, we abbreviated the superscript in
\underline{\sigma}^{1}\sim\mu_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}
and the outer expectation is w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)
and
(\delta a_{m+1},\psi_{a_{m+1}})
. By the conditional law of
(\delta a_{m+1},\psi_{a_{m+1}})
in (
100
), we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\log\bigg\langle\frac{\psi_{a_{m+1}}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a_{m+1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\,\,\bigg|\,\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\bigg]&=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\sum_{\delta a\in V^{k}}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\frac{p(\psi)\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\delta a})}{n^{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\bigg]\,,\end{split}

where the outer expectation
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}
is taken w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\sim p
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\sim{\sf Unif}(V^{k})
independent of everything else. Thus, by tower property, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\bigg[\log\bigg\langle\frac{\psi_{a_{m+1}}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a_{m+1}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\,\,\bigg|\,\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\bigg]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\bigg[\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\bigg]\,\,\Bigg|\,\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\Bigg]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\Bigg[\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\log\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\Bigg]\,,\end{split}

where the last equality follows since
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}
and the conditional expectation over
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
is exchangeable and the expectation w.r.t. to the measure
\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\cdot\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\big)
equals the expectation over
\underline{\sigma}\sim\mu_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}
. Therefore, taking expectation in the equality above and combining with (
102
) concludes the proof.
∎

In order to prove Proposition
3.20
, it remains to approximate
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]
under
\underline{\sigma}\sim\mu_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}
. Recalling that
R_{\underline{\sigma}}
denotes the empirical distribution of
\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}
(cf. (
117
)), consider the event

\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}(n,m):=\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\Big(\|R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}-\pi\|_{\infty}\geq n^{-1/3}\,\Big|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\Big)\leq\exp\big(-n^{1/4}\big)\bigg\}\,.

Then, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists a constant
c\equiv c_{q}
which only depends on
q
such that for any
n,m\geq 1
, we have
\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\in\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}(n,m)\big)\leq\exp(-cn^{1/3})
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C\equiv C_{k,q,\Psi}
depending only on
k,q,\Psi
such that on the w.h.p. event
\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}
, for any
\psi\in\Psi
and
\delta a\in V^{k}
, we have

\Bigg|\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}-\xi^{-1}\cdot\Big\langle\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\Bigg|\leq Cn^{-1/3}.

(103)

Proof.

For the first statement, note that by Markov’s inequality and tower property, we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\in\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}(n,m)\big)\leq\exp\big(n^{1/4}\big)\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\|R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}-\pi\|_{\infty}\geq n^{-1/3}\Big)\,.

Invoking (
58
),
\mathbb{P}\big(\|R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}-\pi\|_{\infty}\geq n^{-1/3}\big)\leq q\exp(-2n^{1/3})
holds, thus plugging this bound shows that
\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\in\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}(n,m)\big)\leq\exp(-cn^{1/3})
holds for some
c\equiv c_{q}>0
.

We next prove (
103
). To this end, assume that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\in\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}(n,m)
happens, and fix any
\psi\in\Psi
and
\delta a\in V^{k}
. Throughout, we denote
C
by a positive constant that depends only depends on
k,q,\Psi
and write
f=O_{k,q,\Psi}(g)
if there exists a constant
C
such that
|f|\leq Cg
. Note that

\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi\big\|_{\infty}\geq n^{-1/3}\Big\}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\leq C\bigg\langle\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi\big\|_{\infty}\geq n^{-1/3}\Big\}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\leq C\exp\big(-n^{1/4}\big)\,,

(104)

where the last inequality holds since
\langle f(\underline{\sigma})\rangle_{G}\equiv\mathbb{E}\big[f(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}=G\big]
holds by definition for
f:[q]^{V}\to\mathbb{R}
. Meanwhile, note that if
\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
satisfies
\|R_{\underline{\tau}}-\pi\|_{\infty}\leq n^{-1/3}
, then

\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau})_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{p}\Bigg[\sum_{\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in[q]^{k}}\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}R_{\underline{\tau}}(i_{s})\Bigg]=\mathbb{E}_{p}\Bigg[\sum_{\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in[q]^{k}}\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}\Bigg]+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,,

which equals
\xi+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})
. Thus, it follows that

\begin{split}\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi\big\|_{\infty}\leq n^{-1/3}\Big\}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}&=\bigg\langle\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\xi+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})}\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi\big\|_{\infty}\leq n^{-1/3}\Big\}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\\
&=\xi^{-1}\cdot\Big\langle\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,,\end{split}

where the last equality holds since
\big\langle\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})\big\{\|R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi\|_{\infty}\geq n^{-1/3}\big\}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)}\leq C\exp\big(-n^{1/4}\big)
holds on the event
\mathcal{A}_{{\sf bal}}(n,m)
. Therefore, combining with (
104
) completes the proof of (
103
).
∎

The proof of Proposition
3.20
is straightforward from Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.20
.

The first statement (
52
) follows immediately from (
99
) and Lemma
6.1
. To establish the second statement (
53
) from the first statement, note that Lemma
6.2
implies the following. Given a continuous function
F:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
C\equiv C_{F,k,q,\Psi}
, which only depends on
F,k,q,\Psi
, such that

\Bigg|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}F\Bigg(\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\Bigg)-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}F\bigg(\xi^{-1}\Big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\bigg)\Bigg|\leq Cn^{-1/3}\,.

(105)

Here, we note that the continuity of
F
suffices (rather than Lipschitz continuity) since there exist constants
c_{i}\equiv c_{i,\Psi}>0
such that for any
\psi\in\Psi
,
\psi(\cdot)\in[c_{1},c_{2}]
holds. Thus, taking
F(x)=x\log x
, the second statement (
53
) follows from (
52
).
∎

6.3
Proof of Proposition
3.21

In order to prove Proposition
3.21
from Proposition
3.20
, we establish the following lemmas. Recall that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m})
, where
\boldsymbol{m}\sim{\sf Poi}(dn/k)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists a constant
C\equiv C_{\Psi}>0
such that for any
m\geq 1
,
\big|\log L(G)\big|\leq Cm
holds for any factor graph
G
with
m
clauses. In particular, we have
\mathbb{E}\big|\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\big|\leq Cdn/k
.

Proof.

We have for a factor graph
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
that

L(G)\equiv\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\prod_{a\in F}\frac{\psi_{a}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}\,.

Note that since
\psi(\cdot),\psi\in\Psi
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
, there exist constants
C_{i}\equiv C_{i,\Psi}>0,i=1,2
, such that for any
\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V},\delta a\in V^{k},\psi\in\Psi
,
\frac{\psi(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]}\in[C_{1},C_{2}]
holds. Thus, if
G
has
m
clauses,
L(G)\in[C_{1}^{m},C_{2}^{m}]
holds. Therefore,
\big|\log L(G)\big|\leq Cm
for some constant
C\equiv C_{\Psi}>0
. In particular, this implies that
\mathbb{E}\big|\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\big|\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{m}=Cdn/k
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For
d<d_{\ast}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, assuming
(MIN)
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta\equiv\delta(\varepsilon,q,\pi)>0
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
which only depends on
\varepsilon,q,\pi
such that the following holds: if
n\geq n_{0}
and there exists an estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds, then
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}>\delta
holds.

Proof.

To start with, invoking (
105
) for
F(x)=x
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}=\xi+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,.

(106)

Thus, recalling the function
\mathcal{F}(R)\equiv\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau})\big]\prod_{s=1}^{k}R(\sigma_{s},\tau_{s})
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\big\langle\mathcal{F}(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi^{2}+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,.

(107)

Let us now assume
d<d_{\ast}
. Then,
\mathbb{E}\big\langle\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
by Theorem
2.10
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}\to\mathbb{E}\mathcal{F}(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})-\xi^{2}=0\,.

To prove the second claim, assume that the condition
{\sf(MIN)}
and fix
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}
satisfies
{\sf(EXG)}
(cf. Lemma
4.2
), Lemma
4.10
shows that there exists
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
such that if
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
, then
\mathbb{E}\big\langle\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\geq\eta
. Note that since
\|R_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\|_{1}\leq q
for
\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
,
\mathbb{E}\big\langle\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\geq\eta
implies that

\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}>\frac{\eta}{2}\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\geq\frac{\eta}{2q}\,.

Meanwhile, as shown in the proof of Lemma
6.2
, there exists
C\equiv C_{q}>0
such that for
\ell=1,2
,

\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{\ell}}-\pi\big\|_{1}>n^{-1/3}\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\leq Cn^{-1/3}\,,

which can be seen by conditioning on
\boldsymbol{m}=m
and decomposing the LHS into the events where
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\in\mathcal{A}(n,m)
or
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m)\notin\mathcal{A}(n,m)
. Thus, there exists a constant
c\equiv c_{q}>0
such that for large enough
n\geq n_{0}(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
,

\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\mathds{1}\Big\{\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}>\frac{\eta}{2}~~\textnormal{and}~~\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{\ell}}-\pi\big\|_{1}\leq n^{-1/3}~~\textnormal{for}~~\ell=1,2\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}>c\eta\,.

(108)

Now, observe that by the condition
{\sf(MIN)}
, there exists
\epsilon\equiv\epsilon(\eta)>0
such that

\min\bigg\{\mathcal{F}(R):R\in[0,1]^{q}\,,\;\;\;R\mathbf{1}=R^{{\mathsf{T}}}\mathbf{1}=\pi\,,\;\;\;\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|\geq\frac{\eta}{2}\bigg\}>\mathcal{F}(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})+\epsilon\,.

Thus, by continuity of
R\to\mathcal{F}(R)
, there exists small enough
\epsilon^{\prime}\equiv\epsilon^{\prime}(\eta)>0
such that

\min\bigg\{\mathcal{F}(R):R\in[0,1]^{q}\,,\;\;\;\big\|R\mathbf{1}-\pi\big\|_{1}+\big\|R^{{\mathsf{T}}}\mathbf{1}-\pi\big\|_{1}<\epsilon^{\prime}\,,\;\;\;\big\|R-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|\geq\frac{\eta}{2}\bigg\}>\mathcal{F}(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})+\epsilon^{\prime}\,.

As a consequence, for any
\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}\in[q]^{V}
, we have
