\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}}-\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}\big\|_{1}>\frac{\eta}{2}~~\textnormal{and}~~\big\|R_{\underline{\sigma}^{\ell}}-\pi\big\|_{1}\leq n^{-1/3}~~\textnormal{for}~~\ell=1,2\Longrightarrow\mathcal{F}(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}})>\xi^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}\,,

(109)

where we used the fact that
\mathcal{F}(\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}})=\xi^{2}
. Finally, recalling the estimate (
107
), we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\Big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\Big)^{2}+O(n^{-1/3})&=\mathbb{E}\big\langle\mathcal{F}(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi^{2}\\
&\geq\epsilon^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\mathds{1}\Big\{\mathcal{F}(R_{\underline{\sigma}^{1},\underline{\sigma}^{2}})\geq\xi^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\\
&>c\epsilon^{\prime}\eta\,,\end{split}

where we used
(MIN)
in the second inequality and the last inequality is by (
108
) and (
109
). Therefore, taking
\delta=c\epsilon^{\prime}\eta
completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.21
.

The statements
\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq 0
follows from Jensen’s inequality. Indeed, for ease of notations, let
F_{0}(x)=x\log x
. Since
F_{0}(\cdot)
is convex,

\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G})\log L(\boldsymbol{G})\geq F_{0}\big(\mathbb{E}L(\boldsymbol{G})\big)=F_{0}(1)=0\,,

where the first equality follows from change of measure (see also (
44
)). Similarly, by Proposition
3.20
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}F_{0}\Bigg(\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\Bigg)\geq\frac{1}{k}F_{0}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\bigg\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}})\big]}\bigg\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\Bigg)=0\,.

To prove the second statement, consider
d_{0}<d_{\ast}
. Note that for any
0<d\leq d_{0}
, we have by Proposition
3.20
that

\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\frac{1}{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}F_{0}\Big(\xi^{-1}\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\Big)+o_{n}(1)\to 0\quad\textnormal{as}\quad n\to\infty\,,

(110)

where the convergence holds since
\xi^{-1}\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
by Lemma
6.4
and
\big(\xi^{-1}\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\big)_{n\geq 1}
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
. Moreover, note that
\lim_{d\to 0}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=0
holds for fixed
n\geq 1
by Lemma
6.3
. Thus, by fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\Big\rvert_{d=d_{0}}=\int_{0}^{d_{0}}\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}){\rm d}d\to 0\quad\textnormal{as}\quad n\to\infty\,,

where the convergence holds by (
110
) and dominated convergence theorem, since the equality (
52
) in Proposition
3.20
shows that
\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
is uniformly bounded for
n\geq 1
and
d\leq d_{0}
.

Next, we prove the third statement. Assume that
{\sf(MIN)}
holds and that
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, Lemma
6.4
yields that under this assumption,
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}>\delta
holds for
n\geq n_{0}
for some
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon,q,\pi)
and
\delta\equiv\delta(\varepsilon,q,\pi)>0
. Moreover, since
F_{0}(x)=x\log x
is strongly convex with
F_{0}(1)=0,F_{0}^{\prime}(1)=1
, there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that
F_{0}(x)\geq x-1+C(x-1)^{2}
holds for
x>0
. Thus, for
n\geq n_{0}
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}F_{0}\Big(\xi^{-1}\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\Big)&\geq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big[\xi^{-1}\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-1\big]+C\xi^{-2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}\\
&=C\xi^{-2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}\big(\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}-\xi\big)^{2}+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\\
&\geq C\xi^{-2}\delta+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,,\end{split}

where the equality follows from (
106
). Combining with Proposition
3.20
, it follows that

\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=k^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},p,u}F_{0}\Big(\xi^{-1}\big\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}}\Big)+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\geq C\xi^{-2}k^{-1}\delta+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,,

thus letting
\eta\equiv C\xi^{-2}k^{-1}\delta/2
concludes the proof of the third statement.

Finally, we prove the fourth statement under
{\sf(MIN)}
condition. To this end, consider
d>d_{\ast}
. Then, there exists
d_{0}\in(d_{\ast},d)
, and since weak recovery is possible at
d_{0}
, there exists a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that for any
\ell\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon
holds for some estimator
\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{0})
where
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{0}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n_{\ell},d_{0},p,\pi)
. Observe that this implies that for any fixed
\ell\geq 1
and
\breve{d}\in(d_{0},d)
,

\mathbb{E}\big[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\big]\geq\frac{1}{q}+\varepsilon\quad\textnormal{for some}\quad\hat{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\hat{\underline{\sigma}}_{n_{\ell}}(\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star})\quad\textnormal{where}\quad\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n_{\ell},\breve{d},p,\pi)

since subsampling the clauses independently with probability
\breve{d}/d
from
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n_{\ell},\breve{d},p,\pi)
gives a sample drawn from
\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n_{\ell},d_{0},p,\pi)
by Poisson thinning. Hence, by the third statement which we established in the previous paragraph, there exists
\eta\equiv\eta(\varepsilon)
and
n_{0}\equiv n_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
n_{\ell}\geq n_{0}
and
\breve{d}\in(d_{0},d)
,
\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star})\geq\eta
holds. Since we proved in the first statement that
\frac{\partial}{\partial d}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq 0
holds in general, it follows that for all
\ell
large enough so that
n_{\ell}\geq n_{0}
,

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\geq\eta(d-d_{0})\quad\textnormal{where}\quad\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf plant}(n_{\ell},d,p,\pi)\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Nontriviality of the weak recovery threshold

In this section, we prove Proposition
3.1
. We begin with the case where
{\sf(SYM)}
does not hold. Then we must have some
\psi,s
and
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s}=\tau]>\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]>\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s}=\tau^{\prime}].

For each vertex
v
let
d_{v}^{\psi}
be the number of clauses of type
\psi
containing
v
. Define

x_{i}:=\frac{dp(\psi)\sum_{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k-1}}\psi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k-1},i)\prod_{j=1}^{j-1}\pi_{\tau_{j}}}{\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]}=\frac{dp(\psi)\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s}=i]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]},

When
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{v}=i
we have that
d_{v}^{\psi}
is Poisson with mean
(1+o(1))x_{i}
. We take the very simple estimator

\hat{\sigma}_{v}=\begin{cases}\tau&d_{v}^{\psi}\geq dp(\psi)\\
\tau^{\prime}&d_{v}^{\psi}\geq dp(\psi).\end{cases}

Then

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q\pi_{\tau}}\mathbb{P}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{v}=\tau,d_{v}^{\psi}\geq dp(\psi)]+\frac{1}{q\pi_{\tau^{\prime}}}\mathbb{P}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{v}=\tau^{\prime},d_{v}^{\psi}<dp(\psi)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\big(\mathbb{P}[\hbox{Pois}(x_{\tau})\geq dp(\psi)]-\mathbb{P}[\hbox{Pois}(x_{\tau^{\prime}})<dp(\psi)]\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q}\big(\mathbb{P}[\hbox{Pois}(x_{\tau})\geq dp(\psi)]-\mathbb{P}[\hbox{Pois}(x_{\tau^{\prime}})\geq dp(\psi)]\big)\geq\frac{1}{q}+\epsilon,

since
x_{\tau}>x_{\tau^{\prime}}
which establishes weak recovery whenever
d>0
.

Now suppose that
{\sf(SYM)}
holds and
d<\frac{1}{k-1}
. In this case the graph is subcritical and almost all the variables are in trees of size
O(1)
. We will consider the local weak limit of
(G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})
under the planted model. A vertex of type
i
is connected to a Poisson with mean

\sum_{\psi}\frac{dp(\psi)\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})\,|\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s}=i]}{\mathbb{E}_{p,\pi}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]}=dp(\psi)

neighbouring clauses. In particular, the distribution does not depend on the type
i
and so the law of the local neighbourhood is a branching process independent of the state of the root. Among all the trees in the graph with a fixed topology, a
\pi_{i}+o(1)
will have state
i
at the root. Since the estimator cannot distinguish between trees with the same topology, it will be independent of
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}
and so
\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]=\frac{1}{q}+o(1)
. Hence weak recovery is impossible when
d<\frac{1}{k-1}
.

Finally, we show that for
d
large that weak recovery is possible. We define

\Psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})=\sum_{a}\psi_{a}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}_{\delta a})

and take our estimator to be the maximizer

\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}}=\argmax\Psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}}).

among
\hat{\sigma}
with empirical distribution
\pi+o(1)
. We will first show that when
d
is large enough that the joint distribution of
(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}})
is not close to the product measure.

Recall that we defined the map
\mathcal{F}(R^{\prime}):=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau})]\prod_{s=1}^{k}R^{\prime}(\sigma_{s},\tau_{s})
in (
18
). Let
R=\pi\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
and
R_{\star}=\hbox{diag}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{q})
. Then

\mathcal{F}(R_{\star})-\mathcal{F}(R)=\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau})]\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi(\sigma_{s})\pi(\tau_{s})-\sum_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{p}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\tau})]\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi(\sigma_{s})\pi(\tau_{s})

This can be expressed as
\sum_{\psi}p(\psi)\psi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})
where
X_{i}
are IID according to
\pi
and since at least one of the
\psi
is not constant we have that this variance is strictly positive and hence
\mathcal{F}(R_{\star})>\mathcal{F}(R)
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
be fixed and let
\underline{\tau}\in[q]^{V}
be a fixed configuration. Define their joint empirical distribution as

W_{ij}=W_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\underline{\tau})=\frac{1}{n}|\{v\in V:\sigma^{\star}_{v}=i,\tau_{v}=j\}|.

The probability that a clause of type
\psi
connects variables
v_{1}\ldots,v_{k}
is

\frac{d}{Z{n\choose k}}\psi(\sigma^{\star}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma^{\star}_{v_{k}})

for some normalizing constant
Z
and so

\mathbb{E}[\Psi(\underline{\tau})]=\frac{d}{Z{n\choose k}}\sum_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}}\psi(\sigma^{\star}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma^{\star}_{v_{k}})\psi(\tau_{v_{1}},\ldots,\tau_{v_{k}})=\frac{dn}{Z}\mathcal{F}(W)+o(n).

and

\mathbb{E}[\Psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})]=\frac{dn}{Z}\mathcal{F}(R_{\star})+o(n).

If we reveal each clause one by one, each step affects the value of
\Psi(\underline{\tau})
by
O(1)
so by the Azuma-Hoeffding Inequality,

\mathbb{P}[|\Psi(\underline{\tau})-\mathbb{E}[\Psi(\underline{\tau})]|\geq d\epsilon n]\leq 2\exp(-C\epsilon^{2}dn)

By the continuity of
\mathcal{F}
, for some
\delta>0
sufficiently small we have that

\inf_{R^{\prime}:\|R^{\prime}-R\|\leq\delta}\mathcal{F}(R_{\star})-\mathcal{F}(R^{\prime})\geq\delta.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\|W(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}})-R\|<\delta]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|\Psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})-\mathbb{E}[\Psi(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}})]|\geq\frac{d\delta n}{3Z}\Big]+\sum_{\underline{\tau}:\|W(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\underline{\tau})-R\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}\Big[|\Psi(\underline{\tau})-\mathbb{E}[\Psi(\underline{\tau})]|\geq\frac{d\delta n}{3Z}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2q^{n}\exp(-C(\delta/3Z)^{2}dn)=o(1),

for large enough
d
. Hence, we have that with high probability
W(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}})
is at least
\delta
away from
\pi\pi^{T}
. The perfomance of our estimator can be written as

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})\right]=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{W_{i,\Gamma(i)}(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}})}{\pi_{i}}\Bigg\}.

Let us write

\Upsilon(R^{\prime})=\max_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{R^{\prime}_{i,\Gamma(i)}}{\pi_{i}}\Bigg\}

Let

\mathcal{R}_{\pi}^{\delta}=\{R^{\prime}:\|R^{\prime}-R\|\geq\delta,\sum_{i}R_{ij}=\pi_{j},\sum_{j}R_{ij}=\pi_{i}\}

Since w.h.p. we have that
W(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}})
is
o(1)
distance from
\mathcal{R}_{\pi}^{\delta}
, to prove weak recovery it is enough to show that

\inf_{R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\Upsilon(R^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{q}+\epsilon.

(111)

Since
\mathcal{R}
is compact it is enough to show that
\Upsilon(R^{\prime})>\frac{1}{q}
for all
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi}^{\delta}
. First we have that for any
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
that

\Upsilon(R^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{q!}\sum_{\Gamma\in S_{q}}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{R^{\prime}_{i,\Gamma(i)}}{\pi_{i}}=\frac{1}{q^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{q}R^{\prime}_{i,i^{\prime}}}{\pi_{i}}=\frac{1}{q}

since the max is always at least the average and since every
\Gamma(i)
is equal to
i^{\prime}
for a
\frac{1}{q}
fraction of
\Gamma
. So if
\Upsilon(R^{\prime})=\frac{1}{q}
then we must have

\forall\ \Gamma:\ \ \frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{R^{\prime}_{i,\Gamma(i)}}{\pi_{i}}=\frac{1}{q}.

In particular, switching two entries of
\Gamma
cannot change the above expression so for any
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}

\frac{R_{ij}}{\pi_{i}}-\frac{R_{ij^{\prime}}}{\pi_{i}}=\frac{R_{i^{\prime}j}}{\pi_{i^{\prime}}}-\frac{R_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}{\pi_{i^{\prime}}}.

But this can only occur if all the rows of
R_{ij}/\pi_{i}
are the same in which case
R^{\prime}=\pi\pi^{T}
. Hence, (
111
) holds and for large enough
d
we have weak recovery.

Appendix B
Hypothesis testing below the weak recovery threshold

In this section, we prove Lemmas
3.13
,
3.15
, and
3.17
.

B.1
Proof of Lemma
3.13

We show that

\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}\equiv\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X_{\zeta\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,.

is discrete if and only if
d\in(0,\frac{1}{k-1})
. The case where
d<\frac{1}{k-1}
is straightforward as in this case

\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\mathbb{P}[X_{\zeta\infty}\geq 1]\leq\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}[X_{\zeta\infty}]<\infty

and so by Borel-Cantelli, only finitely many
X_{\zeta\infty}
are non-zero and so
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
takes values in the set given in equation (
45
) and hence is discrete. When
d\geq\frac{1}{k-1}
we have that
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
is the exponential of a weighted sum of Poisson random variables the sum of whose means is infinite and so we can write

\log(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty})=\lim_{T\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}-g(T)

where
f(t)
and
g(t)
are deterministic functions,
f(t)
is strictly positive and
N_{t}
is a standard Poisson process. We will show that any such random variable must be continuous. Define

R(K,t)=\max_{x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=x_{i}],

that is the maximum probability
\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}
puts on
k
distinct points. We claim that,

\frac{d}{dt}R(K,t)\bigg|_{t=T}\leq-(R(K,T)-R(K-1,T))+(R(K+1,T)-R(K,T)).

(112)

To see this, suppose that
\mathcal{X}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{K}\}
is some maximizing set of
k
atoms at time
T
. Note that at time
t
the size of the
j
-th largest atom is
R(j,t)-R(j-1,t)
. Between time
t
and
t+\delta t
there is probability
\delta t
of a new Poisson point which increases the integral by
f(t)
. Thus we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=x]\bigg|_{t=T}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=x]-\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=x-f(t)])

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=x]+\sum_{y:y+f(T)\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=y]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}:x-f(T)\not\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=x]+\sum_{y\not\in\mathcal{X}:y+f(T)\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dN_{t}=y]

\displaystyle\leq-|\{x\in\mathcal{X}:x-f(T)\not\in\mathcal{X}\}|(R(K,T)-R(K-1,T))

\displaystyle\qquad+|\{y\not\in\mathcal{X}:y+f(T)\in\mathcal{X}\}|(R(K+1,T)-R(K,T))

where the third equality holds by canceling out terms that appear in the first and second sums while the inequality holds by noting that every atom in
\mathcal{X}
is one of the largest
K
atoms while every while every atoms in
\mathcal{X}^{c}
is at most the
(K+1)
-th biggest. Note that

|\{x\in\mathcal{X}:x-f(T)\not\in\mathcal{X}\}|=|\{y\not\in\mathcal{X}:y+f(T)\in\mathcal{X}\}|

and both are at least of size one, since
x_{1}
is not in the former. Hence we have equation (
112
). Also since
R(j,t)-R(j-1,t)
is the size of the
j
-th atoms it is decreasing in
j
so we have in particular that

\frac{d}{dt}R(K,t)\bigg|_{t=T}\leq 0.

Suppose that
R(1,t)\to q>0
as
t\to\infty
. Then set
K=\lceil\frac{1}{q}\rceil
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\sum_{j=1}^{K}R(j,t)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{K}-(R(j,t)-R(j-1,t))+(R(j+1,t)-R(j,t))

\displaystyle=-R(1,t)+(R(K+1,t)-R(K,t))

\displaystyle\leq-q+\frac{1}{K+1}<0.

But
\sum_{j=1}^{K}R(j,0)=K
we have that
\sum_{j=1}^{K}R(j,t)\leq K-t\big(q-(K+1)^{-1}\big)
for all
t
which contradicts the fact that it is non-negative. Hence
R(1,t)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
. Now if
X
and
Y
are independent random variables,

\max_{x}\mathbb{P}[X+Y=x]\leq\max_{x}\mathbb{P}[X=x]

and so

\max_{x}\mathbb{P}[\log(\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty})=x]\leq\inf_{t}R(1,t)=0.

Hence
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
has a continuous distribution.

B.2
Proof of Lemma
3.15

Fix
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
and assume
(SYM)
. We only prove the weak convergence

T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}\equiv\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X^{\star}_{\zeta,\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,,

(113)

since the weak convergence for
T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G})
can be established using the same argument. To start with, for
K\geq 1
, let us denote

T_{n,K}(G):=\prod_{1\leq\ell\leq K}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X_{\zeta}(G)}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,.

Thus,
T_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\equiv T_{n,K_{n}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
holds by definition. Observe that by considering a fixed
K\geq 1
, we have by Fact
3.9
-(2) that as
n\to\infty
,

T_{n,K}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{K}:=\prod_{\ell=1}^{K}\prod_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})^{X^{\star}_{\zeta,\infty}}e^{-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}}\Big\}\,.

Observe that since
\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{K}
converges a.s. to
\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}
as
K\to\infty
, we have
\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}
holds. Thus, combining with the above weak convergence, a diagonal argument shows that there exists an arbitrarily slowly growing sequence
(K_{n}^{\prime})_{n\geq 1}
such that
1\ll K_{n}^{\prime}\leq K_{n}
holds and as
n\to\infty
,

T_{n,K_{n}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}^{\star}_{\infty}\,.

Hence, in order to achieve our goal in Eq. (
113
), it suffices to prove that as
n\to\infty
,

X_{n}:=\sum_{\ell=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{\log(1+\delta_{\zeta})X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}\Big\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0\,,

(114)

since
T_{n,K_{n}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})/T_{n,K^{\prime}_{n}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=e^{X_{n}}
holds by definition. For the rest of the proof, we prove Eq. (
114
) by showing that
\mathbb{E}X_{n}^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

To this end, we calculate the moments of
(X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}))_{\zeta\in\cup_{\ell\leq K_{n}}S_{\ell}}
. Since the length of
\zeta
is
2\ell\leq K_{n}\ll\log n
, it is standard to approximate the the first and second moments of
X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})
(see e.g.
[
COEJ+18
, Eq. (8.7)]
) as follows. For two distinct signatures
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\cup_{\ell\leq K_{n}}S_{\ell}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)\,,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\Var\big(X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})\big)=\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)\,,\\
&\mathbb{E}X_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})X_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta^{\prime}}+O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)\,,\end{split}

where the dominant error
\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}
comes from approximating the empirical distribution of
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
by
\pi
. Moreover, note that the number of signatures
\zeta\in\cup_{\ell\leq K_{n}}S_{\ell}
is at most
e^{O(\log\log n)}\ll n^{1/10}
since
K_{n}=O(\log\log n)
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}X_{n}^{2}
can be bounded by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}X_{n}^{2}&\leq\sum_{\ell=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{\log^{2}(1+\delta_{\zeta})\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}+\big(\log(1+\delta_{\zeta})\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}\big)^{2}\Big\}\\
&~~~~~~~+\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell},\zeta^{\prime}\in S_{\ell^{\prime}}}\Big(\log(1+\delta_{\zeta})\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}-\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}\Big)\Big(\log(1+\delta_{\zeta^{\prime}})\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta^{\prime}}-\lambda_{\zeta^{\prime}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}\Big)+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\\
&\leq\sum_{\ell=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{\infty}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{(1+\delta_{\zeta})\log^{2}(1+\delta_{\zeta})\lambda_{\zeta}+\big((1+\delta_{\zeta})\log(1+\delta_{\zeta})-\delta_{\zeta}\big)^{2}\lambda_{\zeta}^{2}\Big\}\\
&~~~~~~~+\bigg(\sum_{\ell=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{\infty}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\big((1+\delta_{\zeta})\log(1+\delta_{\zeta})-\delta_{\zeta}\big)\lambda_{\zeta}\bigg)^{2}+O_{k,q,\Psi}(n^{-1/3})\,,\end{split}

(115)

where the final inequality holds since
\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}=\lambda_{\zeta}(1+\delta_{\zeta})
. We upper bound the RHS by using a taylor approximation w.r.t.
\delta_{\zeta}
. To do so, we first argue that
\sup_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}|\delta_{\zeta}|\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
, which can be argued using the following lemma.

Lemma B.1
.

Consider a triangular array of stochastic matrices
(P_{n,m})_{1\leq m\leq a_{n},n\geq 1}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
, where
a_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and it satisfies the following. For every
1\leq m\leq a_{n}
,
\pi
is the stationary distribution of
P_{n,m}
, and there exists a constant
c>0
such that the minimial element of
P_{n,m}
is bounded below by
c
, i.e.
P_{n,m}(i,j)\geq c
holds for
i,j\in[q]
. Then, the product
\prod_{m=1}^{a_{n}}P_{n,m}
converges to
\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
and
1\leq t\leq a_{n}
, consider the following distances:

\Delta_{n}(t):=\sup_{i\in[q]}\Big\|\Big(\prod_{m=1}^{t}P_{n,m}\Big)(i,\cdot)-\pi\Big\|_{\tv}\,,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\overline{\Delta}_{n}(t):=\sup_{i,j\in[q]}\Big\|\Big(\prod_{m=1}^{t}P_{n,m}\Big)(i,\cdot)-\Big(\prod_{m=1}^{t}P_{n,m}\Big)(j,\cdot)\Big\|_{\tv}\,.

Then, since
\pi
is the stationary distribution for
\prod_{m=1}^{t}P_{n,m}
,
\Delta_{n}(t)\leq\overline{\Delta}_{n}(t)
holds (see e.g. proof of
[
LPW08
, Lemma 4.10]
). Moreover, a standard coupling argument (see e.g.
[
LPW08
, Lemma 4.11]
) shows that for any
n\geq 1
and
1\leq s\leq s+t\leq a_{n}
, we have

\begin{split}&\overline{\Delta}_{n}(t+s)\leq\overline{\Delta}_{n}(t)\overline{\Delta}_{n}(s;t)\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{where}\,,\\
&\overline{\Delta}_{n}(s;t):=\sup_{i,j\in[q]}\Big\|\Big(\prod_{m=t+1}^{t+s}P_{n,m}\Big)(i,\cdot)-\Big(\prod_{m=t+1}^{t+s}P_{n,m}\Big)(j,\cdot)\Big\|_{\tv}\,.\end{split}

Finally, note that
\overline{\Delta}_{n}(1;t)\leq 1-c
holds for any
0\leq t\leq a_{n}-1
since every element of
P_{n,t+1}
is bounded below by
c>0
. Thus, by the above submultiplicative property, we have that

\Delta_{n}(t)\leq\overline{\Delta}_{n}(t)\leq(1-c)^{t}\,.

By letting
t=a_{n}
, the inequality above establishes that
\prod_{m=1}^{a_{n}}P_{n,m}
converges to
\mathbf{1}\pi^{{\mathsf{T}}}
.
∎

To use Lemma
B.1
, observe that under
(SYM)
, the matrix
\Phi_{\zeta,s,t}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
defined in (
40
) is a stochastic matrix with stationary distribution
\pi
. Moreover, since we assumed that
\Phi
is finite whose elements are positive weight functions, the elements of
\Phi_{\psi,s,t}
are lower bounded by a constant
c_{\Psi}>0
. Recalling that
\Phi_{\zeta}:=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{\psi_{i},s_{i},t_{i}}
for a signature
\zeta=(\psi_{1},s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell},s_{\ell},t_{\ell})
, it follows from Lemma
B.1
that

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\big|\delta_{\zeta}\big|\equiv\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\big|\tr(\Phi_{\zeta})-1\big|=0\,.

Hence, the constants
(\delta_{\zeta})_{\zeta\in\cup_{\ell\geq 1}S_{\ell}}
are bounded from
-1
and
\infty
. Therefore, by using a taylor approximation for the term
\log(1+\delta_{\zeta})
in the RHS of (
115
), we can further bound

\mathbb{E}X_{n}^{2}\leq C\Bigg(\sum_{\ell=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{\infty}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\Big\{\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}+\lambda_{\zeta}^{2}\delta_{\zeta}^{4}\Big\}+\bigg(\sum_{\ell=K_{n}^{\prime}+1}^{\infty}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}\bigg)^{2}+n^{-1/3}\Bigg)\,,

where
C>0
only depends on
k,q,\Psi
. Since for
d<d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}
,
\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\sum_{\zeta\in S_{\ell}}\lambda_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta}^{2}<\infty
holds by Lemma
3.11
, the RHS tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore,
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
, which concludes the proof of (
114
).

B.3
Proof of Lemma
3.17

Let
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
be the factor graph obtained from
\gamma
resampling procedure starting from
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
. By definitions of the planted model and the resampling procedures, the factor graph
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
is distributed as follows. Conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}
, independently draw for each clause
a\in F
the neighborhood
\delta a
and the weight function
\psi_{a}
from the distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\delta a=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\,,\,\psi_{a}=\psi\big)=\frac{p(\psi)}{n^{k}}\bigg(\frac{(1-\gamma)\psi(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]}+\frac{\gamma}{\xi}\bigg)\,.

On the other hand, let
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\equiv(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}(n,m,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},p_{\gamma},\pi)
be the planted model with
\gamma
-modified weight functions defined in (
51
). Then, conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}
, the neighborhood
\delta a
and the weight function
\psi_{a}
in
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}(n,m,\underline{\sigma})
are independently drawn from a slightly different distribution

\mathbb{P}\big(\delta a=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\,,\,\psi_{a}=\psi\big)=\frac{p(\psi)}{n^{k}}\cdot\frac{(1-\gamma)\psi(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})+\gamma\xi}{\xi}\,.

Hence, given
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}
, the likelihood ratio of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
and
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}\equiv(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
evaluated at a factor graph
G=(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F})
is given by

\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big((\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}=\prod_{a\in F}\bigg(1+\frac{\gamma\big(\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]-\xi\big)}{\gamma\xi+(1-\gamma)\psi_{a}(\underline{\sigma}_{\delta a})}\bigg)\,.

(116)

Observe that under
(SYM)
, Lemma
5.6
shows that
\mathbb{E}_{p,u}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]-\xi=O_{p}(n^{-1})
. More precisely, for
\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}
, define the vector
R_{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}}(i)\big)_{i\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
by the empirical distribution of
\underline{\sigma}
:

R_{\underline{\sigma}}(i):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V}\mathds{1}\{\underline{\sigma}_{v}=i\}\,.

(117)

Then, by Lemma
5.6
, for any
\underline{\sigma}\in[q]^{V}
, we have the bound

\Big|\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]-\xi\Big|\leq\frac{C}{n}\big\|\sqrt{n}(R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi)\big\|_{1}^{2}\,,

where
C\equiv C_{k,q,\Psi}>0
. Thus, on the event
\big\{\big\|\sqrt{n}(R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi)\big\|_{1}\leq K\big\}
, which happens with probability
1-o_{K}(1)
under
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\sim\pi^{\otimes n}
by the central limit theorem, we have that
\big|\mathbb{E}_{p,u}\big[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\underline{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\big]-\xi\big|=O_{k,q,\Psi}\big(\frac{K^{2}}{n}\big)
holds. Therefore, using this bound in the RHS of (
116
) shows that on the event
\big\{\big\|\sqrt{n}(R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi)\big\|_{1}\leq K\big\}
, we have

\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big((\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}=\exp\Bigg(O_{k,q,\Psi}\bigg(\frac{K^{2}|F|}{n}\bigg)\Bigg)\,.

Since the number of clauses
\boldsymbol{m}
is Poisson with mean
dn/k
for both
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma}
and
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime}
, we have
|F|\leq Cn
holds with probability tending to
1
under both models. Therefore, we conclude that
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
and
((\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
are contiguous (see e.g.
[
Jan95
, Proposition 9.47]
).

Appendix C
Applications to hypergraph stochastic block models

In this section, we prove Theorems
2.13
,
2.14
, and
2.15
by applying our results for the planted factor models. Throughout, we fix
k,q\geq 2
and
\pi\in\mathscr{P}([q])
. Also, we recall that
\mathscr{G}_{n}
denotes the event consisting of factor graphs that satisfy
(H1)
and
(H2)
. We denote by
(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
the planted model
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
conditioned on the event
\mathscr{G}_{n}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}
is a
k
-uniform hypergraph with no multiple edges by viewing each clause as an hyperedge.

C.1
Proof of Theorem
2.14
and Theorem
2.15

First, we prove that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\sf HSBM}\sim\mathcal{G}^{\sf H}(n,M,\pi)
is mutually contiguous with respect to
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}
with a particular choice of weight function defined as follows. By viewing the symmetric tensor
M
of order
k
as a weight function
M:[q]^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, let
p_{M}
be the prior on the weight functions which puts all of its mass on
M

p_{M}=\delta_{M}\,.

(118)

That is, there is a single weight function
\Psi\equiv\{M\}
. Then, we consider the planted model

\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},p_{M},\pi)\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{where}\;\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{m}\sim{\sf Poi}(dn/k)\,,

where
d
is the average degree of the HSBM defined in (
24
). We let
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
be the
k
-uniform hypergraph obtained from
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
by conditioning on the event
\mathscr{G}_{n}
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
M
be a symmetric tensor of order
k
with positive entries such that the degree condition (
26
) holds for
M_{0}=M/d
. Then, the total variation distance between
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
and
(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tv\Big(\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\big),\big(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\big)\Big)=0\,.

Proof.

Let
Q(G,\underline{\sigma}):=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}
be the likelihood ratio between
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
and
(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
. Then, it is standard to see that

\tv\Big(\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\big),\big(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\big)\Big)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(1-Q(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\big)\mathds{1}\big\{Q(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\leq 1\big\}\Big]\,.

Since the term inside the expectation in the right hand side is bounded, if we establish that

Q(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\,,

(119)

then this implies our goal. Thus, we aim to show (
119
) for the rest of the proof.

Given a factor graph
G=\big(V,F,E,(\psi_{a})_{a\in F}\big)
and a
k
-tuple of variables
\omega\equiv(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\in V^{k}
, let
F_{\omega}(G)
be the set of
a\in F
such that
\delta a=(v_{1},\ldots v_{k})
and denote its size by
f_{\omega}(G)\equiv|F_{\omega}(G)|
. For
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}\equiv\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}(n,\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},p_{M},\pi)
, we have by Poisson thinning that conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
,
\big(f_{\omega}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M})\big)_{\omega\in V^{k}}
are independent Poisson random variables with mean

\mathbb{E}\Big[f_{\omega}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M})\,\Big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}\Big]=\frac{dn}{k}\frac{M(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\omega})}{\sum_{\omega^{\prime}\in V^{k}}M(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}_{\omega^{\prime}})}\,.

Observe that
G\in\mathscr{G}_{n}
if and only if
f_{\omega}(G)=0
for any
\omega=(v_{1},\ldots v_{k})
such that
v_{1},\ldots v_{k}
are not distinct and
f_{\omega}(G)\leq 1
holds for any
\omega\in V^{k}
. Thus, conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}
, each possible hyperedge
(v_{1},\ldots v_{k})
in
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
, where
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
are distinct, is included independently with probability
\frac{\phi(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{1+\phi(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}
. Here, for
\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in[q]^{k}
, the function
\phi(\underline{i})\equiv\phi_{\underline{\sigma}}(\underline{i})
is defined by

\phi(\underline{i}):=\frac{d(k-1)!M(\underline{i})}{\sum_{\omega^{\prime}\in V^{k}}M(\underline{\sigma}_{\omega^{\prime}})}\cdot n=\frac{d(k-1)!M(\underline{i})}{\sum_{i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{k}^{\prime}}M(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{k}^{\prime})\prod_{s=1}^{k}R_{\underline{\sigma}}(i^{\prime}_{s})}n^{-(k-1)}\,,

(120)

where in the last equality,
R_{\underline{\sigma}}\equiv\big(R_{\underline{\sigma}}(i)\big)_{1\leq i\leq q}
denotes the empirical distribution of
\underline{\sigma}
(cf. (
117
)). Thus, if we let
E_{\underline{i}}\equiv E_{\underline{i}}(G)
denote the number of hyperedges in
G
such that the communities of the end points of the hyperedges are given by
\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
, then we have that

\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)=\prod_{\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in[q]^{k}}\bigg(\frac{\phi(\underline{i})}{1+\phi(\underline{i})}\bigg)^{E_{\underline{i}}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{1+\phi(\underline{i})}\bigg)^{n^{k}\prod_{s=1}^{k}R_{\underline{\sigma}}(i_{s})-E_{\underline{i}}}\,.

Hence,
Q(G,\underline{\sigma})
can be expressed by

\begin{split}Q(G,\underline{\sigma})&=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}{\mathbb{P}\big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{\sf HSBM}=G\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}\big)}\\
&=\prod_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{k}}\bigg(\frac{\phi(\underline{i})\cdot\binom{n}{k-1}}{\big(1+\phi(\underline{i})\big)\cdot M(\underline{i})}\bigg)^{E_{\underline{i}}}\cdot\Bigg(\Big(1+\phi(\underline{i})\Big)\bigg(1-\frac{M(\underline{i})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\bigg)\Bigg)^{-n^{k}\prod_{s=1}^{k}R_{\underline{\sigma}}(i_{s})+E_{\underline{i}}}\end{split}

(121)

To this end, we estimate the RHS when
G=\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}
and
\underline{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
. Observe that
\phi(i_{1},\ldots i_{k})
can be estimated as follows. If we let
Z_{\underline{\sigma}}:=\sqrt{n}(R_{\underline{\sigma}}-\pi)
, then
Z_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}
is asymptotically normal by the central limit theorem. In particular,
Z_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}
has
O(1)
fluctuations. Moreover, since
M_{0}=M/d
satisfies the condition (
26
), we have

\sum_{\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots i_{k})\in[q]^{k}}M(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\Big(\pi_{i_{s}}+n^{-1/2}Z_{\underline{\sigma}}(i_{s})\Big)=d+O\bigg(\frac{\big\|Z_{\underline{\sigma}}\big\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\big(\frac{Z_{\underline{\sigma}}}{\sqrt{n}}\vee 1\big)^{k-2}}{n}\bigg)\,.

Thus, by plugging in the estimate above to (
120
), we can approximate

\phi_{\underline{\sigma}}(\underline{i})=\bigg(1+\frac{h_{\underline{\sigma}}(\underline{i})}{n}\bigg)\cdot\frac{M(\underline{i})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\,,\;\;\;\;\textnormal{where}\;\;\;\;\;h_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\leq\log n\;\;\;\;\textnormal{holds w.h.p.}

(122)

Moreover, note that

E_{\underline{i}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=\frac{n^{k}M(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}}{\binom{n}{k-1}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{2/3})=n\cdot(k-1)!M(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{2/3})\,,

(123)

where
Y=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{2/3})
denotes a term that
n^{-2/3}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
holds under
(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})
. Hence, by using the estimates (
122
) and (
123
), we have the approximation

\bigg(\frac{\phi_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(\underline{i})\cdot\binom{n}{k-1}}{\big(1+\phi_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(\underline{i})\big)\cdot M(\underline{i})}\bigg)^{E_{\underline{i}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})}=\exp\Bigg((k-1)!\Big(h_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(\underline{i})-\mathds{1}\{k=2\}M(\underline{i})\Big)M(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\Bigg)\,.

Similarly, the second term of the product in (
121
) can be approximated by

\begin{split}&\Bigg(\Big(1+\phi_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(\underline{i})\Big)\bigg(1-\frac{M(\underline{i})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\bigg)\Bigg)^{-n^{k}\prod_{s=1}^{k}R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(i_{s})+E_{\underline{i}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})}\\
&=\exp\Bigg(-(k-1)!\Big(h_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(\underline{i})-\mathds{1}\{k=2\}M(\underline{i})\Big)M(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(i_{s})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\Bigg)\\
&=\exp\Bigg(-(k-1)!\Big(h_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}(\underline{i})-\mathds{1}\{k=2\}M(\underline{i})\Big)M(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\Bigg)\,,\end{split}

where the last approximation holds since
R_{\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}}=\pi+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/3})
. Therefore, the RHS in the two displays above exactly cancels out, and combing with (
121
) yields that
Q(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
.

∎

Let
\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
denote the weak recovery threshold w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{d\cdot M_{0}}
. That is, recalling the general definition of weak recovery in Definition
4.1
, let

\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi):=\inf\Big\{d>0:\textnormal{weak recovery is possible at $d$ for $\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{d\cdot M_{0}}$}\Big\}\,.

As a consequence of Lemma
C.1
, we have

d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)=\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)\,.

(124)

To transfer Theorem
3.12
for planted factor models to HSBM, we need to further prove that the weak recovery threshold is unchanged after conditioning on
\mathscr{G}_{n}
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
M_{0}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k
with positive entries such that the degree condition (
26
) is satisfied. Then, we have
\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
. and
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
.

Proof.

The final assertion that
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
is immediate from the definition of KS thresholds for HSBM and planted factor models stated in (
29
) and Definition
3.3
respectively. For the rest of the proof, we aim to prove that
\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
holds by showing that

\limsup_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M})}A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})-\frac{1}{q}\right]=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\limsup_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\hat{\underline{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M})}A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})-\frac{1}{q}\right]=0.

Note that
A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})-\frac{1}{q}\in[0,1]
and so the above equivalence would follow if
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
and
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
were mutually contiguous since the events
\{A(\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star},\hat{\underline{\sigma}})-\frac{1}{q}\geq\epsilon\}
would both either tend to 0 or not. Unfortunately, while
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
is contiguous with respect to
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
(which gives one direction of the equivalence)
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
is not contiguous with respect to
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
because there is a constant probability of clauses that violate
(H1)
or
(H2)
. Instead, we will add clauses to
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
, using no information about
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
so that the resulting graph
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star}_{M}
is mutually contiguous with respect to
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
. Let

\mathcal{C}=\{\underline{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}:1\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq\ldots\leq j_{k}\leq n\},

be the set of increasing integer sequences of length
k
between 1 and
n
. For
\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}
let
N_{\underline{j}}
be the number of clauses in
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
with vertex set given by a permutation of
v_{j_{1}},v_{j_{2}},\ldots,v_{j_{k}}
(counting multiplicity if there are repeated
j_{i}
). By construction of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
, conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
, the
N_{\underline{j}}
are independent Poisson random variables with means
p_{\underline{j}}
where

C_{1}^{-1}n^{-(k-1)}\leq p_{\underline{j}}\leq C_{1}n^{-(k-1)}

for some
C_{1}>1
which may depend on the model but not on
n
or
\underline{j}
. We will let
\mathcal{D}
denote the set of
\underline{j}
with all
j_{i}
distinct. The
p_{\underline{j}}
satisfy

\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}}p_{\underline{j}}\leq CN,\quad\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{D}}p_{\underline{j}}\leq C.

(125)

Letting
\widetilde{N}_{\underline{j}}
denote the number of clauses of
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
with vertices
\underline{j}
, the
\widetilde{N}_{\underline{j}}
are conditionally independent with distribution Poisson with mean
p_{\underline{j}}
conditioned to be at most 1 if
\underline{j}\in\mathcal{D}
and are equal to 0 if
\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{D}
.

Next let
N^{\prime}_{\underline{j}}
be independent Poisson with mean
n^{-(k-1)}
and define the transformed graph
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star}_{M}
by adding clauses such that

\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}:=\begin{cases}N^{\prime}_{\underline{j}}&\hbox{if }\widetilde{N}_{\underline{j}}\in\mathcal{D},N^{\prime}_{\underline{j}}\geq 2\\
N^{\prime}_{\underline{j}}&\hbox{if }\widetilde{N}_{\underline{j}}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{D}\\
\widetilde{N}_{\underline{j}}&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that we do not use any information about
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
to construct
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star}_{M}
from
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
. For
\underline{j}\in\mathcal{D}
, the distribution of
\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}
satisfies

\frac{\mathbb{P}[\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}=\ell]}{\mathbb{P}[N_{\underline{j}}=\ell]}=\begin{cases}\frac{\mathbb{P}[N^{\prime}_{\underline{j}}\leq 2]}{\mathbb{P}[N_{\underline{j}}\leq 2]}&\hbox{if }\ell\leq 1\\
\frac{n^{-(k-1)\ell e^{-n^{-(k-1)}}}}{p_{\underline{j}}^{\ell}e^{-p_{\underline{j}}}}&\hbox{if }\ell\geq 1\\
\end{cases}\qquad=\begin{cases}1+O(n^{-2(k-1)})&\hbox{if }\ell\leq 1\\
e^{O(\ell)}&\hbox{if }\ell\geq 2\\
\end{cases}

Hence, if we write
\nu_{\underline{j}}
and
\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}}
for the law of
N_{\underline{j}}
and
\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}
respectively then

\|\nu_{\underline{j}}-\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\nu_{\underline{j}})}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Big|\frac{\mathbb{P}[\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}=\ell]}{\mathbb{P}[N_{\underline{j}}=\ell]}-1\Big|^{2}\mathbb{P}[N_{\underline{j}}=\ell]\leq O(n^{-4(k-1)})+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}C^{2\ell}\frac{p_{\underline{j}}^{\ell}e^{-p_{\underline{j}}}}{\ell!}=O(n^{-(k-1)}p_{\underline{j}}),

and similarly

\|\nu_{\underline{j}}-\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}})}=O(n^{-(k-1)}p_{\underline{j}}).

For
\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{D}
,

\displaystyle\|\nu_{\underline{j}}-\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\nu_{\underline{j}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Big|\frac{n^{-(k-1)\ell}e^{-n^{-(k-1)}}}{p_{\underline{j}}^{\ell}e^{-p_{\underline{j}}^{\ell}}}-1\Big|^{2}\frac{p_{\underline{j}}^{\ell}e^{-p_{\underline{j}}}}{\ell!}

\displaystyle\leq O(n^{-2(k-1)})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}C^{2\ell}\frac{p_{\underline{j}}^{\ell}e^{-p_{\underline{j}}}}{\ell!}

\displaystyle=O(n^{-(k-1)})=O(p_{\underline{j}}),

and similarly

\|\nu_{\underline{j}}-\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}})}=O(p_{\underline{j}}).

Setting
\nu
and
\breve{\nu}
for the law of the vectors
\{N_{\underline{j}}\}_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}}
and
\{\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}\}_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}}
respectively, since both are product measures,

\displaystyle 1+\|\nu-\breve{\nu}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\breve{\nu})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}}\big(1+\|\nu_{\underline{j}}-\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\breve{\nu}_{\underline{j}})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(Cn^{-(k-1)}\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{D}}p_{\underline{j}}+C\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{D}}p_{\underline{j}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq O(1),

where the last inequality used equation (
125
). Similarly

\|\widetilde{\nu}-\breve{\nu}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\widetilde{\nu})}=O(1).

It follows that
\{N_{\underline{j}}\}_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}}
and
\{\breve{N}_{\underline{j}}\}_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{C}}
are mutually contiguous and hence
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}^{\star}_{M}
is mutually contiguous with respect to
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}
. Hence we have that
\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
.

∎

Let
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}
denote the null model
\boldsymbol{G}\sim\mathcal{G}_{\sf null}(n,d,p_{M})
conditioned on the event
\mathscr{G}_{n}
. Here, we drop the subscript
M
since the factors
(\psi_{a})_{a\in F}\equiv M
in
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}
(and
\boldsymbol{G}
) do not play any role. For a factor graph
G
, let

\widetilde{L}(G):=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}=G)}=\frac{L(G)\mathds{1}\{G\in\mathscr{G}_{n}\}}{\mathbb{E}[L(\boldsymbol{G})\,|\,\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{n}]}\,,

(126)

where the last equality holds by definition of
L(G)
(see Eq. (
44
)).
Then, the following proposition plays an important role in the proof of Theorem
2.14
. Recall the constants
(\alpha_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
defined in Eq. (
31
), which are functions of
M_{0}
and
\pi
.

Proposition C.3
.

Let
M_{0}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k
with positive entries. Below the weak revery and KS thresholds
d<d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
, we have that as
n\to\infty
,

\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}:=\prod_{\ell=2+\mathds{1}\{k=2\}}^{\infty}\frac{(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}}{\mathbb{E}(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}}\,,

(127)

where
(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
are independent Poisson random variables with mean
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2\ell}\big((k-1)d\big)^{\ell}
.

Proof.

The proof follows from a combination of Fact
3.9
-(3) Theorem
3.12
. For a signature
\zeta\in S_{\ell}
, if the prior on the weight functions
p
puts all of its mass on
M
, then the constants
\lambda_{\zeta}
and
\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}
in (
41
) equal

\lambda_{\zeta}=\frac{1}{2\ell}\left(\frac{d}{k}\right)^{\ell}\,,\quad\quad\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}=\frac{1}{2\ell}\tr(B^{\ell})\,,

where
B
is the matrix defined in (
28
). Thus,
\lambda_{\zeta},\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}
depends on
\zeta
on through its order
\ell
and

\delta_{\zeta}\equiv\lambda^{\star}_{\zeta}/\lambda_{\zeta}-1=\alpha_{\ell}\,.

Consequently, Theorem
3.12
yields that for
d<d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
,

L(\boldsymbol{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}=\prod_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}}{\mathbb{E}(1+\alpha_{\ell})^{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}}}\,,

where this convergence holds jointly with the weak convergence of the number of cycles of given length in Fact
3.9
-(1).
Meanwhile by Fact
3.9
-(3),
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{n})=\mathbb{P}(C(\boldsymbol{G})=0)+O(1/n)
and
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{G}_{n})=\mathbb{P}(C(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star})=0)+O(1/n)
hold, where
C(G)
is the number of self-loops if
k\geq 3
and the sum of the number of self-loops and the number of cycles of length
2
if
k=2
. Hence, it follows that
L(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})
weakly converges to the distribution of
\boldsymbol{L}_{\infty}
conditional on the event
\big\{\boldsymbol{X}_{1}+\mathds{1}\{k=2\}\boldsymbol{X}_{2}=0\big\}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{L}(G)
and
L(G)
just differs by a multiplicative factor for
G\in\mathscr{G}_{n}
by (
126
) where
\widetilde{L}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{G}})
satisfies the normalization
\mathbb{E}\widetilde{L}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{G}})=1
. Therefore, the desired claim (
127
) follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.14
.

Consider
d<d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)\wedge d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
. Recalling (
124
), we have
d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)=\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
. Moreover,
\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
and
d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)=d_{{\tiny\textsf{{KS}}}}^{\sf H}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
hold by Lemma
C.2
. Hence, Proposition
C.3
yields
\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
. Note that
\widetilde{L}(G)
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
are related by the chain rule

\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)=\widetilde{L}(G)\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G)}\equiv\widetilde{L}(G)\frac{f_{1}(G)}{f_{2}(G)}\,,

(128)

where

f_{1}(G):=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}\,,\quad\quad f_{2}(G):=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G)}\,.

Note that by considering
M_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{k,q}
, the all-
1
-tensor in Lemma
C.1
, we have
f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
. Clearly,
f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
follows from Lemma
C.1
. Note that
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
is mutually contiguous with
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}
by Proposition
C.3
, thus
f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
also holds. Therefore, by combining the established convergences
\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
,
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
for
i=1,2
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
by (
128
). Finally, the stated properties of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}
are a special case of Lemma
3.13
and Corollary
3.14
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.15
.

This is a special case of Lemma
3.15
and Corollary
3.16
.
∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
2.13

The following lemma relates the mutual information and the free energy for HSBMs, which is the analog of Lemma
3.18
for planted factor models.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
M_{0}
be a symmetric tensor of order
k
with positive entries such that the normalization (
25
) is satisfied. Then, the normalized mutual information can be approximated by

\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=\frac{d}{k}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots i_{k}=1}^{q}M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\log\big(M_{0}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\big)\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+o_{n}(1)\,.

Proof.

By definition of mutual information and the likelihood ratio
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)
, we have the identity

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{n}I(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star})\\
&=-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{G,\underline{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}\,.\end{split}

(129)

Note that for any
G
and
\underline{\sigma}
, we can compute

\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}=\prod_{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\in E(G)}\frac{\frac{M(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}{\frac{d}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}\prod_{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\notin E(G)}\frac{1-\frac{M(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}{1-\frac{d}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}\,,

where
E(G)
denote the set of hyperedges in
G
. Recall that
M=dM_{0}
, so taking logarithm and conditional expectation w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}
conditional on
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma}
in the RHS gives

\begin{split}&\sum_{G}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}\\
&=\sum_{(v_{1},\ldots v_{k})}\frac{dM_{0}(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\log M_{0}(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})+\bigg(1-\frac{dM_{0}(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\bigg)\log\Bigg(\frac{1-\frac{dM_{0}(\sigma_{v_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{k}})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}{1-\frac{d}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}\Bigg)\,,\end{split}

where the sum is over distinct
k
vertices
(v_{1},\ldots v_{k})
. Taking expectation over
\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}
yields

\begin{split}&\sum_{G,\underline{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G\,|\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\sigma}}^{\star}=\underline{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}\\
&=\sum_{\underline{i}=(i_{1},\ldots i_{k})\in[q]^{k}}\binom{n}{k}\cdot\Bigg(\frac{dM_{0}(\underline{i})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\log M_{0}(\underline{i})+\bigg(1-\frac{dM_{0}(\underline{i})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\bigg)\log\Bigg(\frac{1-\frac{dM_{0}(\underline{i})}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}{1-\frac{d}{\binom{n}{k-1}}}\Bigg)\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}\\
&=\frac{dn}{k}\Bigg(\sum_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{k}}M_{0}(\underline{i})\log M_{0}(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+\sum_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{k}}\big(1-M_{0}(\underline{i})\big)\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+o_{n}(1)\Bigg)\\
&=\frac{dn}{k}\sum_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{k}}M_{0}(\underline{i})\log M_{0}(\underline{i})\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}+o_{n}(1)\,,\end{split}

where we used
\sum_{\underline{i}\in[q]^{k}}\big(1-M_{0}(\underline{i})\big)\prod_{s=1}^{k}\pi_{i_{s}}=0
(cf. (
25
)) in the last equality. Combining this with (
129
) concludes the proof.
∎

We next prove that the free energy is bounded away from
0
along a subsequence by transferring Proposition
3.21
for the planted factor models to HSBM.

Proposition C.5
.

For any
n\geq 1
and
d>0
, we have
\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})\geq 0
. Moreover, if
d>d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
and the condition
{\sf(MIN)}
holds, then there exists
\eta>0
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})\geq\eta\,.

(130)

Proof.

Letting
F_{0}(x)=x\log x
, we have by a change of measure that

\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}})\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}})\geq F_{0}(\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}))=0\,,

where the inequality follows from Jensen’s inequality and the last equality holds by
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}})=1
.

Next, suppose that
d>d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
and the condition
(MIN)
holds. Assume by contraction that (
130
) does not hold. Then, since
\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})\geq 0
, we must have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=0\,.

Now, recall the chain rule
\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)=\widetilde{L}(G)f_{1}(G)/f_{2}(G)
in (
128
), where we defined
f_{1}(G)\equiv\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}=G)}
and
f_{2}(G)\equiv\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}=G)}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}=G)}
. Then, note that for
i=1,2
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=0\,.

Indeed,
i=2
case holds since
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}
and
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M}
are mutually contiguous by Lemma
C.1
and
n^{-1}\log f_{2}(\cdot)
is bounded. For
i=1
,
n^{-1}\log f_{1}(G)
can be calculated directly since
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}
doesn’t have planted structure, from which it can be seen that for any sparse random graph
\breve{\boldsymbol{G}}
with at most linear number of edges in expectation,
n^{-1}\mathbb{E}\log f_{1}(\breve{\boldsymbol{G}})=o_{n}(1)
. Therefore, it follows that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=0\,.

Since
\tv(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M})=o_{n}(1)
holds by Lemma
C.1
and
n^{-1}\log\widetilde{L}(G)=O(1)
holds for factor graph
G
with at most
O(n)
clauses, it follows that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M})=0\,.

Note that by Azuma Hoeffding’s inequality,
n^{-1}\log\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M})
and
n^{-1}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M})
concentrate tightly around their expectation with
O(n^{-1/2})
fluctuation, and that
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M}\in\mathscr{G}_{n}
holds with uniformly positive probability. Thus,
n^{-1}\mathbb{E}\log\widetilde{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}^{\star}_{M})=n^{-1}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M})+O(n^{-1/2})
holds. Consequently, we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log L(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{M})=0\,,

which contradicts Proposition
3.21
-(3).
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.13
.

The first statement regarding the mutual contiguity follows immediately from Theorem
2.14
and Le Cam’s first lemma (see e.g.
[
Vaa98
, Lemma 6.4]
or
[
JLuR00
, Proposition 9.49]
) since
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}>0
a.s. and
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\infty}=1
. The mutual contiguity between
\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}
implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})=0\,.

Combining with Lemma
C.4
finishes the proof of the second statement. For the third statement, recall that
d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)=\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
holds (cf. (
124
)) and that
\widetilde{d}_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)=d_{\ast}(p_{M_{0}},\pi)
holds by Lemma
C.2
. Thus, for
d>d_{\ast}^{\sf H}(M_{0},\pi)
, Proposition
C.5
yields that there exists a constant
\eta>0
such that
n^{-1}\mathbb{E}\log\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}})\geq\eta>0
holds along a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
. Then, consider the event

\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n}=\{G:\mathcal{L}_{n}(G)\geq e^{\eta n/2}\}\,.

Then, the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
2.8
shows that
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{{\sf ER}}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n_{\ell}})\leq e^{-\eta^{\prime}n}
and
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\star}_{{\sf HSBM}}\in\mathscr{A}^{\ast}_{n_{\ell}})\geq 1-e^{-\eta^{\prime}n}
for some constant
\eta^{\prime}>0
. Finally, the fourth statement is immediate from Lemma
C.4
and Proposition
C.5
, which concludes the proof.
∎
